# Psychiatry

## Mood Disorders & Depression

**Q:** A 34-year-old has depressed mood, anhedonia, hypersomnia, weight gain, fatigue, and poor concentration for 6 weeks. She denies suicidal ideation. DSM-5 criteria met. First-line treatment?

**A:** Major Depressive Disorder (MDD); first-line: SSRI (escitalopram, sertraline, fluoxetine) or SNRI (venlafaxine, duloxetine) + psychotherapy (CBT is most evidence-based)

CANMAT (Canadian Network for Mood and Anxiety Treatments) MDD Guidelines: SSRIs/SNRIs are first-line pharmacotherapy. CBT is equally effective as medication for mild-moderate MDD and prevents relapse. Combined pharmacotherapy + psychotherapy is superior to either alone.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for a major depressive episode?

**A:** ≥5 of 9 symptoms for ≥2 weeks, including EITHER depressed mood OR anhedonia: SIGECAPS — Sleep (insomnia/hypersomnia), Interest (loss of), Guilt (worthlessness), Energy (fatigue), Concentration, Appetite (change), Psychomotor (retardation/agitation), Suicidality — causing significant impairment

MDD diagnosis requires: ≥5 symptoms, present most of the day nearly every day for ≥2 weeks, including depressed mood or anhedonia. Must cause significant distress/impairment. Rule out: substance use, medical conditions (hypothyroidism, Cushing), bereavement (can meet criteria for MDD).

**Q:** What is the approach when a patient with MDD fails to respond to the first SSRI after 6-8 weeks?

**A:** Switch to another SSRI or different class (SNRI, bupropion, mirtazapine), or augment (add lithium, atypical antipsychotic — quetiapine or aripiprazole, or T3)

CANMAT MDD guidelines: adequate trial is 4-8 weeks at therapeutic dose. Treatment failure: switch to another first-line agent or augment. Two failed trials = treatment-resistant depression (TRD). TRD options: lithium augmentation, atypical antipsychotic augmentation, ECT (most effective for TRD), esketamine nasal spray.

**Q:** What is the duration of antidepressant treatment for a first MDD episode?

**A:** Continue antidepressants for 6-12 months after full remission for a first episode; 2-3 years after 2 episodes; indefinite for 3+ episodes or high-risk features

CANMAT guidelines: premature discontinuation is the most common cause of relapse. Taper slowly over 4 weeks minimum when discontinuing (avoid discontinuation syndrome: FINISH — Flu-like symptoms, Insomnia, Nausea, Imbalance, Sensory disturbances, Hyperarousal). Patient education is critical.

**Q:** A 28-year-old woman has a history of MDD and asks about starting an antidepressant while planning to become pregnant. What is the appropriate approach?

**A:** Discuss risks of untreated depression vs. medication risks; SSRIs are preferred if pharmacotherapy needed (most safety data); paroxetine: avoid (cardiac defects); sertraline: lowest risk; neonatal adaptation syndrome may occur

CANMAT/SOGC guidelines: untreated maternal depression is associated with preterm birth, low birth weight, poor prenatal care. SSRI risk: neonatal adaptation syndrome (transient jitteriness, poor feeding) — not life-threatening. Persistent pulmonary hypertension of newborn: rare association with late pregnancy SSRI exposure.

**Q:** What is postpartum depression (PPD) and how is it different from baby blues?

**A:** Baby blues: mild mood fluctuations within 3-5 days of delivery, resolving within 2 weeks; PPD: meets MDD criteria within 12 months of delivery, requires treatment (psychotherapy, SSRIs)

SOGC/CANMAT guidelines recommend universal PPD screening (Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale, EPDS) at 6 weeks and 3 months postpartum. Score ≥13 suggests depression. SSRIs are safe with breastfeeding (sertraline has lowest breast milk transfer). Postpartum psychosis (rare 1-2/1000) is a medical emergency.

**Q:** What is bipolar disorder type I and how does it differ from type II?

**A:** Bipolar I: at least 1 manic episode (DSM-5: ≥1 week elevated/expansive/irritable mood + ≥3 DIGFAST symptoms); Bipolar II: recurrent MDD + hypomanic episodes (4-7 days, less severe, no psychosis or hospitalization needed)

CANMAT Bipolar Guidelines: bipolar I has episodes of mania that may require hospitalization. Bipolar II is often misdiagnosed as recurrent depression (hypomania missed). Antidepressant monotherapy is contraindicated in bipolar disorder — can trigger mania or mixed states.

**Q:** What is the DIGFAST mnemonic for manic symptoms?

**A:** Distractibility, Irresponsibility/Impulsivity, Grandiosity, Flight of ideas, Activity (increased goal-directed), Sleep (decreased need), Talkativeness (pressured speech)

DSM-5 manic episode: ≥7 days (or any duration if hospitalized) of elevated/expansive/irritable mood + ≥3 DIGFAST symptoms (≥4 if mood is irritable only). Psychotic features can occur in mania. Decreased need for sleep (not insomnia) is a key feature that distinguishes mania from MDD.

**Q:** What are the first-line mood stabilizers for bipolar disorder?

**A:** Acute mania: lithium, valproate, or atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine); Bipolar maintenance: lithium (most evidence), valproate, lamotrigine (especially for bipolar depression)

CANMAT Bipolar Guidelines: lithium is the gold standard for long-term bipolar management, reducing suicide risk by 6x. Lamotrigine is preferred for bipolar depression (less risk of switching to mania vs. antidepressants). Combination therapy is often required.

**Q:** What is lithium toxicity and what are the signs?

**A:** Mild (1.5-2.0 mmol/L): tremor, polyuria, nausea; Moderate (2.0-2.5 mmol/L): confusion, ataxia, coarse tremor; Severe (>2.5 mmol/L): seizures, coma, permanent neurological damage — treat with IV hydration, hold lithium, dialysis for severe toxicity

Lithium has a narrow therapeutic index (0.6-1.2 mmol/L). Toxicity precipitants: dehydration, NSAIDs, ACEi/diuretics (reduce renal clearance), sodium depletion. Monitor: lithium level (12h post-dose), TSH, BMP, renal function every 6 months.

**Q:** What is dysthymia (persistent depressive disorder) and how is it managed?

**A:** Persistent depressive disorder: depressed mood most of the day, more days than not, for ≥2 years with ≥2 additional symptoms (insomnia/hypersomnia, low energy, poor concentration, hopelessness) — less severe than MDD but more chronic

CANMAT guidelines: dysthymia responds to the same treatments as MDD (SSRIs, SNRIs, CBT) but may require longer treatment duration. Double depression: MDD superimposed on dysthymia. Chronic depression has higher response rates with combined pharmacotherapy + psychotherapy.

**Q:** What is the role of electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) in mood disorders?

**A:** ECT is highly effective (80-90% response rate) for: treatment-resistant depression, severe MDD with psychosis, suicidal ideation with inability to wait for medications, bipolar depression, catatonia — fastest-acting treatment for severe MDD

CANMAT guidelines: ECT is the most effective treatment for TRD. Main side effect: short-term anterograde and retrograde memory impairment (usually reversible). Conducted under general anesthesia. Maintenance ECT for prevention of relapse. No absolute contraindications.

**Q:** What screening tool is used for depression in Canada and what score indicates depression?

**A:** PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9): score 0-27; 5-9 = mild, 10-14 = moderate, 15-19 = moderately severe, ≥20 = severe depression; score ≥10 is the standard clinical cutoff for probable MDD

PHQ-9 is widely used in primary care and is validated in Canadian populations. CANMAT recommends routine depression screening with PHQ-2 (2 questions) and follow-up PHQ-9 if positive. EPDS for perinatal screening. PHQ-9 also used to monitor treatment response.

**Q:** What are the indications for hospitalization in MDD?

**A:** Active suicidal ideation with plan/intent/means, psychotic depression, severe self-neglect (unable to care for self), inability to contract for safety, severe comorbidities requiring inpatient management

CANMAT guidelines: involuntary hospitalization under provincial mental health acts when patient poses serious harm to self or others and lacks capacity or refuses voluntary admission. Least restrictive alternative should be considered — intensive outpatient, crisis beds, ACT teams.

**Q:** What is seasonal affective disorder (SAD) and how is it treated?

**A:** SAD: recurrent depressive episodes with seasonal pattern (fall/winter onset, spring remission); treat with bright light therapy (10,000 lux for 30 min/morning), SSRIs, CBT

CANMAT guidelines: bright light therapy is first-line for SAD — equal efficacy to SSRIs with faster onset. Light therapy should be started in early fall before symptom onset. SSRIs (bupropion extended-release is specifically approved for SAD prevention). CBT adapted for SAD has lasting benefits.

**Q:** What are DSM-5 criteria for major depressive episode?

**A:** >=5 of 9 criteria for >=2 weeks (must include depressed mood or anhedonia): SIGECAPS — Sleep, Interest, Guilt, Energy, Concentration, Appetite, Psychomotor, Suicidality

PHQ-9 screens severity. Mild: psychotherapy. Moderate-severe: SSRI + psychotherapy. Treatment response assessed at 4-6 weeks. Minimum 6-12 months after remission.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for major depressive disorder?

**A:** >=5 symptoms during the same 2-week period (must include depressed mood OR anhedonia): SIG-E-CAPS: Sleep changes, Interest loss (anhedonia), Guilt/worthlessness, Energy loss, Concentration difficulty, Appetite/weight change, Psychomotor agitation/retardation, Suicidal ideation. Causes significant distress or functional impairment. Not attributable to substance or medical condition.

MDD affects ~5% of Canadians at any time. CANMAT (Canadian Network for Mood and Anxiety Treatments) guidelines are the standard for treatment in Canada. The diagnosis requires functional impairment-meeting symptom criteria alone is insufficient. Screen for bipolar before starting antidepressants.

**Q:** What is the PHQ-9 and how is it used in clinical practice?

**A:** Patient Health Questionnaire-9: 9 items corresponding to DSM-5 depression criteria, each scored 0-3 (not at all to nearly every day). Total score 0-27. Severity: 0-4 minimal, 5-9 mild, 10-14 moderate, 15-19 moderately severe, 20-27 severe. Item 9 specifically screens for suicidal ideation.

PHQ-9 is widely used in Canadian primary care for depression screening and monitoring treatment response. A decrease of >=5 points indicates clinically meaningful improvement. CTFPHC recommends against universal depression screening in primary care, but targeted screening with PHQ-9 is appropriate for at-risk populations.

**Q:** What is the CANMAT first-line pharmacological treatment for MDD?

**A:** First-line: SSRIs (escitalopram, sertraline, fluoxetine), SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine), bupropion, mirtazapine. Second-line: TCAs, quetiapine XR, trazodone. Third-line: MAOIs, lithium augmentation. Choice based on: side effect profile, comorbidities, prior response, drug interactions. Adequate trial: therapeutic dose for 4-8 weeks.

CANMAT 2023 guidelines recommend SSRIs/SNRIs as first-line due to efficacy, tolerability, and safety in overdose. Escitalopram and sertraline have best overall evidence. Bupropion is preferred when sexual dysfunction, weight gain, or smoking cessation are concerns. Start low, titrate to therapeutic dose.

**Q:** What are the key side effects of common antidepressant classes?

**A:** SSRIs: GI (nausea, diarrhea), sexual dysfunction (30-50%), weight gain, hyponatremia (SIADH, especially elderly), serotonin syndrome, increased suicidality in <25 (black box warning). SNRIs: same as SSRIs + elevated BP (venlafaxine), discontinuation syndrome. Bupropion: insomnia, seizure risk (dose-dependent, CI if eating disorder/seizures), NO sexual dysfunction. Mirtazapine: sedation, weight gain, NO sexual dysfunction. TCAs: anticholinergic, orthostatic hypotension, cardiac toxicity in overdose (QRS widening).

CANMAT considers side effect profiles in treatment selection. Serotonin syndrome (hyperthermia, rigidity, clonus, agitation) is a medical emergency-stop offending agent, cyproheptadine. SSRI discontinuation syndrome (dizziness, nausea, paresthesias) is most common with paroxetine and venlafaxine due to short half-lives.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for bipolar I vs bipolar II disorder?

**A:** Bipolar I: >=1 manic episode (>=7 days or any duration if hospitalized: elevated/expansive/irritable mood + increased energy + >=3 of: decreased sleep, talkativeness, racing thoughts, distractibility, increased goal-directed activity, risky behavior). Bipolar II: >=1 hypomanic episode (>=4 days, same symptoms but less severe, no psychosis, no hospitalization) + >=1 major depressive episode.

Bipolar I does NOT require a depressive episode for diagnosis-mania alone suffices. Bipolar II is not milder than bipolar I; patients spend more time depressed and have high suicide risk. CANMAT emphasizes screening for bipolarity before starting antidepressants (antidepressant monotherapy can trigger mania).

**Q:** What is the pharmacological management of bipolar disorder (acute mania and maintenance)?

**A:** Acute mania: lithium or valproate (first-line mood stabilizers) + atypical antipsychotic (quetiapine, risperidone, olanzapine, aripiprazole) for acute stabilization. Bipolar depression: quetiapine monotherapy (first-line), lamotrigine, lithium, or lurasidone. Maintenance: lithium (gold standard, reduces suicide), valproate, quetiapine, lamotrigine. AVOID: antidepressant monotherapy (triggers mania/rapid cycling).

CANMAT bipolar guidelines recommend mood stabilizers as backbone therapy. Lithium requires monitoring: levels (0.6-1.2 mmol/L), renal function, TSH, calcium q6-12 months. Lithium has narrow therapeutic index-toxicity: coarse tremor, ataxia, confusion, renal failure. Valproate: monitor LFTs, CBC, levels.

**Q:** What lithium monitoring is required and what are the signs of toxicity?

**A:** Monitoring: serum lithium levels (12h post-dose, target 0.6-1.2 mmol/L for acute, 0.6-0.8 for maintenance), creatinine/eGFR q6-12mo, TSH q6-12mo (causes hypothyroidism 20%), calcium/PTH (hyperparathyroidism), pregnancy test (Ebstein anomaly risk). Toxicity: mild (1.5-2.0): coarse tremor, GI, lethargy. Moderate (2.0-2.5): confusion, myoclonus, hyperreflexia. Severe (>2.5): seizures, coma, renal failure, cardiac arrhythmia.

Lithium has the strongest evidence for reducing suicide in bipolar disorder. Common precipitants of toxicity: dehydration, NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, thiazides (all increase lithium levels). Management of toxicity: stop lithium, IV fluids, hemodialysis if severe (>2.5 mmol/L or severe symptoms).

**Q:** What are the features and management of serotonin syndrome?

**A:** Triad: altered mental status, autonomic instability (hyperthermia, diaphoresis, tachycardia, HTN), neuromuscular excitability (clonus-especially lower extremity, hyperreflexia, rigidity). Caused by serotonergic drug combinations (SSRI + MAOI most dangerous, also tramadol, linezolid, triptans). Management: stop offending agents, IV fluids, benzodiazepines, cyproheptadine (serotonin antagonist) 12mg then 4mg q2h, active cooling.

Serotonin syndrome is distinct from neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS): SS has clonus/hyperreflexia and rapid onset; NMS has lead-pipe rigidity and slow onset. Hunter Serotonin Toxicity Criteria: clonus (spontaneous or inducible) is the most important diagnostic feature. Canadian guidelines mandate 14-day washout between MAOI and SSRI.

**Q:** What is the approach to treatment-resistant depression (TRD)?

**A:** Definition: failure to respond to >=2 adequate antidepressant trials. CANMAT approach: (1) Optimize current therapy (dose, duration, adherence). (2) Switch within or between classes. (3) Augment: lithium, atypical antipsychotic (aripiprazole, quetiapine), thyroid hormone (T3). (4) Combination: SSRI + bupropion or mirtazapine. (5) Esketamine nasal spray (approved in Canada). (6) ECT (most effective treatment for severe TRD, catatonia, psychotic depression).

ECT has 50-70% response rate in TRD and is the most effective acute treatment for severe depression. Canadian guidelines support ECT availability as an essential psychiatric service. Esketamine (Spravato) is available in Canada under restricted access programs. Repetitive TMS is another option for TRD.

**Q:** What are the risk factors and features of postpartum depression vs postpartum psychosis?

**A:** PPD: onset within 4 weeks to 12 months postpartum, features of MDD, may include anxiety, guilt about parenting, thoughts of harming infant. Risk factors: prior depression, poor social support, unplanned pregnancy. Postpartum psychosis: RARE (1/1000), onset within first 2 weeks, features: confusion, disorganized behavior, mood lability, hallucinations/delusions. Psychiatric EMERGENCY-risk of infanticide.

SOGC and CPS recommend Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) screening. Score >=13 warrants further assessment. PPD treated with SSRIs (sertraline safe in breastfeeding) + psychotherapy. Postpartum psychosis requires hospitalization, antipsychotics, mood stabilizers. History of bipolar is the strongest risk factor for postpartum psychosis.

**Q:** What is the role of psychotherapy in mood disorders?

**A:** CBT (cognitive behavioral therapy): first-line for mild-moderate MDD, equivalent to antidepressants. IPT (interpersonal therapy): effective for MDD. DBT (dialectical behavior therapy): borderline personality disorder, chronic suicidality. CANMAT recommends combined pharmacotherapy + psychotherapy for moderate-severe MDD (superior to either alone). Mindfulness-based cognitive therapy: reduces relapse in recurrent depression.

CANMAT guidelines recommend psychotherapy as first-line for mild depression and combined with pharmacotherapy for moderate-severe. Access to psychotherapy in Canada is limited-many provinces lack public funding. Online CBT programs (e.g., BounceBack) are increasingly available across Canada.

**Q:** What is the definition and management of persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia)?

**A:** DSM-5: depressed mood most of the day, more days than not, for >=2 years (1 year in children/adolescents) + >=2 of: appetite change, sleep change, low energy, low self-esteem, poor concentration, hopelessness. Never symptom-free for >2 months. May coexist with MDD episodes (double depression).

Persistent depressive disorder (formerly dysthymia) has lower symptom severity but longer duration, causing significant cumulative impairment. Treatment combines antidepressants + psychotherapy (CBT or CBASP). CANMAT recommends same antidepressant choices as MDD.

**Q:** What medical conditions should be excluded in the differential diagnosis of depression?

**A:** Hypothyroidism, Cushing syndrome, Addison disease, hyperparathyroidism, B12/folate deficiency, anemia, sleep apnea, chronic pain, cancer (particularly pancreatic), neurological disease (Parkinson, MS, dementia, stroke), medications (beta-blockers, corticosteroids, interferon, isotretinoin, oral contraceptives), substance use (alcohol, benzodiazepine withdrawal).

CANMAT recommends baseline investigations for new MDD diagnosis: TSH, CBC, electrolytes, glucose, B12 if risk factors. Medical conditions causing depressive symptoms should be treated before attributing to primary MDD. Depression is also highly comorbid with chronic medical conditions.

**Q:** When should antidepressant discontinuation be considered and how?

**A:** After first episode: continue for 6-12 months after remission. After 2+ episodes or chronic: consider indefinite treatment. Discontinuation: taper gradually over 4+ weeks (longer for paroxetine, venlafaxine). Discontinuation syndrome: dizziness, nausea, anxiety, insomnia, electric shock sensations (brain zaps), irritability. Onset 1-3 days, lasts 1-2 weeks. Fluoxetine has lowest risk due to long half-life.

CANMAT recommends gradual taper to minimize discontinuation syndrome. Relapse risk is highest in the first 6 months after stopping. Shared decision-making about duration. Canadian primary care guidelines recommend monitoring for relapse during and after taper.

**Q:** What is seasonal affective disorder (SAD) and its treatment?

**A:** DSM-5: recurrent MDD with regular seasonal pattern (typically fall/winter onset, spring/summer remission) for >=2 years. More prevalent in northern latitudes (relevant to Canada). First-line treatment: light therapy (10,000 lux for 30 min/day in morning, within 1 hour of waking). Also effective: SSRIs (fluoxetine), bupropion (prophylactic starting in fall), CBT.

SAD is particularly relevant in Canadian practice given latitude. Light therapy has rapid onset (1-2 weeks). Bupropion XR started in autumn prevents SAD episodes (Modell et al). CANMAT includes light therapy as first-line treatment for SAD, equivalent to pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** What is the approach to treatment-resistant depression?

**A:** After 2 adequate SSRI/SNRI trials (>=6 weeks each): augment with lithium or atypical antipsychotic (aripiprazole/quetiapine), switch class, consider ECT, ketamine/esketamine, or combination therapy

CANMAT guidelines. ECT most effective treatment for severe/psychotic depression (70-90% response). rTMS for moderate treatment resistance. Psychotherapy (CBT) should continue throughout. Psilocybin therapy emerging.

**Q:** What are the medical complications of anorexia nervosa requiring admission?

**A:** HR <50, BP <90/60, temp <36C, BMI <15, electrolyte abnormalities (hypokalemia, hypophosphatemia), arrhythmia, suicidality, failed outpatient treatment

Refeeding syndrome: rapid reintroduction of nutrition causes intracellular shift of phosphate, potassium, magnesium. Complications: cardiac failure, seizures, death. Prevention: start low (10-20kcal/kg), supplement phosphate, monitor daily.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for anorexia nervosa?

**A:** 1) Restriction of energy intake leading to significantly low body weight (BMI <18.5 in adults); 2) Intense fear of gaining weight or persistent behavior interfering with weight gain; 3) Disturbance in body image or persistent lack of recognition of seriousness of low weight. Subtypes: restricting type vs binge-eating/purging type.

DSM-5 removed the amenorrhea criterion. Anorexia nervosa has the highest mortality of any psychiatric disorder (~5-10%). Canadian Practice Guidelines (CPA) emphasize early intervention and multidisciplinary care including psychiatry, medicine, and dietetics.

**Q:** A 17-year-old female with anorexia nervosa (BMI 14) presents with bradycardia (HR 42), hypothermia, and lanugo hair. What medical complications should you assess for?

**A:** Cardiac: bradycardia, QTc prolongation, mitral valve prolapse, cardiac arrest; Metabolic: hypoglycemia, electrolyte abnormalities (hypokalemia, hypophosphatemia, hypomagnesemia); Endocrine: amenorrhea, low estrogen, osteoporosis, euthyroid sick syndrome; Hematologic: pancytopenia; GI: gastroparesis, constipation, superior mesenteric artery syndrome; Dermatologic: lanugo, hair loss, dry skin.

Bradycardia <50 bpm is an indication for medical admission per Canadian guidelines. Osteoporosis risk increases significantly after 6 months of amenorrhea. QTc prolongation increases sudden cardiac death risk — obtain ECG on all patients with severe anorexia.

**Q:** What is refeeding syndrome, its pathophysiology, and how do you prevent it?

**A:** Refeeding syndrome: potentially fatal shifts in fluids and electrolytes when malnourished patients receive nutritional repletion. Pathophysiology: refeeding causes insulin surge → intracellular shift of phosphate, potassium, magnesium → severe hypophosphatemia (hallmark), hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia → cardiac arrhythmias, respiratory failure, rhabdomyolysis, delirium, death. Prevention: start low calorie refeeding (10-20 kcal/kg/day), supplement phosphate/K/Mg prophylactically, monitor electrolytes BID for first week.

Hypophosphatemia is the hallmark of refeeding syndrome — phosphate is required for ATP synthesis, and its depletion causes multiorgan failure. NICE guidelines recommend identifying high-risk patients (BMI <16, >15 days minimal intake). Canadian eating disorder programs typically start at 1200-1500 kcal/day and increase by 200-300 kcal every 2-3 days.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for bulimia nervosa and the key differences between purging and non-purging subtypes?

**A:** DSM-5: 1) Recurrent binge eating (large amount in discrete period with sense of loss of control); 2) Recurrent compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain; 3) Both occur at least once/week for 3 months; 4) Self-evaluation unduly influenced by body shape/weight; 5) Not exclusively during anorexia nervosa episodes. Purging type: self-induced vomiting, laxatives, diuretics. Non-purging type: fasting, excessive exercise.

Unlike anorexia, patients with bulimia are typically normal weight or overweight, making it harder to detect. The purging subtype is more common and associated with more medical complications. CPA guidelines recommend CBT as first-line treatment, with fluoxetine (60 mg) as adjunct.

**Q:** What is Russell's sign, and what metabolic and dental complications are characteristic of bulimia nervosa?

**A:** Russell's sign: calluses/scarring on dorsum of hand from repeated self-induced vomiting (teeth scraping knuckles). Metabolic: hypochloremic hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis (from vomiting — loss of HCl and K+); hyponatremia if water loading. Dental: perimolysis (erosion of dental enamel, especially lingual surfaces of upper teeth), parotid gland enlargement (sialadenosis), dental caries.

Metabolic alkalosis with hypokalemia in a young person should raise suspicion for purging behavior. Parotid enlargement is bilateral and painless, developing 3-4 days after binge-purge episodes. Canadian dentists are trained to screen for eating disorders based on enamel erosion patterns.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for binge eating disorder (BED) and its evidence-based treatments?

**A:** DSM-5: Recurrent binge eating episodes (at least once/week for 3 months) with 3+ of: eating rapidly, eating until uncomfortably full, eating large amounts when not hungry, eating alone due to embarrassment, feeling disgusted/depressed/guilty afterward. Marked distress about binging. NO compensatory behaviors. Treatment: CBT (first-line), lisdexamfetamine (Vyvanse — only medication with Health Canada approval for moderate-severe BED), topiramate, SSRIs.

BED is the most common eating disorder. Lisdexamfetamine (30-70 mg/day) reduces binge days per week but is a controlled substance with abuse potential. CBT for BED has strong evidence and is recommended as first-line by CPA. Unlike bulimia, fluoxetine is not specifically indicated for BED.

**Q:** A patient with anorexia nervosa (BMI 13.5) is being re-fed in hospital. On day 3, she develops confusion, tachycardia, and respiratory distress. Labs show phosphate 0.3 mmol/L (normal 0.8-1.5). What is the diagnosis and immediate management?

**A:** Refeeding syndrome with severe hypophosphatemia. Immediate management: 1) IV phosphate replacement (20-40 mmol over 6-12h with cardiac monitoring); 2) Reduce caloric intake by 50%; 3) Check and correct K+ and Mg2+; 4) Continuous cardiac monitoring (risk of arrhythmias); 5) Check thiamine level and supplement (250-300 mg IV before refeeding); 6) Gradual caloric increase once electrolytes stabilize.

Thiamine deficiency can precipitate Wernicke encephalopathy during refeeding — always supplement thiamine before carbohydrate reintroduction. Severe hypophosphatemia (<0.3 mmol/L) can cause cardiac arrest, respiratory failure from diaphragm weakness, and seizures. Canadian hospital protocols mandate BID electrolyte monitoring during initial refeeding.

**Q:** What are the medical stabilization criteria for inpatient admission of a patient with an eating disorder?

**A:** Admit if any of: HR <50 bpm (or <40 at night); systolic BP <90 mmHg; orthostatic changes (HR increase >20, SBP drop >20); temperature <35.5°C; BMI <15 (adults) or <75% median BMI (adolescents); severe electrolyte abnormalities (K+ <3.0, phosphate <0.8); QTc >450 ms; acute medical complications (syncope, seizures, cardiac failure); suicidal ideation; failed outpatient treatment.

These criteria are based on Canadian and APA guidelines for medical stabilization. Orthostatic vital signs are critical — perform lying-to-standing vitals on all eating disorder patients. Adolescents may have rapid medical deterioration even at higher BMI due to the rate and percentage of weight loss.

**Q:** What electrolyte abnormality pattern distinguishes vomiting from laxative abuse in eating disorders?

**A:** Vomiting: hypochloremic hypokalemic metabolic ALKALOSIS (loss of HCl from stomach); urine chloride <25 mmol/L (chloride-responsive). Laxative abuse: hypokalemic metabolic ACIDOSIS (or normal pH) with non-anion gap (loss of bicarbonate from GI tract); may also see hyponatremia and dehydration. Diuretic abuse: hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis similar to vomiting, but urine chloride >40 mmol/L.

Urine chloride helps distinguish vomiting (low urine Cl) from diuretic abuse (high urine Cl) when both present with metabolic alkalosis. Laxative abuse can be confirmed with urine laxative screen. Canadian eating disorder programs routinely check urine electrolytes and laxative screens in suspected purging.

**Q:** What is the evidence-based pharmacotherapy for eating disorders and which eating disorder responds best to medication?

**A:** Anorexia nervosa: NO medication has strong evidence for weight restoration; olanzapine may modestly help with weight gain and anxiety. Bulimia nervosa: fluoxetine 60 mg/day (only FDA/Health Canada-approved medication — higher dose than for depression). BED: lisdexamfetamine (Health Canada approved), also CBT first-line. SSRIs and topiramate have some evidence for BED. Bulimia responds best to combined pharmacotherapy + psychotherapy.

Fluoxetine at 60 mg (not 20 mg) is specifically effective for bulimia — this higher dose is a common exam question. For anorexia, nutritional rehabilitation is the cornerstone; medications address comorbid conditions. CPA guidelines emphasize that psychotherapy (especially CBT) remains the gold standard across all eating disorders.

**Q:** What is the recommended approach to lithium management in bipolar disorder?

**A:** Target serum level 0.6-0.8 mmol/L for maintenance, 0.8-1.2 for acute mania. Monitoring: serum lithium (5-7 days after dose change, then q3-6 months), TSH and creatinine q6 months, calcium annually, ECG at baseline. Ensure adequate hydration. Avoid NSAIDs, ACEi/ARBs, thiazides (increase lithium levels).

CANMAT guidelines recommend lithium as first-line maintenance for bipolar I disorder — it is the only mood stabilizer with proven anti-suicide benefit. Narrow therapeutic index requires careful monitoring. Lithium toxicity presents with tremor, GI symptoms, ataxia, and can progress to seizures, coma, and death. Pregnancy: associated with Ebstein anomaly (low absolute risk ~0.1%).

**Q:** What are the key monitoring parameters for valproate in bipolar disorder?

**A:** Target level 350-700 mcmol/L. Monitor: LFTs at baseline and periodically, CBC with platelets (thrombocytopenia risk), weight, lipid panel (metabolic effects), serum level. Valproate is CONTRAINDICATED in women of childbearing potential unless no alternative (NTD risk 1-2%, IQ reduction). Requires effective contraception.

CANMAT and Health Canada emphasize avoiding valproate in women who may become pregnant. It is classified as teratogenic with dose-dependent NTD risk. If used, folic acid 5 mg daily is mandatory. Polycystic ovary syndrome is associated with valproate use. Pancreatitis and hepatotoxicity are rare but serious adverse effects.

**Q:** What defines treatment-resistant depression (TRD) and what are the management options?

**A:** TRD: failure to respond to >=2 adequate antidepressant trials (adequate dose for >=6-8 weeks each) from different classes. Options: augmentation with lithium, atypical antipsychotic (aripiprazole, quetiapine), or thyroid hormone (T3). Switching strategies: different class SSRI->SNRI->TCA. ECT for severe TRD. Esketamine nasal spray (Spravato) is Health Canada-approved.

CANMAT 2016 guidelines recommend systematic approach: optimize current medication, then switch or augment. ~30% of MDD is treatment-resistant. Psychotherapy (CBT) combined with pharmacotherapy improves outcomes. Esketamine was approved in Canada in 2020 — administered under supervision due to dissociative effects and abuse potential.

**Q:** What are the indications for electroconvulsive therapy (ECT)?

**A:** Severe depression with psychotic features, treatment-resistant depression, catatonia, severe suicidality requiring rapid response, bipolar depression/mania refractory to medication, pregnancy (when medications carry unacceptable risk), neuroleptic malignant syndrome. ECT is the most effective acute treatment for severe depression (remission rate 50-70%).

CANMAT recommends ECT as first-line when rapid response is critical (severe suicidality, psychotic depression, catatonia). Common side effects: short-term memory impairment (usually resolves), headache, myalgias. Maintenance ECT prevents relapse. Informed consent requires discussion of cognitive side effects. No absolute contraindications — increased ICP is a relative contraindication.

**Q:** How should perinatal depression be screened for and managed?

**A:** Screen: Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) at first prenatal visit, 6 weeks, and 6 months postpartum. Score >=13 indicates probable depression. Management: mild = supportive psychotherapy, CBT/IPT. Moderate-severe: SSRIs (sertraline preferred in breastfeeding — low infant exposure). Severe: consider ECT. Always assess for psychosis and suicidality.

SOGC and CPS recommend universal perinatal depression screening. Prevalence is 10-15% postpartum, 10% antenatal. Untreated maternal depression affects infant attachment and development. SSRIs in pregnancy carry small absolute risks (persistent pulmonary HTN, neonatal adaptation syndrome) but benefits usually outweigh risks. CANMAT provides specific perinatal guidelines.

**Q:** What is the CANMAT-recommended first-line pharmacotherapy for bipolar depression?

**A:** Quetiapine (monotherapy), lurasidone (+ lithium or valproate), or lithium. Lamotrigine is effective for prevention of depressive episodes. Standard antidepressants are generally AVOIDED in bipolar depression (risk of switch to mania/rapid cycling), except SSRIs cautiously with a mood stabilizer.

Bipolar depression is more disabling than mania, accounting for 3x more time ill. CANMAT guidelines distinguish bipolar depression treatment from unipolar depression. Antidepressant monotherapy without a mood stabilizer is contraindicated. Lurasidone was shown effective in PREVAIL studies with minimal metabolic side effects.

**Q:** What features distinguish bipolar I from bipolar II disorder?

**A:** Bipolar I: at least one manic episode (>=7 days or hospitalization required) with possible depressive episodes. Bipolar II: at least one hypomanic episode (>=4 days, no hospitalization, less functional impairment) AND at least one major depressive episode. Bipolar II is NOT a milder form — depression burden is typically greater.

Bipolar II is often misdiagnosed as unipolar depression (average delay to correct diagnosis is 8-10 years). CANMAT recommends screening for hypomania in all patients presenting with depression using the Mood Disorder Questionnaire (MDQ). Treatment differs: lithium/quetiapine for both types; valproate primarily for bipolar I.

**Q:** What rapid-cycling bipolar disorder and how is it managed?

**A:** >=4 mood episodes (mania, hypomania, depression, or mixed) within 12 months. Affects 15-20% of bipolar patients, more common in women and bipolar II. Management: discontinue antidepressants (often trigger rapid cycling), optimize mood stabilizer (lithium or valproate), quetiapine. Thyroid optimization important.

CANMAT guidelines recommend addressing modifiable triggers: antidepressant withdrawal, thyroid dysfunction, substance use, and circadian rhythm disruption. Lamotrigine may help prevent depressive episodes in rapid cycling. Combination therapy is often needed. Prognosis is generally poorer than non-rapid cycling bipolar disorder.

**Q:** What are the signs and management of lithium toxicity?

**A:** Mild (1.5-2.0 mmol/L): tremor, nausea, diarrhea, polyuria. Moderate (2.0-2.5): ataxia, confusion, dysarthria, fasciculations. Severe (>2.5): seizures, coma, renal failure, cardiac arrhythmia. Management: hold lithium, aggressive IV normal saline hydration, hemodialysis for severe toxicity (>2.5, or symptomatic >2.0 with renal impairment).

Common precipitants of lithium toxicity include dehydration, NSAIDs, ACEi/ARBs, thiazide diuretics, and acute kidney injury. CANMAT emphasizes patient education about maintaining hydration and recognizing early symptoms. Chronic toxicity can occur at lower levels than acute toxicity. Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus is a common long-term effect.

**Q:** What are the CANMAT-recommended first-line antidepressants for major depressive disorder?

**A:** SSRIs (escitalopram, sertraline, fluoxetine), SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine), bupropion, mirtazapine, and agomelatine (unique in Canada). Selection based on patient profile: anxiety comorbidity (SSRI/SNRI), pain (duloxetine/venlafaxine), insomnia/weight loss (mirtazapine), sexual dysfunction concern (bupropion), fatigue (bupropion).

CANMAT 2016 guidelines recommend all first-line agents as equivalent in efficacy (~60% response rate). Treatment should be continued for 6-12 months after remission for first episode, longer for recurrent depression. Agomelatine (melatonin receptor agonist) requires LFT monitoring but has favorable side effect profile.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of Propofol?

**A:** GABA-A receptor agonist causing CNS depression; 30% decreased BP due to vasodilation

Propofol enhances GABAergic inhibition in the CNS, producing hypnosis with rapid onset and recovery due to its high lipid solubility.

**Q:** What respiratory effects do volatile anesthetics cause?

**A:** Respiratory depression with severely decreased tidal volume and increased respiratory rate, decreased response to CO2 reflexes, bronchodilation

All volatile agents depress respiratory drive and response to hypercapnia, requiring controlled ventilation.

**Q:** What is atrophy in dermatological context?

**A:** Histological decrease in size or number of cells or tissues, resulting in thinning or depression of the skin

Skin atrophy can result from aging, corticosteroid use, or various dermatological conditions.

**Q:** What is skin atrophy?

**A:** Histological decrease in size or number of cells or tissues, resulting in thinning or depression of the skin

Atrophy can result from aging, corticosteroid use, or inflammatory conditions, leading to fragile, translucent-appearing skin.

**Q:** What are the precipitating causes of Telogen Effluvium? Use the 'SEND' mnemonic

**A:** S - Stress (biological/psychological), E - Endocrine (postpartum, thyroid), N - Nutritional (iron/protein deficiency), D - Drugs (acitretin, heparin, lithium, IFN, beta-blockers, valproic acid, SSRIs)

Any metabolic stress can shift hair follicles prematurely from anagen to telogen, with a characteristic 2-4 month delay in visible shedding.

**Q:** What are precipitants of Telogen Effluvium (remember with SEND)?

**A:** Stress (biological/psychological), Surgery, Severe illness, Nutritional deficiency (iron/protein), Drugs (acitretin, heparin, lithium, IFN, beta-blockers, valproic acid, SSRIs)

These factors push hair follicles prematurely from anagen into telogen, causing increased shedding 2-4 months later.

**Q:** What common side effects of isotretinoin should patients be counseled about?

**A:** Decreased night vision, decreased tolerance to contact lenses, dry mucous membranes (dry skin, lips, eyes), transient acne exacerbation, depression, myalgia; teratogenic

Isotretinoin affects all epithelial tissues causing mucocutaneous dryness. Depression is a controversial concern requiring monitoring. Dry eyes affect contact lens tolerance.

**Q:** What drugs can precipitate HHS?

**A:** Diuretics, glucocorticoids, lithium, and atypical antipsychotics.

These medications can impair glucose metabolism or cause dehydration.

**Q:** What can cause false positive results on dexamethasone suppression test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, and medications or oral contraceptive pills that induce dexamethasone metabolism

These conditions can cause inappropriate cortisol levels despite normal HPA axis function.

**Q:** What can cause false positive results on the DXM Suppression Test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications or oral contraceptive pills inducing metabolism of DXM

These conditions can increase DXM metabolism, leading to inadequate cortisol suppression despite normal HPA axis function.

**Q:** What can cause false positive results on DXM suppression test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications inducing DXM metabolism, oral contraceptive pills

These conditions can interfere with dexamethasone metabolism or affect cortisol levels.

**Q:** What are causes of false positive DXM suppression test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications, oral contraceptive pills inducing DXM metabolism

**Q:** What can cause false positive results in the DEX suppression test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications, or oral contraceptive pills that induce metabolism of DEX

**Q:** What are false positive causes of Dexamethasone Suppression Test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications inducing dexamethasone metabolism, or oral contraceptive pills

These conditions can cause failure to suppress cortisol despite normal HPA axis.

**Q:** What are causes of false positive dexamethasone suppression test?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, medications, or oral contraceptive pills that induce dexamethasone metabolism

These conditions can cause inappropriate cortisol levels despite normal HPA axis function.

**Q:** What can cause false positive results on dexamethasone suppression testing?

**A:** Obesity, depression, alcoholism, pregnancy, and medications (including oral contraceptive pills) that induce dexamethasone metabolism

These conditions can cause inappropriate cortisol secretion that fails to suppress with dexamethasone.

**Q:** What are the contraindications for varenicline?

**A:** Caution with pre-existing psychiatric conditions (risk of psychosis, depression, suicidal ideation)

Patients with psychiatric history should be monitored closely for mood changes while on varenicline.

**Q:** What is the first-line SSRI for youth per Toronto Notes 2025?

**A:** Fluoxetine (Prozac®)

Fluoxetine is recommended as first-line SSRI therapy for youth. Paroxetine is not recommended for youth due to controversy.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of SSRIs?

**A:** Block serotonin reuptake

SSRIs work by inhibiting the reuptake of serotonin at the synaptic cleft, increasing serotonin availability.

**Q:** Which SSRI requires a baseline ECG due to QT interval effects?

**A:** Citalopram

Citalopram can increase QT interval, so a baseline ECG is suggested before starting therapy.

**Q:** Why is bupropion often chosen for depressed patients?

**A:** It causes less sexual dysfunction than SSRIs

Bupropion's mechanism (dopamine and NE reuptake blockade) results in fewer sexual side effects compared to SSRIs.

**Q:** What is the recurrence risk of depression after 1, 2, and 3 episodes?

**A:** 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, 90% after 3 episodes

Depression recurrence risk increases substantially with each episode, reaching 90% after 3 episodes.

**Q:** What percentage of depression cases resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months?

**A:** Up to 40%

Approximately 40% of depression cases resolve spontaneously without treatment within 6-12 months.

**Q:** What is the first-line SSRI for youth according to Toronto Notes 2025?

**A:** Fluoxetine (Prozac®)

Fluoxetine is recommended as first-line therapy for youth with depression, while paroxetine is not recommended for this population.

**Q:** Why is bupropion often chosen for depression treatment?

**A:** It has less sexual dysfunction side effects compared to SSRIs

Bupropion is often selected when sexual side effects are a concern, and can be used for augmentation of antidepressant effects with other medication classes.

**Q:** What is the spontaneous resolution rate of depression within 6-12 months?

**A:** Up to 40%

A significant proportion of depression cases resolve spontaneously, but many require treatment to prevent recurrence.

**Q:** Which SSRI is NOT recommended for youth?

**A:** Paroxetine (Paxil)

Paroxetine is controversial and not recommended for youth due to concerns about efficacy and safety in this population.

**Q:** What baseline investigation is suggested before prescribing certain SSRIs?

**A:** ECG (due to QT interval prolongation risk)

Some SSRIs, particularly citalopram, can prolong the QT interval, necessitating baseline ECG in at-risk patients.

**Q:** What are the recurrence risks for depression after episodes?

**A:** 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, 90% after 3 episodes

Depression has a high recurrence rate that increases with each episode.

**Q:** What is the spontaneous resolution rate for depression?

**A:** Up to 40% resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months

Some cases of depression can resolve without treatment, but treatment is recommended to reduce recurrence risk.

**Q:** What is the first-line SSRI therapy for youth according to Canadian guidelines?

**A:** Fluoxetine (Prozac®)

Fluoxetine is the first-line SSRI for youth, while paroxetine is not recommended due to safety concerns.

**Q:** Why is bupropion often chosen as an antidepressant?

**A:** Less sexual dysfunction compared to SSRIs; can be used for augmentation with other antidepressant classes

Bupropion's favorable side effect profile, particularly regarding sexual function, makes it a popular choice.

**Q:** What is the prognosis for untreated depression?

**A:** Up to 40% resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months

While some depression resolves spontaneously, treatment significantly reduces duration and prevents recurrence.

**Q:** What baseline investigations are needed when starting certain antidepressants?

**A:** Baseline ECG suggested for SSRIs (especially citalopram) due to QT interval prolongation risk

Some antidepressants can cause cardiac conduction abnormalities; baseline ECG is recommended.

**Q:** Which SSRI is not recommended for youth and why?

**A:** Paroxetine is not recommended for youth (controversial)

Paroxetine has been associated with increased suicidal ideation in pediatric populations.

**Q:** What is a common side effect of SSRIs that involves QT interval?

**A:** QT interval prolongation

SSRIs can increase QT interval. A baseline ECG is suggested for patients on SSRIs.

**Q:** What is the major disadvantage of TCAs (tricyclic antidepressants)?

**A:** Narrow therapeutic window, lethal in overdose

TCAs block serotonin and NE reuptake. They have a narrow therapeutic window and can be fatal in overdose.

**Q:** What is a notable advantage of bupropion over SSRIs?

**A:** Less sexual dysfunction

Bupropion causes less sexual dysfunction than SSRIs and can be used for augmentation of antidepressant effects.

**Q:** What is the recurrence risk of depression after 2 episodes?

**A:** 70%

Recurrence risks are 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, and 90% after 3 episodes.

**Q:** What is the recurrence risk of depression after 3 episodes?

**A:** 90%

After 3 episodes of depression, the recurrence risk is 90%.

**Q:** What is vortioxetine (Trintellix) classified as?

**A:** SSRI

Vortioxetine (Trintellix) is an SSRI that blocks serotonin reuptake.

**Q:** What is the first-line SSRI for youth with depression?

**A:** Fluoxetine

Fluoxetine is the first-line SSRI for youth with depression, while paroxetine is not recommended for this age group due to controversy.

**Q:** Which SSRI requires a baseline ECG due to QT interval prolongation risk?

**A:** Citalopram

Citalopram can prolong QT interval, so a baseline ECG is recommended before starting treatment.

**Q:** Which antidepressant is often chosen for lack of sexual side effects and can be used for augmentation?

**A:** Bupropion

Bupropion blocks dopamine and norepinephrine reuptake, causing less sexual dysfunction than SSRIs and can augment other antidepressants.

**Q:** What is the recurrence rate of depression within 6-12 months?

**A:** Up to 40% resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months

Depression has a significant spontaneous remission rate, with up to 40% resolving without treatment within 6-12 months.

**Q:** What is the role of bupropion in depression treatment?

**A:** Often chosen for lack of sexual side effects, can be used for augmentation of antidepressant effects with other classes

Bupropion is particularly useful when sexual side effects are problematic or for augmentation strategies.

**Q:** What is a serious psychiatric side effect of varenicline?

**A:** Increased risk of psychosis, depression, and suicidal ideation

Varenicline carries a black box warning for psychiatric effects; patients with pre-existing psychiatric conditions should be monitored closely.

**Q:** What is the first-line SSRI therapy for youth according to Toronto Notes 2025?

**A:** Fluoxetine (Prozac®)

Fluoxetine is recommended as first-line SSRI therapy for youth, while paroxetine is not recommended due to controversy.

**Q:** What is a notable advantage of paroxetine among SSRIs?

**A:** It has a short half-life allowing for quick discontinuation

Paroxetine has the shortest half-life among SSRIs, which can be advantageous when quick discontinuation is needed.

**Q:** Which SSRI requires baseline ECG monitoring due to QT interval prolongation?

**A:** Citalopram

Citalopram can increase QT interval, so a baseline ECG is suggested before initiating therapy.

**Q:** Which SSRI is NOT recommended for youth according to Canadian guidelines?

**A:** Paroxetine (Paxil®)

Paroxetine is not recommended for youth due to controversial safety profile in this population.

**Q:** What percentage of depression resolves spontaneously within 6-12 months?

**A:** Up to 40%

Up to 40% of depression cases resolve spontaneously without intervention within 6-12 months.

**Q:** What is the first-line therapy for depression in youth?

**A:** Fluoxetine

Fluoxetine is the first-line SSRI for pediatric depression due to better safety profile.

**Q:** What is the prognosis for depression?

**A:** Up to 40% resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months; recurrence risk: 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, 90% after 3 episodes

Depression has high recurrence rates, increasing significantly with each episode.

**Q:** What is the first-line therapy for youth with depression?

**A:** Fluoxetine

Fluoxetine is the first-line SSRI for children and adolescents due to its safety profile and FDA approval for pediatric use.

**Q:** Which antidepressant should not be recommended for youth?

**A:** Paroxetine

Paroxetine is controversial/not recommended for youth due to increased risk of suicidal ideation.

**Q:** What is the baseline ECG recommendation when prescribing SSRIs?

**A:** Baseline ECG is suggested due to QT interval prolongation risk

Some SSRIs (particularly citalopram) can prolong QT interval, necessitating baseline ECG in at-risk patients.

**Q:** Can bupropion be used for augmentation of other antidepressants?

**A:** Yes, often chosen for augmentation due to lack of sexual side effects

Bupropion's unique mechanism (dopamine/norepinephrine reuptake inhibition) makes it useful for augmentation.

**Q:** What class of medication is vortioxetine?

**A:** SSRI (but also has multimodal action)

Vortioxetine is classified as an SSRI but also acts as a serotonin receptor modulator.

**Q:** What is a common side effect of TCAs that SSRIs do not typically cause?

**A:** Significant weight gain

TCAs commonly cause weight gain and anticholinergic effects, unlike most SSRIs which may cause weight loss.

**Q:** What is the prognosis for untreated major depressive disorder?

**A:** Up to 40% resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months

Without treatment, approximately 40% of major depressive episodes will resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months.

**Q:** What are the recurrence risks of major depressive disorder?

**A:** 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, 90% after 3 episodes

The risk of recurrence increases with each episode: 50% after the first, 70% after the second, and 90% after the third episode.

**Q:** What are common medication causes of constipation?

**A:** Narcotics and antidepressants are the most common

Medication side effects are the most common cause of organic constipation. Opioids cause significant slowed motility.

**Q:** Which antidepressants are used for IBS pain?

**A:** Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) - first-line; SSRIs only for concomitant anxiety/depression; SNRIs also used

TCAs help visceral hypersensitivity and pain in IBS. SSRIs have no evidence for pain improvement in IBS alone.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of constipation?

**A:** Most common is functional/idiopathic constipation; medication side effects (narcotics, antidepressants) are the most common organic cause

Secondary causes should be considered when other symptoms accompany constipation.

**Q:** What is the role of TCAs in IBS treatment and when are they indicated?

**A:** Tricyclic antidepressants are used for IBS, particularly when pain is predominant; helpful for concomitant anxiety or depression

TCAs have analgesic and anticholinergic effects that can help IBS symptoms, especially in IBS-D.

**Q:** When are SSRIs indicated in IBS treatment?

**A:** SSRIs are only helpful to treat concomitant anxiety or depression in IBS patients; no evidence they help pain alone

Unlike TCAs, SSRIs have no independent analgesic effect for IBS pain.

**Q:** What are common drug causes of constipation?

**A:** Narcotics and antidepressants are the most common medication causes

Opioids cause constipation by reducing gut motility. Many antidepressants (especially TCAs) also slow intestinal transit.

**Q:** List the common drug causes of constipation

**A:** Narcotics and antidepressants are the most common medication causes

Medication side effects are the most common cause of constipation overall.

**Q:** What antidepressants are used for IBS and what is their role?

**A:** TCAs for pain-predominant IBS; SSRIs for concomitant anxiety/depression; SNRIs also used

TCAs help with pain in IBS but SSRIs have no evidence for pain improvement - only for anxiety/depression.

**Q:** What is the most common organic cause of constipation?

**A:** Medication side effects (e.g., narcotics, antidepressants)

Medication-induced constipation is the most common identifiable organic cause, highlighting the importance of medication review.

**Q:** What is the role of SSRIs in IBS treatment?

**A:** SSRIs are only helpful to treat concomitant anxiety or depression in IBS patients

There is no evidence that SSRIs help pain in IBS when used alone. They may benefit patients with comorbid anxiety or depression.

**Q:** What medications are most commonly responsible for drug-induced constipation?

**A:** Narcotics (opioids) and antidepressants

Medication side effects are the most common cause of secondary constipation. Opioids cause constipation through decreased gut motility.

**Q:** Which antidepressants are used in IBS treatment and what is their indication?

**A:** TCAs (tricyclic antidepressants) for pain-predominant IBS; SSRIs for concomitant anxiety/depression; SNRIs for pain.

TCAs help pain through anticholinergic and analgesic effects; SSRIs only help when anxiety/depression is primary.

**Q:** Which antidepressant class is first-line for IBS pain modulation?

**A:** Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs)

TCAs modulate visceral pain pathways and are first-line for IBS pain. SSRIs only help if comorbid anxiety or depression.

**Q:** List common medication causes of constipation.

**A:** Narcotics and antidepressants are the most common medication causes of constipation.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are used for IBS with predominant pain?

**A:** Antispasmodics (hyoscine, pinaverium, trimebutine) before meals; tricyclic antidepressants (TCA); SNRIs; SSRIs for comorbid anxiety/depression.

TCAs help with pain and diarrhea-predominant IBS through anticholinergic effects and visceral hypersensitivity modulation.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are first-line for IBS with predominant pain?

**A:** Antispasmodics before meals (hyoscine, pinaverium, trimebutine), tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs), or SSRIs for concomitant anxiety/depression

Antispasmodics target smooth muscle spasms. TCAs help visceral hypersensitivity and pain modulation. SSRIs only help if anxiety or depression is present.

**Q:** What does the mnemonic DOPED stand for regarding causes of constipation?

**A:** Drugs, Obstruction, Pain, Endocrine dysfunction, Depression

DOPED is a useful mnemonic for secondary causes of constipation. Medications (especially narcotics and antidepressants) are the most common organic cause.

**Q:** Which antidepressants are used in IBS treatment and what is their purpose?

**A:** TCAs (tricyclic antidepressants) for pain; SSRIs for concomitant anxiety/depression; SNRIs for pain and mood.

TCAs help pain via gut neuromodulation; SSRIs/SNRIs treat comorbid mood disorders but SSRIs alone don't help IBS pain.

**Q:** What does the acronym MOAPS represent in the psychiatric review of systems?

**A:** MOAPS: Mood, Orientation/cognition (implied), Affect, Psychosis, Safety.

The MOAPS framework ensures systematic coverage of psychiatric domains during an emergency assessment. Each domain guides targeted history-taking and examination to identify the most pressing clinical concern.

**Q:** What is the first-line antidepressant for youth with depression, and which SSRI is NOT recommended for this population?

**A:** First-line SSRI for youth is fluoxetine. Paroxetine is NOT recommended for youth (controversial).

Fluoxetine has the best evidence in pediatric populations. Paroxetine has been associated with increased suicidal ideation in youth and is considered controversial for this age group by Canadian guidelines.

**Q:** List the mnemonic M-SIGECAPS for the diagnostic criteria of major depressive disorder.

**A:** M - Depressed Mood; S - Increased/decreased Sleep; I - Decreased Interest; G - Guilt/worthlessness; E - decreased Energy; C - poor Concentration; A - increased/decreased Appetite and weight; P - Psychomotor agitation/retardation; S - Suicidal ideation. Requires depressed mood for ≥2 weeks.

M-SIGECAPS is a widely used mnemonic for DSM-5 criteria of MDD. A diagnosis requires 5 or more symptoms including depressed mood or anhedonia, present for at least 2 weeks.

**Q:** What are the risks of recurrence after episodes of major depressive disorder?

**A:** 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, 90% after 3 episodes. Up to 40% resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months.

The risk of recurrence increases substantially with each episode of MDD. After 3 episodes, the risk is 90%, emphasizing the importance of long-term maintenance therapy in recurrent depression.

**Q:** What are the side effects of SSRIs and what baseline investigation is suggested before starting?

**A:** Side effects: sexual dysfunction (impotence, decreased libido, delayed ejaculation, anorgasmia), headache, GI upset, weight loss, tremors, insomnia, fatigue, increased QT interval. A baseline ECG is suggested due to QT prolongation risk.

SSRIs are generally well-tolerated but the QT prolongation risk (especially citalopram/escitalopram at higher doses) warrants a baseline ECG. Sexual dysfunction is the most common reason for non-compliance.

**Q:** What antidepressant class is often chosen for its lack of sexual side effects and can augment other antidepressants?

**A:** Bupropion (Wellbutrin®) — an NDRI (blocks dopamine and NE reuptake). It has less sexual dysfunction than SSRIs and can be used for augmentation of antidepressant effects with other medication classes.

Bupropion's mechanism of action (dopamine/NE reuptake inhibition rather than serotonin) accounts for its favorable sexual side effect profile. Key side effects include headache, insomnia, nightmares, and seizures (dose-dependent).

**Q:** Why are TCAs considered dangerous compared to newer antidepressants?

**A:** TCAs (e.g., amitriptyline) have a narrow therapeutic window and are lethal in overdose. They also cause sexual dysfunction, weight gain, tremors, tachycardia, and sweating.

TCAs block serotonin and NE reuptake but also block muscarinic, histaminergic, and alpha-adrenergic receptors, causing significant anticholinergic and cardiovascular toxicity. Overdose can cause fatal arrhythmias.

## Psychosis & Schizophrenia

**Q:** A 22-year-old man has 6 months of auditory hallucinations (voices commenting on his actions), persecutory delusions, disorganized speech, and social withdrawal. He has no substance use or medical cause. Diagnosis?

**A:** Schizophrenia (DSM-5: ≥2 characteristic symptoms for ≥1 month, with continuous disturbance for ≥6 months including prodrome); distinguish from schizophreniform (<6 months) and brief psychotic disorder (<1 month)

CANMAT Schizophrenia Guidelines: schizophrenia is a chronic disorder affecting 1% of the population. Early psychosis intervention (EPI) programs improve outcomes. Antipsychotic medication is the cornerstone of treatment. Psychosocial interventions (CBT, supported employment, family therapy) are essential adjuncts.

**Q:** What are positive vs. negative symptoms of schizophrenia?

**A:** Positive (excess): hallucinations, delusions, disorganized speech/behavior, catatonia; Negative (deficit): avolition, alogia, anhedonia, affective flattening, asociality — negative symptoms respond less to antipsychotics and predict functional disability

CANMAT guidelines: negative symptoms are associated with poor functional outcome and quality of life. Antipsychotics primarily improve positive symptoms. Paliperidone, amisulpride, and clozapine have modest benefits for negative symptoms. Depression must be differentiated from negative symptoms.

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacological treatment for a first episode of psychosis?

**A:** Second-generation antipsychotic (SGA) at the lowest effective dose: olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine, aripiprazole — start low, go slow; avoid FGA (first-generation) as first-line due to EPS risk

CANMAT guidelines: SGAs are preferred for first episode psychosis (lower EPS/tardive dyskinesia risk). Clozapine is reserved for treatment-resistant schizophrenia (failed ≥2 adequate antipsychotic trials). Long-acting injectable (LAI) antipsychotics improve adherence — consider early.

**Q:** What is clozapine and when is it indicated?

**A:** Clozapine: most effective antipsychotic; indicated for treatment-resistant schizophrenia (failed ≥2 antipsychotics including ≥1 SGA at adequate doses/duration); must monitor ANC (absolute neutrophil count) weekly x26 weeks due to agranulocytosis risk

CANMAT guidelines: clozapine is the only antipsychotic proven effective for treatment-resistant schizophrenia (30% of cases). Risk of agranulocytosis: 0.8% (fatal in 0.1%). Other serious side effects: seizures (dose-related), myocarditis, metabolic syndrome. REMS (Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy) program required.

**Q:** What are the extrapyramidal side effects (EPS) of antipsychotics and how are they treated?

**A:** Acute dystonia (hours-days): diphenhydramine or benztropine IM; Akathisia (inner restlessness): beta-blockers (propranolol), benzodiazepines; Parkinsonism: reduce dose or switch to SGA; Tardive dyskinesia (months-years): switch to clozapine, valbenazine

CANMAT guidelines: EPS are more common with FGAs (haloperidol) than SGAs. Tardive dyskinesia is the most serious irreversible EPS — characterized by repetitive involuntary movements (orofacial, limb). AIMS (Abnormal Involuntary Movement Scale) monitoring required.

**Q:** What is neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) and how is it treated?

**A:** NMS: rare, life-threatening reaction to antipsychotics — hyperthermia, severe muscle rigidity (lead pipe), autonomic instability (tachycardia, BP changes), altered consciousness; treat by stopping antipsychotic, cooling, IV dantrolene or bromocriptine, ICU supportive care

NMS occurs with FGAs > SGAs. Risk factors: rapid dose escalation, dehydration, agitation, high potency antipsychotics. Labs: elevated CK (>1000 IU/L), leukocytosis, elevated LFTs. Distinguished from serotonin syndrome: slower onset, more rigidity, no clonus. Do NOT restart antipsychotic for 2 weeks.

**Q:** What metabolic monitoring is required for patients on second-generation antipsychotics?

**A:** Baseline and every 3 months: weight/BMI, waist circumference; fasting glucose, lipid panel at baseline and 3 months; BP, HbA1c annually — olanzapine, clozapine, quetiapine have highest metabolic risk

CANMAT guidelines: metabolic syndrome occurs in 40-50% of schizophrenia patients. SGAs (especially clozapine, olanzapine) cause weight gain, hyperglycemia, and dyslipidemia. Aripiprazole and ziprasidone have the lowest metabolic risk. Metformin effective for SGA-induced weight gain.

**Q:** What is schizoaffective disorder and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Schizoaffective disorder: uninterrupted period of illness with both psychotic symptoms (hallucinations/delusions) AND a major mood episode (depressive or manic), with ≥2 weeks of psychosis WITHOUT prominent mood symptoms

Schizoaffective disorder sits between schizophrenia and bipolar disorder. Distinguished from bipolar with psychotic features: psychosis must occur independently of mood episodes. Treatment: antipsychotic + mood stabilizer (for bipolar type) or antipsychotic + antidepressant (depressive type).

**Q:** What is brief psychotic disorder and what is the key distinguishing feature from schizophrenia?

**A:** Brief psychotic disorder: sudden onset psychotic symptoms lasting ≥1 day but <1 month, with full return to premorbid functioning; often triggered by severe stressor

Brief psychotic disorder has excellent prognosis with full recovery. Distinguished from schizophrenia by duration (<1 month) and full recovery. Schizophreniform disorder: 1-6 months. Treatment: short-term low-dose antipsychotic, supportive therapy, address stressor.

**Q:** What psychosocial interventions are evidence-based for schizophrenia?

**A:** CBT for psychosis (CBTp), family-based therapy, supported employment (IPS model), cognitive remediation, social skills training, assertive community treatment (ACT)

CANMAT guidelines recommend multimodal treatment for schizophrenia — antipsychotics alone are insufficient. ACT (Assertive Community Treatment) reduces hospitalizations and homelessness. Supported employment (IPS) helps patients return to work. Family psychoeducation reduces relapse rates by 20-30%.

**Q:** A 30-year-old on haloperidol 10mg/day develops neck stiffening and oculogyric crisis 2 hours after a dose increase. What is the diagnosis and immediate treatment?

**A:** Acute dystonic reaction; IV or IM benztropine 2mg or diphenhydramine 50mg — relief within 15-20 minutes; switch to SGA or lower dose

Acute dystonia is more common with high-potency FGAs (haloperidol, trifluoperazine) and in young males. Laryngeal dystonia can cause airway compromise — emergency. Oculogyric crisis: involuntary eye deviation. Prophylactic benztropine may be given with first doses of high-potency antipsychotics.

**Q:** What is the role of long-acting injectable (LAI) antipsychotics in schizophrenia?

**A:** LAI antipsychotics (aripiprazole, paliperidone, haloperidol decanoate given every 2-4 weeks) improve adherence, reduce relapse, and decrease hospitalizations — consider for any patient with adherence issues

CANMAT guidelines: LAI antipsychotics eliminate covert non-adherence. Non-adherence is the #1 predictor of relapse in schizophrenia. LAI should be offered as an option to all patients with schizophrenia, not just non-adherent ones. LAI may be preferable as first-line in high-relapse-risk patients.

**Q:** What is the difference between delusions and overvalued ideas?

**A:** Delusion: fixed false belief held with absolute certainty, not shared by cultural group, not amenable to reason; Overvalued idea: intense preoccupation that is understandable given history but abnormally dominant (borderline between delusion and OCD)

Delusions are classified by content (persecutory, referential, grandiose, erotomanic, somatic) and quality (bizarre or non-bizarre). Bizarre delusions (impossible given cultural context) are more specific to schizophrenia. Overvalued ideas are seen in OCD, anorexia, and body dysmorphic disorder.

**Q:** What are the phases of schizophrenia and what is the prodrome?

**A:** Prodrome: 1-5 years before psychosis — social withdrawal, odd behavior, attenuated psychotic symptoms, decline in functioning; Acute phase: florid positive symptoms; Residual phase: negative symptoms and reduced positive symptoms

CANMAT Early Psychosis Guidelines: identifying the prodrome allows intervention before full psychosis. At-Risk Mental State (ARMS) clinics provide early intervention. Duration of Untreated Psychosis (DUP) correlates with worse outcomes — reduce DUP is a key public health goal.

**Q:** What is the management of acute agitation with psychosis in the ED?

**A:** De-escalation first; pharmacological: oral or IM haloperidol + lorazepam (combined most effective), or olanzapine IM (avoid with alcohol/benzodiazepines), or droperidol IM — monitor for EPS, respiratory depression

CPS/CANMAT emergency psychiatry guidelines: verbal de-escalation is the first approach for agitation. Parenteral antipsychotics for severe agitation. Olanzapine IM + parenteral benzodiazepine increases respiratory depression risk — do not combine. ECG monitoring for QTc prolongation with IV haloperidol.

**Q:** What are the key side effects of antipsychotics?

**A:** Typical (haloperidol): EPS (dystonia, akathisia, parkinsonism, tardive dyskinesia), NMS. Atypical (olanzapine, quetiapine): metabolic syndrome (weight gain, diabetes, dyslipidemia)

NMS: fever, rigidity, autonomic instability, elevated CK. Treat: dantrolene + bromocriptine. Clozapine for treatment-resistant schizophrenia — monitor for agranulocytosis (weekly CBC initially).

**Q:** What are DSM-5 criteria for schizophrenia?

**A:** >=2 of: delusions, hallucinations, disorganized speech, disorganized/catatonic behavior, negative symptoms. At least 1 must be delusions/hallucinations/disorganized speech. Duration >=6 months

Active phase >=1 month. Rule out schizoaffective, mood disorders with psychotic features, substance use, medical conditions. First-line: second-generation antipsychotics.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for schizophrenia?

**A:** >=2 of the following (>=1 must be 1, 2, or 3): (1) delusions, (2) hallucinations, (3) disorganized speech, (4) grossly disorganized or catatonic behavior, (5) negative symptoms (flat affect, alogia, avolition). Duration: continuous signs >=6 months with >=1 month of active symptoms. Functional decline from premorbid level. Rule out schizoaffective, mood disorders, substance, medical causes.

Schizophrenia affects ~1% of the population. Canadian guidelines emphasize early detection and intervention. Duration criterion (6 months) distinguishes schizophrenia from schizophreniform disorder (<6 months) and brief psychotic disorder (<1 month). Negative symptoms are most disabling and hardest to treat.

**Q:** What are the first-line and second-line antipsychotics for schizophrenia?

**A:** First-line: atypical (second-generation) antipsychotics-risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, aripiprazole, paliperidone, ziprasidone, lurasidone. All have similar efficacy for positive symptoms. Second-line: typical (first-generation)-haloperidol, chlorpromazine, fluphenazine. Treatment-resistant: clozapine (gold standard after failure of >=2 adequate antipsychotic trials at adequate dose/duration).

Canadian Schizophrenia Guidelines recommend SGAs as first-line due to lower EPS risk. Choice is based on side effect profile. Long-acting injectable (LAI) antipsychotics (paliperidone palmitate, aripiprazole lauroxil) improve adherence. Clozapine is the only antipsychotic with proven superiority for treatment-resistant schizophrenia.

**Q:** What are the major side effects of antipsychotic medications by class?

**A:** Typical/FGA: extrapyramidal symptoms (acute dystonia, akathisia, parkinsonism), tardive dyskinesia, neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Atypical/SGA: metabolic syndrome (weight gain, dyslipidemia, hyperglycemia-worst with olanzapine and clozapine), QTc prolongation (ziprasidone), hyperprolactinemia (risperidone, paliperidone), sedation (quetiapine, olanzapine).

Metabolic monitoring is mandatory per Canadian Psychiatric Association: weight, waist circumference, fasting glucose, lipids at baseline, 3 months, then annually. Olanzapine and clozapine carry highest metabolic risk. Aripiprazole and ziprasidone are most weight-neutral. EPS treated with benztropine or benzodiazepines.

**Q:** What is neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) and how is it managed?

**A:** Clinical features: lead-pipe rigidity, hyperthermia (>38°C), autonomic instability (tachycardia, labile BP, diaphoresis), altered mental status. Labs: elevated CK (often >1000), leukocytosis, elevated LFTs, metabolic acidosis, myoglobinuria. Onset: days to weeks after antipsychotic initiation or dose increase. Management: stop antipsychotic, supportive care (cooling, IV fluids), dantrolene (muscle relaxant), bromocriptine (dopamine agonist).

NMS has 10-20% mortality if untreated. Differentiate from serotonin syndrome: NMS has lead-pipe rigidity (not clonus) and slow onset. Risk factors: high-potency FGA (haloperidol), dehydration, rapid dose escalation. Can recur-restart antipsychotic cautiously after >=2 weeks, different class, low dose.

**Q:** What is the monitoring protocol for clozapine?

**A:** Mandatory ANC monitoring (risk of agranulocytosis ~1%): weekly CBC for first 26 weeks, then q2 weeks for next 26 weeks, then monthly. Hold clozapine if ANC <1500 (non-benign ethnic neutropenia) or <1000 (BEN). Other monitoring: fasting glucose/lipids q3-6mo, weight monthly, ECG (myocarditis risk in first month), bowel function (constipation/ileus). Annual echocardiogram in some protocols.

Clozapine is the gold standard for treatment-resistant schizophrenia (30% respond who failed other antipsychotics). Unique side effects: agranulocytosis, myocarditis (first month), metabolic syndrome, seizures (dose-dependent), sialorrhea, severe constipation/ileus. Canadian Clozapine Registry tracks all patients.

**Q:** What is the approach to first-episode psychosis in Canada?

**A:** Early intervention is critical: assess safety, medical workup (CBC, electrolytes, TSH, B12, HIV, syphilis, urine drug screen, CT/MRI brain if indicated). Start low-dose atypical antipsychotic. Refer to early psychosis intervention (EPI) program. Psychoeducation, family involvement, CBT for psychosis. Monitor closely for 2 years. Consider substance-induced psychosis as alternative diagnosis.

Canadian EPI programs (available in most provinces) improve outcomes through early identification and comprehensive treatment. First episode patients are more responsive and sensitive to antipsychotics-use lower doses. Duration of untreated psychosis (DUP) is the strongest modifiable predictor of outcome.

**Q:** What are the key differences between positive and negative symptoms of schizophrenia and their treatment implications?

**A:** Positive symptoms: delusions, hallucinations, disorganized speech/behavior-respond well to antipsychotics. Negative symptoms: flat affect, alogia (poverty of speech), avolition (lack of motivation), anhedonia, social withdrawal-poorly responsive to antipsychotics (except clozapine has modest benefit). Cognitive symptoms: impaired attention, memory, executive function-poorly responsive to medications.

Negative and cognitive symptoms are the primary determinants of functional disability in schizophrenia. Canadian Schizophrenia Guidelines recommend psychosocial interventions (supported employment, social skills training, cognitive remediation) for these domains. Cariprazine may have modest benefit for negative symptoms.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis of psychosis?

**A:** Primary psychiatric: schizophrenia, schizoaffective, brief psychotic disorder, delusional disorder, bipolar with psychotic features, MDD with psychotic features. Substance-induced: cannabis, methamphetamine, cocaine, hallucinogens, alcohol withdrawal (DT), corticosteroids. Medical: delirium (most common in hospital), autoimmune encephalitis (anti-NMDAR), CNS infections, brain tumor, temporal lobe epilepsy, metabolic (hepatic encephalopathy, porphyria), endocrine (thyroid, Cushing).

Canadian guidelines emphasize thorough medical workup before diagnosing primary psychotic disorder. Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis is an increasingly recognized cause of psychosis, especially in young women. Substance-induced psychosis: if symptoms persist >1 month after cessation, consider primary psychotic disorder.

**Q:** What is schizoaffective disorder and how does it differ from schizophrenia and mood disorders?

**A:** DSM-5: concurrent period with mood episode (major depressive or manic) AND delusions/hallucinations, PLUS >=2 weeks of delusions/hallucinations WITHOUT mood symptoms (key distinguishing criterion). If psychosis only during mood episodes = mood disorder with psychotic features. If mood symptoms brief relative to psychosis = schizophrenia.

Schizoaffective disorder is often a diagnostic challenge. Treatment combines antipsychotic + mood stabilizer or antidepressant depending on subtype (bipolar vs depressive). Canadian clinical practice often revisits this diagnosis over time as longitudinal course clarifies the picture.

**Q:** What are the types of extrapyramidal symptoms (EPS) and their management?

**A:** Acute dystonia (hours-days): sustained muscle contractions (torticollis, oculogyric crisis). Treat: benztropine 1-2mg IM/IV or diphenhydramine 50mg IM. Akathisia (days-weeks): inner restlessness, inability to sit still. Treat: reduce dose, switch antipsychotic, propranolol, benzodiazepine, mirtazapine. Parkinsonism (weeks-months): bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor. Treat: reduce dose, benztropine. Tardive dyskinesia (months-years): involuntary choreoathetoid movements (lip smacking, tongue protrusion). Treat: switch to clozapine/quetiapine, valbenazine, deutetrabenazine.
