EPS are more common with high-potency FGA (haloperidol) but can occur with any antipsychotic. TD is potentially irreversible-screen regularly with AIMS (Abnormal Involuntary Movement Scale). Valbenazine is approved in Canada for TD treatment. Young males are highest risk for acute dystonia.

**Q:** What are the long-acting injectable (LAI) antipsychotic options and their role?

**A:** Available in Canada: paliperidone palmitate (monthly or q3month), aripiprazole lauroxil/monohydrate (monthly or q2month), risperidone LAI (q2 weeks), fluphenazine decanoate (q2-4 weeks), haloperidol decanoate (q4 weeks). Role: non-adherence (most common cause of relapse), patient preference, forensic settings, first-episode psychosis (CANMAT recommends early consideration).

LAI antipsychotics reduce relapse and rehospitalization by 30-50% compared to oral formulations. CANMAT now recommends discussing LAI early in treatment, not just after non-adherence. Overlap with oral medication is required during initiation (varies by formulation).

**Q:** What psychosocial interventions are recommended for schizophrenia?

**A:** Supported employment (IPS model): most effective for improving employment outcomes. Social skills training. Cognitive behavioral therapy for psychosis (CBTp): reduces positive symptoms and distress. Family psychoeducation: reduces relapse. Assertive community treatment (ACT): for severely ill patients. Cognitive remediation. Peer support programs.

Canadian Schizophrenia Guidelines recommend comprehensive psychosocial programs alongside pharmacotherapy. ACT teams (intensive community-based multidisciplinary teams) are available in most Canadian provinces. Recovery-oriented care is the guiding philosophy of Canadian mental health policy.

**Q:** What is the approach to catatonia?

**A:** Features: immobility/stupor, mutism, posturing, waxy flexibility, negativism, staring, stereotypies, echolalia, echopraxia. Can occur in schizophrenia, mood disorders, or medical conditions. Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale for diagnosis. Treatment: (1) lorazepam challenge test (1-2mg IV-diagnostic and therapeutic, response within minutes confirms catatonia), (2) if no response: ECT (highly effective, potentially life-saving for malignant catatonia).

Catatonia is a medical emergency when malignant (autonomic instability, hyperthermia, rhabdomyolysis). AVOID antipsychotics in acute catatonia (can worsen symptoms or precipitate NMS). Benzodiazepines are first-line. ECT has response rates >80% in catatonia and should not be delayed if benzodiazepines fail.

**Q:** What are the metabolic monitoring requirements for patients on antipsychotics?

**A:** Baseline: weight, BMI, waist circumference, fasting glucose, HbA1c, lipid profile, BP. 1 month: weight. 3 months: weight, fasting glucose, lipids. 6 months: weight, fasting glucose, lipids. Annually: all of the above + HbA1c. If significant metabolic changes: lifestyle interventions, consider switch to metabolically neutral antipsychotic (aripiprazole, ziprasidone), consider metformin.

Canadian Psychiatric Association consensus guidelines mandate metabolic monitoring. Patients on antipsychotics have 20-year reduced life expectancy, largely due to cardiovascular disease. Metabolic syndrome rates: 30-40% in schizophrenia. Metformin (off-label) can reduce antipsychotic-associated weight gain by 3-4 kg.

**Q:** What is the clozapine monitoring protocol?

**A:** Treatment-resistant schizophrenia (failed >=2 antipsychotics). Mandatory: weekly CBC for 26 weeks, then biweekly for 26 weeks, then monthly. Monitor for: agranulocytosis (ANC<500), metabolic syndrome, myocarditis, seizures

Agranulocytosis risk ~1%. Stop if ANC<1000, do not rechallenge if <500. Also monitor: fasting glucose/lipids q3-6mo, weight monthly, ECG at baseline. Despite monitoring burden, clozapine reduces suicide in schizophrenia.

**Q:** What is the recommended workup for first-episode psychosis?

**A:** Complete medical workup: CBC, electrolytes, glucose, renal/liver function, TSH, calcium, urinalysis, urine toxicology screen, syphilis serology, HIV (if risk factors), B12/folate. Neuroimaging: CT or MRI brain (rule out structural causes). ECG (baseline QTc before antipsychotics). Neuropsychological testing when stabilized.

Canadian guidelines (Schizophrenia Society of Canada, CANMAT) recommend thorough medical investigation to exclude organic causes. Up to 5% of first-episode psychosis has a medical etiology. Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis should be considered in young women (CSF antibodies). Early intervention programs improve outcomes — target DUP (duration of untreated psychosis) <3 months.

**Q:** What is required for clozapine monitoring and what are the key risks?

**A:** Mandatory: absolute neutrophil count (ANC) weekly for first 26 weeks, biweekly for weeks 27-52, then monthly. Must be >=1.5 to continue (>=1.0 for benign ethnic neutropenia). Key risks: agranulocytosis (1-2%), metabolic syndrome, myocarditis (first 4-6 weeks — monitor troponin/CRP), seizures (dose-dependent), sedation, constipation (can be fatal — ileus).

Clozapine is the gold standard for treatment-resistant schizophrenia (failed >=2 adequate antipsychotic trials). CANMAT recommends not delaying clozapine trial unnecessarily. In Canada, clozapine dispensing requires enrollment in a monitoring program (Clozaril Support and Assistance Network). Clozapine reduces suicidality in schizophrenia (InterSePT trial).

**Q:** What is brief psychotic disorder and how does it differ from schizophreniform disorder?

**A:** Brief psychotic disorder: >=1 psychotic symptom (delusions, hallucinations, disorganized speech/behaviour) lasting 1 day to <1 month, with full return to premorbid function. Schizophreniform: same symptoms lasting 1-6 months. Schizophrenia: symptoms >=6 months. Stressor may or may not be present for brief psychotic disorder.

DSM-5 distinguishes these by duration. Brief psychotic disorder has the best prognosis (50% never have another episode). Short-term antipsychotic treatment (1-3 months) is recommended. Must rule out substance-induced and medical causes. Follow-up is essential as some patients progress to schizophrenia or bipolar disorder.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic features of delusional disorder?

**A:** Non-bizarre delusions (plausible situations: persecutory, jealous, erotomanic, grandiose, somatic) lasting >=1 month. Functioning relatively preserved apart from impact of the delusion. No prominent hallucinations, disorganized speech, or negative symptoms. Does not meet criteria for schizophrenia. Subtypes guide clinical approach.

Delusional disorder is uncommon but clinically challenging due to preserved functioning and encapsulated delusions. Patients rarely present voluntarily. Treatment response to antipsychotics is modest (~50% partial response). CBT may be beneficial. Forensic implications exist especially with jealous and erotomanic subtypes.

**Q:** How do you differentiate substance-induced psychosis from primary psychotic disorder?

**A:** Substance-induced: temporal relationship (onset during intoxication/withdrawal), resolves with sustained abstinence (usually within 1 month), urine drug screen positive, visual hallucinations more common. Primary: symptoms persist beyond 1 month of abstinence, auditory hallucinations predominate, negative symptoms present, premorbid decline.

Cannabis, methamphetamine, cocaine, and alcohol (withdrawal) are common causes of substance-induced psychosis in Canada. Up to 25% of cannabis-induced psychosis converts to schizophrenia. CANMAT recommends monitoring patients with substance-induced psychosis for 6 months for conversion. Methamphetamine psychosis can persist for months.

**Q:** What are the metabolic side effects of atypical antipsychotics and how should they be monitored?

**A:** Weight gain (worst: clozapine, olanzapine), dyslipidemia, hyperglycemia/type 2 diabetes, metabolic syndrome. Monitoring: weight/BMI at baseline and monthly for 3 months then quarterly, fasting glucose and lipids at baseline, 3 months, then annually, waist circumference, blood pressure.

CANMAT metabolic monitoring guidelines are often underimplemented. Metabolic syndrome affects 30-50% of patients on atypical antipsychotics. Switching from olanzapine to aripiprazole or ziprasidone can improve metabolic profile. Metformin can be added for antipsychotic-associated weight gain. Cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death in schizophrenia.

**Q:** What is the CANMAT-recommended approach to first-episode schizophrenia pharmacotherapy?

**A:** Start a single second-generation antipsychotic (SGA) at low dose, titrate slowly. First-line: risperidone, paliperidone, aripiprazole, quetiapine, olanzapine (considering side effect profile). Lower doses effective in first-episode vs chronic. Continue for >=18 months after remission. Consider long-acting injectable (LAI) for adherence.

First-episode patients are more medication-sensitive and respond to lower doses. Treatment response rate in first episode is 70-80% vs 30-40% in chronic schizophrenia. CANMAT emphasizes early intervention services including psychoeducation, family therapy, and vocational rehabilitation. Clozapine should be tried after 2 failed adequate trials.

**Q:** What are the extrapyramidal side effects (EPS) of antipsychotics and their management?

**A:** Acute dystonia (hours-days): treat with benztropine 1-2 mg IM or diphenhydramine 50 mg IM. Akathisia (days-weeks): reduce dose, switch agent, or add propranolol/benzodiazepine. Parkinsonism (weeks): reduce dose or add anticholinergic. Tardive dyskinesia (months-years): switch to lower-risk agent, consider valbenazine or deutetrabenazine.

EPS are more common with first-generation antipsychotics (haloperidol) but occur with all antipsychotics. Akathisia is often misdiagnosed as agitation and is a risk factor for suicidality. Tardive dyskinesia affects 20-30% on long-term typical antipsychotics. VMAT2 inhibitors (valbenazine) are Health Canada-approved for TD treatment.

**Q:** What is neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) and how is it managed?

**A:** NMS: hyperthermia (>38C), severe muscle rigidity (lead-pipe), altered mental status, autonomic instability (tachycardia, labile BP, diaphoresis). Labs: elevated CK (often >1000), leukocytosis, elevated LFTs. Management: stop all antipsychotics/dopamine-blocking agents immediately, IV fluids, cooling, dantrolene (muscle relaxant), bromocriptine (dopamine agonist), ICU monitoring.

NMS is a rare but potentially fatal (10-20% mortality) reaction to dopamine-blocking agents. Risk factors include high-potency FGAs, rapid dose escalation, dehydration, and agitation. Distinguish from serotonin syndrome (clonus and hyperreflexia predominate vs rigidity in NMS). Can rechallenge with a different antipsychotic after 2 weeks at low dose.

**Q:** What long-acting injectable antipsychotics are available in Canada and what are their advantages?

**A:** Available: paliperidone palmitate (monthly or 3-monthly), aripiprazole lauroxil (monthly or bimonthly), risperidone microspheres (biweekly), flupentixol decanoate, haloperidol decanoate. Advantages: improved adherence, reduced relapse and hospitalization, steady drug levels, reduced mortality.

CANMAT and Canadian Schizophrenia guidelines increasingly recommend LAI early in treatment (not just as last resort). PROACTIVE and PRELAPSE studies support LAI for first-episode patients. Non-adherence is the strongest predictor of relapse. LAI reduces relapse by 50% compared to oral antipsychotics in real-world studies.

**Q:** What are causes of secondary psychosis (medical causes)?

**A:** Delirium, substance intoxication/withdrawal, medications (steroids, anticholinergics), CNS infections, metabolic disorders, structural lesions, autoimmune encephalitis

Psychosis can be primary or secondary to medical conditions; medical causes must be ruled out

**Q:** What are the MOAPS components in psychiatric review of systems?

**A:** Mood, Psychosis, Affect, Safety

MOAPS is a framework for systematic psychiatric assessment

**Q:** What are the components of the MOAPS psychiatric review system?

**A:** Mood, Other (organic causes), Affect, Psychosis, Safety

MOAPS is a mnemonic for systematic psychiatric assessment in the emergency department.

**Q:** What are the causes of secondary psychosis?

**A:** Medical conditions (delirium, metabolic disorders), drugs (substance intoxication/withdrawal, medications like steroids), infections (CNS), structural lesions, autoimmune (anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis)

Secondary psychosis must be ruled out before diagnosing primary psychotic disorders. These causes produce psychotic symptoms due to organic brain dysfunction.

**Q:** What are the first-line medications for acute agitation in the ED?

**A:** Benzodiazepines (lorazepam 2mg PO/IM/SL) or antipsychotics (olanzapine 5-10mg PO/IM, haloperidol 5mg PO/IM)

Both classes are effective for acute agitation. Offer medication to patients when possible rather than administering with force to maintain therapeutic alliance.

**Q:** What are the negative symptoms of psychosis?

**A:** Affective flattening, alogia (reduced speech), avolition (lack of motivation)

Negative symptoms represent loss of normal functions and are characteristic of schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders.

**Q:** What are the MOAPS components of psychiatric review?

**A:** Mood, Organic causes, Assessment, Psychosis, Safety

MOAPS is a mnemonic for psychiatric review: Mood, Organic causes (rule out medical), Assessment, Psychosis, Safety (SI/HI).

**Q:** What are the first-line medications for acute agitation in psychiatric emergency?

**A:** Benzodiazepines (lorazepam 2mg) or Antipsychotics (olanzapine 5-10mg, haloperidol 5mg)

First-line treatment for acute agitation includes lorazepam 2mg PO/IM/SL or olanzapine 5-10mg PO/IM or haloperidol 5mg PO/IM.

**Q:** What medications increase risk of T2DM?

**A:** Glucocorticoids, atypical antipsychotics, HAART

These medications can induce insulin resistance or impair beta-cell function, increasing diabetes risk.

**Q:** What medications increase the risk of developing diabetes?

**A:** Glucocorticoids and atypical antipsychotics

Certain medications including glucocorticoids and atypical antipsychotics (e.g., HAART) can increase the risk of developing diabetes.

**Q:** Which medications increase risk of diabetes?

**A:** Glucocorticoids and atypical antipsychotics

Certain medications including glucocorticoids and atypical antipsychotics can increase the risk of developing diabetes.

**Q:** What medications increase risk of diabetes?

**A:** Glucocorticoids and atypical antipsychotics

Glucocorticoids (steroids) and atypical antipsychotics are known to increase the risk of developing diabetes.

**Q:** What are the differential diagnoses for acute psychosis in the ED?

**A:** Primary psychotic disorder (e.g., schizophrenia), secondary to medical condition (e.g., delirium), drugs (substance intoxication/withdrawal, medications such as steroids or anticholinergics), infectious (CNS), metabolic (hypoglycemia, hepatic, renal, thyroid), structural (hemorrhage, neoplasm), autoimmune (anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis).

Organic causes must be excluded before diagnosing a primary psychotic disorder. Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis is an important treatable cause particularly in young women that can mimic schizophrenia and requires LP for diagnosis.

**Q:** What medications are used for chemical restraint of an agitated psychotic patient in the ED?

**A:** Benzodiazepines: lorazepam 2 mg PO/IM/SL. Antipsychotics: olanzapine 5–10 mg PO/IM or haloperidol 5 mg PO/IM. Whenever possible, offer medication voluntarily rather than using force.

Combining a benzodiazepine with an antipsychotic provides rapid sedation and reduces the required dose of each drug. Olanzapine IM should NOT be given with IM benzodiazepines due to risk of respiratory depression. Haloperidol carries risk of QTc prolongation and extrapyramidal effects.

## Anxiety & PTSD

**Q:** A 30-year-old has excessive worry about multiple life domains (work, family, health) that she cannot control, occurring more days than not for 8 months, with muscle tension, fatigue, and sleep difficulty. Diagnosis and first-line treatment?

**A:** Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD); first-line: SSRI (escitalopram, sertraline) or SNRI (venlafaxine, duloxetine) + CBT (cognitive restructuring, worry management)

CANMAT Anxiety Guidelines: CBT is the most effective psychotherapy for GAD. SSRIs/SNRIs take 4-8 weeks to work. Buspirone is an alternative non-benzodiazepine anxiolytic. Benzodiazepines: short-term only, risk of dependence. Pregabalin has evidence for GAD.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for panic disorder?

**A:** Recurrent unexpected panic attacks (≥1 month of concern about future attacks OR behavioral change), at least 1 attack in past month; panic attack: abrupt surge of intense fear peaking within minutes with ≥4 symptoms (palpitations, dyspnea, chest pain, dizziness, paresthesias, depersonalization, fear of dying/going crazy)

CANMAT guidelines: panic disorder responds well to CBT (interoceptive exposure) and SSRIs. Breathing retraining and relaxation are adjuncts. Agoraphobia frequently complicates panic disorder. Benzodiazepines provide rapid relief but do not address underlying pathology and risk dependence.

**Q:** What is the CANMAT recommended pharmacotherapy for panic disorder?

**A:** First-line: SSRIs (paroxetine, sertraline, escitalopram, citalopram) or SNRIs (venlafaxine); second-line: TCAs (imipramine, clomipramine); adjunct: benzodiazepines (short-term only) — avoid abrupt discontinuation

CANMAT panic disorder guidelines: start SSRI at low dose to avoid initial anxiogenic effect (jitteriness — worsen symptoms in first 1-2 weeks). Titrate slowly. Minimum 12 months of pharmacotherapy after remission. Propranolol is NOT effective for panic disorder (only for performance anxiety).

**Q:** A 25-year-old refuses to leave home alone, won't drive, and avoids shopping malls due to fear of having a panic attack in a place she can't escape. Diagnosis?

**A:** Agoraphobia; treat with exposure therapy (graduated real-life exposure is the most effective psychotherapy), SSRIs, or combined approach

Agoraphobia: fear of ≥2 situations (public transport, open spaces, enclosed spaces, crowds, outside alone) due to fear of escape being difficult or help unavailable if panic occurs. Comorbid with panic disorder in 50%. 40% of agoraphobia occurs without panic disorder.

**Q:** What is social anxiety disorder (social phobia) and how is it treated?

**A:** Marked fear of social situations where one may be scrutinized; first-line: CBT (exposure + cognitive restructuring) or SSRI/SNRI; performance-only subtype: propranolol or benzodiazepine PRN

CANMAT: paroxetine, sertraline, and venlafaxine have strongest evidence for SAD. CBT with exposures is highly effective. For performance anxiety: propranolol 10-40 mg PRN (30-60 min before performance). CBT + pharmacotherapy superior to either alone for severe SAD.

**Q:** What are the criteria for specific phobia and what is the most effective treatment?

**A:** Specific phobia: marked fear of specific object/situation (animals, blood-injection-injury, natural environment, situational) causing avoidance, >6 months, disproportionate to actual danger; treatment: exposure therapy (in vivo exposure)

CANMAT: exposure therapy is the gold standard for specific phobia (85% response rate). Imaginal exposure for phobias where in vivo is impractical. Blood-injection-injury phobia: applied tension technique (prevents vasovagal syncope). Medications generally not effective for specific phobia.

**Q:** What is PTSD and what are the DSM-5 criteria?

**A:** PTSD: exposure to trauma (death/threatened death, actual/threatened serious injury, sexual violence) → 4 symptom clusters: Intrusion (flashbacks, nightmares), Avoidance, Negative cognitions/mood, Hyperarousal — lasting >1 month

CANMAT PTSD Guidelines: 70% of Canadians will experience a traumatic event; PTSD develops in ~20%. Risk factors: severity of trauma, peritraumatic dissociation, prior trauma, lack of social support. First-line treatments: prolonged exposure therapy, EMDR, CPT (cognitive processing therapy), SSRIs/SNRIs.

**Q:** What pharmacotherapy is recommended for PTSD?

**A:** SSRIs (sertraline, paroxetine — FDA-approved) and SNRIs (venlafaxine) are first-line; prazosin for PTSD nightmares; benzodiazepines are NOT recommended (no evidence, may worsen PTSD outcomes)

CANMAT PTSD guidelines: psychotherapy (PE, CPT, EMDR) is preferred over pharmacotherapy alone. Prazosin (alpha-1 blocker) reduces PTSD-associated nightmares and sleep disturbance. Benzodiazepines increase avoidance, impair fear extinction, and may worsen long-term outcomes.

**Q:** What is complex PTSD and how does it differ from PTSD?

**A:** Complex PTSD (ICD-11 but not DSM-5): PTSD symptoms + disturbances in self-organization: affect dysregulation, negative self-concept, interpersonal difficulties — results from prolonged, repeated trauma (childhood abuse, domestic violence, torture)

ICD-11 (used in Canada) includes complex PTSD as a separate diagnosis. CANMAT guidelines recognize that treatment is more challenging and requires longer duration. Stabilization phase before trauma processing is crucial. DBT and schema therapy useful for affect dysregulation.

**Q:** A 19-year-old has intrusive contamination obsessions and compulsive hand-washing for 3 hours daily causing significant distress. Diagnosis and first-line treatment?

**A:** OCD (Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder); first-line: CBT with exposure and response prevention (ERP) AND/OR SSRI (fluvoxamine, fluoxetine, sertraline, paroxetine, clomipramine)

CANMAT OCD guidelines: ERP (exposure to feared stimuli + preventing compulsive response) is the most effective psychotherapy for OCD. SSRIs at higher doses than depression (e.g., fluoxetine up to 80 mg). Clomipramine (TCA) most effective SSRI but poorest tolerability. Augmentation with atypical antipsychotics for partial response.

**Q:** What is the difference between OCD and OCD-related disorders?

**A:** OCD-related: Body dysmorphic disorder (preoccupation with perceived defect), hoarding disorder (inability to discard possessions), trichotillomania (hair pulling), excoriation (skin picking), illness anxiety disorder — all involve repetitive behaviors/thoughts

CANMAT/DSM-5: OCD spectrum disorders respond to SSRIs and habit-reversal training (trichotillomania, excoriation). BDD has overlap with social anxiety — treats with SSRI + CBT. Hoarding disorder: SSRI + specialized hoarding-specific CBT; traditional CBT less effective.

**Q:** What is the difference between adjustment disorder and acute stress reaction?

**A:** Acute stress reaction: immediate brief emotional/behavioral response within 3 days of trauma, resolving within 1 month; Adjustment disorder: emotional/behavioral symptoms within 3 months of identifiable stressor, disproportionate, lasting up to 6 months (12 months for chronic stressors)

Adjustment disorder: impairment must exceed expected reaction given the stressor. Does not meet criteria for other mental disorders. Treatment: supportive psychotherapy, brief CBT. Consider if full PTSD, MDD, or anxiety disorder diagnosis is more appropriate.

**Q:** What is health anxiety (illness anxiety disorder) and how is it managed?

**A:** Illness anxiety: preoccupation with having or acquiring a serious illness despite no/minimal medical symptoms; reassurance-seeking reinforces anxiety; treat with CBT (exposure + cognitive restructuring, stop reassurance-seeking) ± SSRI

Formerly somatic symptom disorder with poor illness anxiety (DSM-IV hypochondriasis). CBT targeting reassurance-seeking behaviors is most effective. Physicians should avoid extensive unnecessary testing. Regular brief scheduled appointments instead of symptom-driven visits reduce healthcare utilization.

**Q:** What is separation anxiety disorder in adults and how does it present?

**A:** Separation anxiety disorder (SAD): excessive fear of separation from attachment figures (usually parents or partner); may persist from childhood or develop in adulthood; avoid separation situations, somatic complaints on separation

DSM-5 removed the "childhood onset" criterion — SAD can be diagnosed in adults. CANMAT: more common in women. Treat with CBT (exposure to separation) and SSRIs. Distinguish from agoraphobia (fear of panic situations, not specifically separation) and dependent personality disorder.

**Q:** What is the response to benzodiazepines in anxiety treatment and what are the risks?

**A:** Rapid symptom relief (30-60 min) via GABA-A receptor enhancement; risks: tolerance, physical dependence (withdrawal seizures if abrupt discontinuation), cognitive impairment, falls in elderly, sedation, rebound anxiety

CANMAT guidelines: benzodiazepines are not recommended as long-term anxiolytic monotherapy. Short-term use (2-4 weeks) acceptable for acute anxiety. Taper gradually when discontinuing (10% dose reduction per week). Diazepam or clonazepam preferred for tapering due to longer half-life.

**Q:** What defines PTSD per DSM-5?

**A:** Exposure to traumatic event + intrusion symptoms + avoidance + negative cognitions/mood + hyperarousal. Duration >1 month. Causes significant distress/impairment

<1 month = acute stress disorder. Treatment: trauma-focused CBT (first-line), EMDR. Pharmacotherapy: SSRIs (sertraline, paroxetine). Prazosin for nightmares.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for generalized anxiety disorder?

**A:** Excessive anxiety and worry occurring more days than not for >=6 months about multiple events/activities. Difficulty controlling worry. >=3 of 6 symptoms: restlessness, fatigue, difficulty concentrating, irritability, muscle tension, sleep disturbance. Causes significant distress or functional impairment.

GAD is the most common anxiety disorder in primary care. CANMAT guidelines recommend both CBT and pharmacotherapy as first-line treatments. Differentiate from adjustment disorder (identifiable stressor, <6 months) and normal worry (no impairment).

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacotherapy for anxiety disorders per CANMAT?

**A:** GAD: escitalopram, sertraline, venlafaxine, duloxetine, pregabalin. Panic disorder: escitalopram, sertraline, venlafaxine. Social anxiety disorder: escitalopram, sertraline, venlafaxine, fluvoxamine, paroxetine. OCD: fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, sertraline, paroxetine (higher doses needed). PTSD: sertraline, paroxetine, venlafaxine.

CANMAT recommends SSRIs/SNRIs as first-line for all anxiety disorders. Start at half the antidepressant dose (anxiety patients are sensitive to activation effects). Benzodiazepines are second-line and time-limited. OCD requires higher SSRI doses (e.g., fluoxetine 40-80mg) than depression.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for panic disorder and its management?

**A:** Recurrent unexpected panic attacks (abrupt surge of intense fear peaking within minutes with >=4 of 13 symptoms: palpitations, sweating, trembling, SOB, choking, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills/heat, paresthesias, derealization, fear of losing control, fear of dying). >=1 month of persistent concern about additional attacks OR maladaptive behavioral change.

CBT with interoceptive exposure is the most effective psychotherapy for panic disorder. CANMAT recommends SSRI/SNRI + CBT as optimal combination. Benzodiazepines for acute attacks but avoid long-term use. Rule out cardiac, pulmonary, and endocrine causes before diagnosing.

**Q:** What is the approach to obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD)?

**A:** DSM-5: obsessions (recurrent intrusive thoughts causing anxiety) AND/OR compulsions (repetitive behaviors/mental acts in response to obsessions). Time-consuming (>1h/day) or causing distress/impairment. Treatment: first-line is exposure and response prevention (ERP)-specific CBT for OCD. Pharmacotherapy: SSRI at high dose (e.g., fluoxetine 40-80mg, sertraline 200mg). Augment with low-dose antipsychotic if partial SSRI response.

OCD has a distinct neurobiology (cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical circuit) and treatment approach from other anxiety disorders. CANMAT emphasizes ERP as the most effective treatment. Treatment-resistant OCD may respond to IV clomipramine, deep brain stimulation (experimental), or neurosurgery (extremely rare).

**Q:** What is the diagnosis and management of PTSD?

**A:** DSM-5 criteria: exposure to traumatic event + symptoms in 4 clusters lasting >1 month: (1) intrusion (flashbacks, nightmares, distress with reminders), (2) avoidance (of memories, triggers), (3) negative cognitions/mood (guilt, detachment, anhedonia), (4) hyperarousal (hypervigilance, startle, irritability, sleep disturbance, reckless behavior). Treatment: trauma-focused CBT (cognitive processing therapy, prolonged exposure) first-line. Pharmacotherapy: SSRI (sertraline, paroxetine) or venlafaxine.

CANMAT PTSD guidelines recommend trauma-focused psychotherapy as first-line over pharmacotherapy. Prazosin (alpha-1 blocker) can be used for PTSD-related nightmares. Benzodiazepines are NOT recommended for PTSD (may impair extinction of fear memory). Screen for comorbid substance use and depression.

**Q:** What is social anxiety disorder and how is it differentiated from normal shyness?

**A:** DSM-5: marked fear/anxiety about social situations where scrutinized (speaking, eating, performing), fear of showing anxiety symptoms and being judged, social situations almost always provoke anxiety, avoided or endured with intense anxiety, out of proportion, >=6 months, functional impairment. Differentiate: shyness does not cause significant impairment or avoidance.

Social anxiety disorder (social phobia) is one of the most common anxiety disorders. CANMAT recommends CBT (including group CBT) and/or SSRI/SNRI as first-line. Performance-only subtype may respond to PRN propranolol or benzodiazepine. Comorbid alcohol use disorder is common (self-medication).

**Q:** What is the role and limitations of benzodiazepines in anxiety treatment?

**A:** Role: rapid symptom relief for acute anxiety, panic attacks, agitation. Short-term use (<2-4 weeks) during SSRI initiation. Limitations: tolerance, physical dependence (withdrawal: seizures, delirium), cognitive impairment (especially elderly), falls, paradoxical disinhibition, respiratory depression (especially with opioids), impair psychotherapy outcomes. Long-acting (clonazepam, diazepam) preferred over short-acting (alprazolam, lorazepam).

CANMAT recommends benzodiazepines as second-line and time-limited for anxiety disorders. Canadian guidelines emphasize deprescribing benzodiazepines in elderly (Beers criteria). Taper gradually (10-25% reduction every 1-2 weeks) to prevent withdrawal seizures. CTFPHC identifies benzodiazepines as a potentially inappropriate medication in elderly.

**Q:** What are specific phobias and their treatment?

**A:** DSM-5: marked fear/anxiety about a specific object or situation (animals, natural environment, blood-injection-injury, situational, other), almost always provokes immediate anxiety, avoided or endured with intense anxiety, out of proportion, >=6 months. Treatment: exposure therapy (systematic desensitization, graded exposure) is the ONLY effective treatment. Pharmacotherapy is NOT first-line.

Specific phobias are the most common anxiety disorder. Blood-injection-injury phobia is unique: causes vasovagal response (bradycardia, hypotension, fainting) rather than tachycardia. Applied tension technique (tensing muscles during exposure) is the specific treatment for BII phobia. CBT exposure therapy has >90% success rate.

**Q:** What is illness anxiety disorder (hypochondriasis) and somatic symptom disorder?

**A:** Illness anxiety disorder (DSM-5): preoccupation with having/acquiring a serious illness, somatic symptoms absent or mild, high anxiety about health, excessive health-related behaviors (checking, reassurance-seeking) or maladaptive avoidance. Somatic symptom disorder: >=1 distressing somatic symptom + disproportionate thoughts, feelings, or behaviors related to symptoms. Treatment: scheduled regular visits, CBT, SSRI, avoid unnecessary investigations.

These disorders are common in primary care. Key management principle: validate suffering without reinforcing sick role. Scheduled visits (not contingent on symptoms) reduce ED utilization. CANMAT recommends CBT as most effective treatment. Canadian guidelines emphasize avoiding iatrogenic harm from unnecessary investigations.

**Q:** What is the approach to acute agitation in the ED?

**A:** De-escalation first: quiet environment, verbal techniques, offer PRN oral medication. Oral: lorazepam 1-2mg OR olanzapine 5-10mg (dissolving tablet). If oral refused: IM olanzapine 10mg (do NOT combine with IM benzodiazepine-respiratory depression risk), OR IM haloperidol 5mg + lorazepam 2mg + diphenhydramine 50mg (B52 cocktail). Monitor vitals, consider medical causes (hypoglycemia, delirium, intoxication).

Canadian ER psychiatric guidelines emphasize verbal de-escalation and voluntary oral medication before chemical restraint. Physical restraint requires continuous monitoring and documentation. Rule out medical causes-20% of psychiatric presentations in ED have an underlying medical etiology.

**Q:** What is the approach to body dysmorphic disorder (BDD)?

**A:** DSM-5: preoccupation with perceived defects in physical appearance not observable by others, repetitive behaviors (mirror checking, excessive grooming, reassurance-seeking, comparing). Related to OCD spectrum. Treatment: CBT (ERP-type, targeting avoidance and checking behaviors) + SSRI at high doses (same as OCD). Do NOT refer for cosmetic procedures (high dissatisfaction rate).

BDD is often underdiagnosed due to patient shame. Prevalence ~2%. Canadian guidelines recognize BDD as an OCD-spectrum disorder. Insight ranges from good to delusional (absent insight treated with SSRI augmented by antipsychotic, not antipsychotic alone). Suicide risk is elevated (25% attempt).

**Q:** What is separation anxiety disorder in adults and children?

**A:** DSM-5: developmentally inappropriate, excessive anxiety about separation from attachment figures, >=3 of 8 symptoms (distress when separated, worry about losing attachment figure, reluctance to go out/be alone, nightmares about separation, somatic symptoms). Duration: >=4 weeks in children, >=6 months in adults. Peak onset: childhood, but can present in adults.

Separation anxiety disorder was previously only a childhood diagnosis-DSM-5 extended it to adults. Treatment: CBT focusing on graduated exposure to separation. In children, parental involvement in therapy is essential. SSRI for moderate-severe cases. Canadian child psychiatry guidelines emphasize school refusal as a common presentation.

**Q:** What are the features and management of adjustment disorder?

**A:** DSM-5: emotional/behavioral symptoms within 3 months of identifiable stressor, out of proportion to severity of stressor, significant impairment. Does NOT meet criteria for another mental disorder. Subtypes: with depressed mood, with anxiety, with mixed, with disturbance of conduct, unspecified. Resolves within 6 months of stressor ending. Treatment: supportive therapy, brief psychotherapy, short-term pharmacotherapy if needed.

Adjustment disorder is the most common psychiatric diagnosis in consultation-liaison psychiatry. Distinguished from normal stress reactions by functional impairment and from MDD/GAD by duration and precipitant. Self-limited by definition. If symptoms persist >6 months after stressor resolves, reconsider diagnosis.

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacotherapy for generalized anxiety disorder?

**A:** SSRIs (escitalopram, sertraline) or SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine). Start low, titrate over 4-6 weeks. Buspirone as alternative. Benzodiazepines for short-term only (max 2-4 weeks)

CANMAT guidelines. Full response takes 8-12 weeks. CBT equally effective and preferred as first-line (or combined). Pregabalin is second-line. Avoid benzodiazepines long-term (dependence, falls in elderly, cognitive impairment).

**Q:** What are the key differences between panic disorder, social anxiety, and GAD?

**A:** Panic: recurrent unexpected panic attacks with fear of future attacks. Social anxiety: intense fear of social/performance situations. GAD: excessive worry about multiple life areas for >=6 months, muscle tension, restlessness

Panic: spontaneous attacks with somatic symptoms (palpitations, dyspnea, chest pain). Agoraphobia often comorbid. Social anxiety: avoidance of public speaking, eating in public. All respond to SSRIs + CBT.

**Q:** What is borderline personality disorder and how is it managed?

**A:** DSM-5: >=5 of 9: abandonment fears, unstable relationships, identity disturbance, impulsivity, self-harm, affective instability, emptiness, anger, transient paranoia/dissociation. Treatment: DBT (dialectical behavior therapy)

Splitting: idealization/devaluation of healthcare providers. Management: consistent boundaries, DBT skills (mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation, interpersonal effectiveness). Pharmacotherapy targets symptoms not disorder.

**Q:** Describe the three clusters of personality disorders with their defining features and key examples.

**A:** Cluster A (odd/eccentric): Paranoid PD (pervasive distrust), Schizoid PD (detachment from social relationships, restricted affect), Schizotypal PD (cognitive/perceptual distortions, eccentric behavior, magical thinking). Cluster B (dramatic/emotional/erratic): Borderline, Narcissistic, Histrionic, Antisocial PD. Cluster C (anxious/fearful): Avoidant PD (social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy), Dependent PD (excessive need to be taken care of), Obsessive-Compulsive PD (preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism, control).

Mnemonic: Cluster A is 'mad' (psychotic-spectrum), Cluster B is 'bad' (impulsive/dramatic), Cluster C is 'sad' (anxious). Schizotypal PD has genetic links to schizophrenia. OCPD is distinct from OCD — OCPD is ego-syntonic (the person sees their rigidity as a virtue).

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for borderline personality disorder (BPD) and its core features?

**A:** 5 or more of 9 criteria: 1) Frantic efforts to avoid real/imagined abandonment; 2) Pattern of unstable, intense relationships (idealization/devaluation); 3) Identity disturbance (unstable self-image); 4) Impulsivity in 2+ areas (spending, sex, substances, reckless driving, binge eating); 5) Recurrent suicidal behavior/self-harm; 6) Affective instability; 7) Chronic feelings of emptiness; 8) Inappropriate intense anger; 9) Transient stress-related paranoid ideation or dissociation.

BPD affects ~1-2% of the population and is associated with high healthcare utilization. 'Splitting' (seeing people as all good or all bad) is the characteristic defense mechanism. CPA guidelines emphasize that BPD is a treatable condition with good prognosis — 50% no longer meet criteria after 10 years.

**Q:** What is dialectical behavior therapy (DBT) and what are its four modules?

**A:** DBT is the gold-standard evidence-based treatment for borderline personality disorder developed by Marsha Linehan. Four modules: 1) Mindfulness (core skill — present-moment awareness); 2) Distress tolerance (crisis survival skills without making things worse); 3) Emotion regulation (understanding and managing intense emotions); 4) Interpersonal effectiveness (assertiveness, maintaining relationships while respecting self). Standard DBT includes: individual therapy, skills group, phone coaching, and consultation team.

DBT has the strongest evidence for reducing self-harm and suicidal behavior in BPD. It balances acceptance (validation) with change (behavioral strategies). In Canada, DBT programs are available through many provincial mental health systems, though waitlists can be lengthy. Other evidence-based therapies for BPD include mentalization-based therapy (MBT) and transference-focused psychotherapy.

**Q:** How should 'splitting' in a patient with BPD be managed in a healthcare setting?

**A:** Splitting: defense mechanism where patient views staff as 'all good' or 'all bad,' causing team conflict. Management: 1) Consistent treatment team with clear communication; 2) Regular team meetings to ensure consistent messaging; 3) Clear treatment plan documented and shared with all staff; 4) Firm but empathic boundary-setting; 5) Validate the patient's emotions while maintaining limits; 6) Avoid 'special' treatment or punitive responses; 7) Single point of contact for decisions when possible.

Splitting can lead to staff burnout and inconsistent care if not recognized. The key principle is a unified, compassionate, and consistent approach. Canadian hospital policies increasingly include BPD care pathways that emphasize team consistency and trauma-informed care rather than labeling patients as 'difficult.'

**Q:** What defines antisocial personality disorder (ASPD) and what is its relationship to conduct disorder?

**A:** ASPD DSM-5: Age ≥18; pervasive pattern of disregard for/violation of rights of others since age 15 with 3+: failure to conform to laws, deceitfulness, impulsivity, irritability/aggressiveness, reckless disregard for safety of self/others, consistent irresponsibility, lack of remorse. REQUIRES evidence of conduct disorder before age 15. Conduct disorder: repetitive pattern of behavior violating others' rights or age-appropriate norms (aggression, destruction, deceitfulness, rule violations).

Not all children with conduct disorder develop ASPD (~40% do). ASPD has strong comorbidity with substance use disorders. There are no effective pharmacotherapies for ASPD itself — treatment targets comorbidities. Forensic psychiatry in Canada frequently deals with ASPD in criminal justice assessments (NCR and fitness evaluations under the Criminal Code).

**Q:** Compare avoidant personality disorder with social anxiety disorder. How do they differ?

**A:** Avoidant PD: pervasive pattern of social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy, hypersensitivity to negative evaluation; avoids occupational activities requiring interpersonal contact; reluctant to engage unless certain of being liked; views self as socially inept and inferior. Social anxiety disorder (SAD): fear/anxiety of social situations where scrutiny is possible — but the person's core identity and self-concept are intact. Key difference: Avoidant PD is more pervasive, affects all domains of functioning, has deeper identity-level feelings of inferiority, while SAD is more circumscribed to specific social performance situations.

These conditions frequently co-occur (~50% overlap). Avoidant PD responds to similar treatments as SAD (CBT, SSRIs), but psychotherapy is typically longer and more focused on core schema change. CPA guidelines note the distinction is clinically relevant for treatment planning and prognosis.

**Q:** What are the key features of narcissistic personality disorder?

**A:** DSM-5 (5+ of 9): Grandiose sense of self-importance; preoccupied with fantasies of unlimited success/power/beauty; believes they are 'special' and should associate with high-status people; requires excessive admiration; sense of entitlement; interpersonally exploitative; lacks empathy; often envious or believes others are envious of them; arrogant/haughty behaviors. Underlying: fragile self-esteem vulnerable to 'narcissistic injury' (perceived slight) → rage or depression.

Narcissistic PD is more common in males. 'Narcissistic injury' (criticism or failure) can precipitate depression, substance abuse, or suicidal behavior. Treatment is challenging as patients rarely present voluntarily — typically present during crisis or at partner's insistence. Schema therapy and transference-focused psychotherapy have some evidence.

**Q:** What distinguishes schizotypal personality disorder from schizoid personality disorder and schizophrenia?

**A:** Schizoid PD: social detachment, restricted affect, DOES NOT have perceptual distortions or magical thinking — simply prefers solitude, appears cold/indifferent. Schizotypal PD: odd beliefs/magical thinking (telepathy, sixth sense), unusual perceptual experiences (illusions, bodily sensations), odd speech (vague, metaphorical), suspiciousness, eccentric behavior — has attenuated psychotic features but maintains reality testing. Schizophrenia: frank psychosis (delusions, hallucinations, disorganized thought) with functional decline.

Schizotypal PD is genetically related to schizophrenia (higher prevalence in first-degree relatives of schizophrenia patients) and is considered part of the schizophrenia spectrum. ~10% of schizotypal PD patients eventually develop schizophrenia. Low-dose antipsychotics may help with perceptual symptoms in schizotypal PD.

**Q:** How does obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD) differ from obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD)?

**A:** OCPD: ego-SYNTONIC (patient sees their rigidity, perfectionism, and need for control as virtues); pervasive pattern of preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism, and mental/interpersonal control at the expense of flexibility and efficiency; hoarding, excessive devotion to work, miserliness, rigidity. OCD: ego-DYSTONIC (patient recognizes obsessions as intrusive and unwanted); obsessions (recurrent intrusive thoughts) + compulsions (ritualistic behaviors to reduce anxiety); causes significant distress. OCPD is a personality pattern; OCD is an anxiety-spectrum disorder.

OCPD is the most common personality disorder (~3-8% prevalence). Only ~25% of patients with OCPD also have OCD. OCPD patients often present at the insistence of frustrated family/colleagues. Treatment: psychotherapy (CBT focusing on cognitive flexibility). OCPD is associated with better work functioning but worse interpersonal relationships.

**Q:** A patient with BPD presents to the ED after superficial self-harm. She is angry at staff and threatening to leave. What is the approach to crisis management?

**A:** 1) Ensure safety (remove means of further self-harm); 2) Use calm, validating, non-judgmental approach ('I can see you're in a lot of pain right now'); 3) Assess suicide risk thoroughly (self-harm ≠ suicide attempt, but BPD patients DO die by suicide — 8-10% lifetime); 4) Treat medical injuries; 5) Explore precipitant (often interpersonal crisis/perceived abandonment); 6) Develop safety plan collaboratively; 7) Brief intervention — do NOT admit unless genuinely suicidal (prolonged admissions can worsen BPD through regression); 8) Arrange outpatient follow-up within 24-72h; 9) Document clearly and communicate with outpatient team.

Short, structured ED visits with validation and safety planning are preferred over admission for chronic self-harm in BPD. However, always take suicidal ideation seriously — BPD has significant completed suicide rates. Canadian ED mental health guidelines emphasize trauma-informed care and avoiding pejorative language ('attention-seeking').

**Q:** What are the differences between histrionic and borderline personality disorders?

**A:** Histrionic PD: excessive emotionality and attention-seeking; uncomfortable when not center of attention; inappropriate sexually seductive behavior; rapidly shifting shallow emotions; uses physical appearance to draw attention; impressionistic/vague speech; theatrical/exaggerated emotions; suggestible. Borderline PD: unstable relationships with idealization/devaluation (splitting); identity disturbance; self-harm and suicidal behavior; chronic emptiness; intense inappropriate anger; abandonment fears; transient paranoia/dissociation. Key distinctions: Histrionic — seeks attention and admiration, emotions are shallow and shifting but not as intense; BPD — fears abandonment, emotions are deeply felt, self-destructive behavior is prominent, identity is fundamentally unstable.

Both are Cluster B personality disorders and can co-occur. Histrionic PD is less associated with self-harm and suicidal behavior compared to BPD. Histrionic PD has less evidence-based treatment available compared to BPD (which has DBT, MBT). In clinical practice, the distinction can be difficult and often requires longitudinal assessment.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for OCD and what is the first-line treatment?

**A:** Obsessions (recurrent intrusive thoughts causing anxiety) and/or compulsions (repetitive behaviours/mental acts to reduce anxiety). Time-consuming (>=1 hour/day) or causing significant distress/impairment. First-line: CBT with exposure and response prevention (ERP), and/or SSRI (fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, sertraline, paroxetine) at higher doses than for depression.

CANMAT recommends ERP as first-line for mild-moderate OCD and combined ERP + SSRI for moderate-severe. OCD SSRIs require higher doses (e.g., fluoxetine 60-80 mg) and longer trial duration (8-12 weeks) compared to depression treatment. Clomipramine is effective but used as second-line due to side effects. Treatment-resistant OCD may benefit from augmentation with low-dose antipsychotic.

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacotherapy for social anxiety disorder?

**A:** SSRIs (escitalopram, paroxetine, sertraline) or SNRIs (venlafaxine XR) are first-line. Start low, titrate slowly. Continue 12+ months after response. CBT (cognitive restructuring + exposure) is equally effective and preferred by some patients. Combination CBT + medication for severe cases.

CANMAT guidelines rate SSRIs and venlafaxine as first-line with Level 1 evidence. Beta-blockers (propranolol) are useful for performance-only subtype but not generalized SAD. Benzodiazepines are NOT recommended due to dependence risk. MAOIs (phenelzine) are effective but reserved as third-line due to dietary restrictions.

**Q:** What is the difference between specific phobia treatment and other anxiety disorders?

**A:** Specific phobia: exposure-based therapy (systematic desensitization or flooding) is the ONLY evidence-based first-line treatment. Medications are NOT effective for specific phobia (unlike other anxiety disorders). Single-session exposure therapy can be curative. No role for SSRIs or benzodiazepines as maintenance.

Specific phobias are the most common anxiety disorder but rarely present for treatment unless functionally limiting (e.g., needle phobia affecting medical care, flight phobia). CANMAT confirms that pharmacotherapy has no sustained benefit. Short-term benzodiazepines may be used situationally (e.g., pre-flight) but do not address the phobia itself.

**Q:** How is separation anxiety disorder diagnosed in children and what is the treatment?

**A:** Developmentally inappropriate, excessive anxiety about separation from attachment figures lasting >=4 weeks in children. Symptoms: refusal to go to school/sleep alone, nightmares about separation, somatic complaints (stomachache, headache) when separation anticipated. Treatment: CBT (parent-involved), gradual exposure, parent training. SSRIs for moderate-severe or CBT-refractory cases.

Separation anxiety is normal until age 3-4; pathological when persistent, excessive, and impairing. CPS and CANMAT recommend CBT as first-line. School refusal is a common presentation requiring collaborative approach with school. SSRIs (fluoxetine, sertraline) are evidence-based for pediatric anxiety disorders including separation anxiety.

**Q:** What differentiates OCD from obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD)?

**A:** OCD: ego-dystonic (patient recognizes thoughts as irrational and distressing), specific obsessions/compulsions, causes significant distress. OCPD: ego-syntonic (patient views rigidity as appropriate), pervasive pattern of perfectionism, orderliness, and control, no specific obsessions/compulsions, patient does not typically seek treatment for these traits.

OCD and OCPD are distinct disorders despite similar names. They can co-occur (~25% overlap). OCD responds to SSRIs and ERP; OCPD is treated with psychotherapy (not typically pharmacotherapy). DSM-5 classifies OCD separately from anxiety disorders in its own chapter. OCPD is a Cluster C personality disorder.

**Q:** What are the CANMAT-recommended first-line treatments for generalized anxiety disorder?

**A:** Pharmacotherapy: SSRIs (escitalopram, paroxetine, sertraline) or SNRIs (venlafaxine XR, duloxetine). Pregabalin is first-line in CANMAT (unlike US guidelines). Psychotherapy: CBT. Combined approach for severe or refractory cases. Continue pharmacotherapy for 12+ months after response.

CANMAT uniquely includes pregabalin as first-line for GAD based on European evidence. Buspirone is second-line (slower onset, modest efficacy). Benzodiazepines are third-line only for short-term use due to dependence risk, cognitive impairment in elderly, and falls. CANMAT emphasizes tapering benzodiazepines that were previously prescribed.

**Q:** What is the evidence for ERP (Exposure and Response Prevention) in OCD treatment?

**A:** ERP is the most effective psychotherapy for OCD: 50-70% of patients achieve clinically significant improvement. Involves systematic, prolonged exposure to anxiety-provoking stimuli while preventing compulsive responses. Typically 12-16 sessions. Effect sizes are larger than SSRIs alone. Y-BOCS is used to measure severity and response.

CANMAT recommends ERP as first-line for all OCD severity levels. Response defined as >=35% reduction in Y-BOCS score. Barriers include patient avoidance, limited therapist availability, and high dropout rates. Intensive ERP (daily sessions over 3 weeks) shows comparable results to standard delivery. Combination with SRI is superior to either alone for severe OCD.

**Q:** How is panic disorder managed and what is the role of benzodiazepines?

**A:** First-line: SSRIs (paroxetine, sertraline, fluoxetine), SNRIs (venlafaxine), and/or CBT (interoceptive exposure + cognitive restructuring). Benzodiazepines are NOT first-line: reserved for short-term bridging (2-4 weeks) while waiting for SSRI onset. Long-term benzodiazepine use leads to dependence and is associated with worse outcomes.

CANMAT recommends CBT as first-line either alone or combined with pharmacotherapy. Panic-focused CBT includes education about fight-or-flight, interoceptive exposure (inducing physical symptoms to reduce fear), and in-vivo exposure to avoided situations. Agoraphobia often co-occurs and requires specific exposure targets.

**Q:** A child presents with excessive worry about multiple domains (school, health, family) lasting >6 months with restlessness and sleep difficulty. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Generalized anxiety disorder in children. Management: first-line is CBT (child-focused, with parent involvement). If moderate-severe or CBT-refractory: SSRI (fluoxetine or sertraline — best evidence in pediatric anxiety). Monitor for suicidality during SSRI initiation (Health Canada black box warning for <25 years).

Anxiety disorders are the most common psychiatric conditions in children (10-20% prevalence). The CAMS trial showed CBT + sertraline was superior to either alone (81% response). Canadian guidelines recommend CBT first for mild cases. School-based interventions and parent training augment treatment. Screen for comorbid depression and ADHD.

**Q:** What are hoarding disorder's key features and how is it treated?

**A:** Persistent difficulty discarding possessions regardless of value, perceived need to save items, distress associated with discarding, accumulation clutters living spaces compromising intended use. Treatment: CBT specifically adapted for hoarding (sorting, decision-making, exposure to discarding). SSRIs have limited efficacy. Harm reduction approach when treatment-resistant.

Hoarding disorder was separated from OCD in DSM-5 as a distinct diagnosis. It has poor response to standard OCD treatments (SSRIs, ERP). Specialized CBT for hoarding includes in-home sessions and motivational interviewing. Prevalence is 2-6%. Fire safety and public health concerns often trigger referral. Canadian municipal bylaws may mandate cleanup.

## Substance Use Disorders

**Q:** A 45-year-old man is brought to the ED in acute alcohol withdrawal 24 hours after his last drink. He has tremors, diaphoresis, tachycardia, and is anxious. CIWA-Ar score is 18. What is the management?

**A:** Alcohol withdrawal: symptom-triggered benzodiazepine dosing using CIWA-Ar scale (lorazepam or diazepam); IV thiamine 100mg BEFORE glucose; monitor for delirium tremens and seizures

CCSMH (Canadian Coalition for Seniors Mental Health) and CAMH guidelines: IV/IM thiamine must be given BEFORE glucose to prevent Wernicke encephalopathy. Symptom-triggered benzodiazepine reduces medication use vs. fixed-dose schedule. Delirium tremens (Day 3-5): severe withdrawal with hyperthermia, hallucinations, autonomic instability.

**Q:** What medications are approved for alcohol use disorder (AUD) treatment in Canada?

**A:** Naltrexone (reduces craving, blocks opioid reward), acamprosate (reduces GABA/glutamate imbalance), disulfiram (aversive — acetaldehyde accumulation); extended-release naltrexone (Vivitrol) IM monthly

CANMAT/CAMH AUD guidelines: all three medications approved in Canada. Naltrexone preferred for heavy drinkers who want to reduce or abstain. Acamprosate for maintaining abstinence. Disulfiram requires high motivation (patient must consent to aversive reaction). Combine with behavioral therapy.

**Q:** What is opioid use disorder and what is the preferred treatment in Canada?

**A:** OUD: pattern of opioid use causing distress/impairment + ≥2 DSM-5 criteria; first-line treatment: buprenorphine-naloxone (Suboxone) or methadone — OAT (Opioid Agonist Therapy) reduces mortality 60-70%

CAMH and CCS (College of Family Physicians of Canada) guidelines: OAT is the gold standard for OUD. Buprenorphine-naloxone is preferred (lower overdose risk, office-based prescribing). Methadone requires monitored daily dosing at licensed pharmacy. Extended-release naltrexone (Vivitrol) as alternative.

**Q:** A patient is found unresponsive with pinpoint pupils, bradypnea (4 breaths/min), and is cyanotic. Suspected opioid overdose. What is the immediate management?

**A:** Naloxone (Narcan) 0.4-2 mg IV/IM/intranasal; support airway (BVM ventilation); repeat naloxone q2-3 min up to 10 mg if no response; titrate to adequate breathing (not full reversal to avoid withdrawal and re-narcotization)

CAMH naloxone guidelines: naloxone half-life (30-90 min) is shorter than most opioids — monitor for re-narcotization. Fentanyl overdose may require multiple higher doses (4-6 mg total). Take-home naloxone kits should be offered to all opioid users. Naloxone is available OTC in all Canadian provinces.

**Q:** What is the CAGE questionnaire and the AUDIT-C for alcohol screening?

**A:** CAGE: Cut down, Annoyed, Guilty, Eye-opener — ≥2 positive = probable AUD (sensitivity 71%, specificity 90%); AUDIT-C: first 3 questions of AUDIT — score ≥4 (men) or ≥3 (women) suggests harmful drinking

CAMH guidelines recommend AUDIT (Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test) as standard screening tool in primary care. AUDIT-C is a brief validated screen. CAGE is simpler but less sensitive for detecting hazardous drinking (only detects dependence). Use in combination with clinical interview.

**Q:** What is the delirium tremens (DT) and what are the risk factors and management?

**A:** DT: severe alcohol withdrawal (Day 3-5): altered consciousness, vivid hallucinations (tactile, visual), autonomic instability (hyperthermia, tachycardia, hypertension), seizures — 5-15% mortality if untreated

Risk factors for DT: prior DT, high CIWA-Ar, long duration of heavy drinking, BAL >300 mg/dL, acute medical illness, older age. Management: ICU admission, high-dose IV benzodiazepines (lorazepam or diazepam), phenobarbital for refractory cases, supportive care, treat precipitants.

**Q:** What is cannabis use disorder and what are the health risks of chronic cannabis use?

**A:** Cannabis use disorder: ≥2 DSM-5 criteria from 11; health risks: CUD, cannabis hyperemesis syndrome, increased psychosis risk (2x in heavy users, especially with high-THC products), cognitive impairment (especially with adolescent onset), bronchitis

CANMAT and CAMH: no approved pharmacotherapy for cannabis use disorder. CBT and motivational enhancement therapy are evidence-based. Cannabis hyperemesis syndrome: recurrent vomiting, paradoxically relieved by hot showers — treat with haloperidol, capsaicin cream, and cessation of cannabis.

**Q:** What is the management of stimulant (cocaine, methamphetamine) intoxication?

**A:** Cocaine intoxication: benzodiazepines for agitation/seizures, supportive care; avoid beta-blockers (unopposed alpha stimulation); CCBs or nitrates for coronary vasospasm; methamphetamine: similar management + treat hyperthermia aggressively

CAMH guidelines: cocaine overdose has no antidote. Cardiac complications (MI, VF) require standard ACS management — avoid beta-blockers. Hyperthermia from methamphetamine is life-threatening — active cooling with misting fans + benzodiazepines. Haloperidol for psychosis.

**Q:** What screening tools are available for substance use in primary care?

**A:** AUDIT (alcohol), ASSIST (Alcohol, Smoking and Substance Involvement Screening Test — multiple substances), DAST-10 (Drug Abuse Screening Test), CAGE-AID (adapted for drugs)

CANMAT guidelines recommend brief intervention after positive substance screening. 5-minute brief motivational interview at primary care level can reduce alcohol consumption. SBIRT (Screening, Brief Intervention, and Referral to Treatment) model is evidence-based.

**Q:** What are the principles of harm reduction in substance use?

**A:** Harm reduction: reducing health, social, and economic harms without requiring abstinence; includes needle exchange programs, naloxone distribution, supervised consumption sites, drug checking services, OAT without abstinence requirements

CAMH/PHAC guidelines support harm reduction as an evidence-based approach to SUD. Supervised consumption sites (SCS) in Canada reduce overdose deaths, infections (HIV, hepatitis C), and emergency department visits without increasing community drug use. OAT is the highest-impact harm reduction intervention.

**Q:** What is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome and how is it prevented?

**A:** Wernicke: acute thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency — confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia; Korsakoff: chronic amnestic syndrome from thiamine deficiency — anterograde amnesia, confabulation; prevent with IV thiamine before glucose in all alcohol-related presentations

Wernicke encephalopathy is reversible with IV thiamine — treatment must be given BEFORE glucose (glucose depletes thiamine). Korsakoff syndrome: 80% permanent amnesia. IV thiamine dose: 200-500 mg TID for 3-5 days (high doses needed for bioavailability). Give to all alcohol-related ED presentations.

**Q:** What is tobacco use disorder and what are the most effective cessation treatments?

**A:** Most effective: varenicline (Champix) — nicotinic receptor partial agonist (NRT 2.3x placebo, most effective individual treatment); combination NRT (patch + short-acting); bupropion; counseling enhances all pharmacotherapies

CANMAT/CAMH tobacco guidelines: varenicline doubles quit rates compared to NRT alone. Start 1 week before quit date. Side effects: nausea, vivid dreams, rare psychiatric effects (screen carefully). Combination NRT (patch + gum/lozenge) is more effective than patch alone.

**Q:** What is the treatment for benzodiazepine dependence?

**A:** Gradual taper (10% dose reduction per week or per month for long-term users) using equivalent long-acting benzodiazepine (diazepam); CBT and psychoeducation; do NOT abruptly stop (seizure risk); avoid switching to another benzodiazepine

CANMAT guidelines: benzodiazepine withdrawal can be life-threatening (seizures). Convert to equivalent diazepam dose and taper over months. Carbamazepine or valproate may help. Gabapentin as adjunct for protracted withdrawal symptoms. Prevent new long-term benzodiazepine prescribing.

**Q:** A 35-year-old heroin user is starting buprenorphine-naloxone (Suboxone). When should the first dose be given?

**A:** Give first dose when COWS score ≥8 (Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale — moderate withdrawal) or 12-24 hours after last short-acting opioid, 24-72 hours after last long-acting opioid

CAMH/CPSO guidelines: buprenorphine must be given when patient is in moderate withdrawal to prevent precipitated withdrawal (buprenorphine displaces full agonists from receptors, precipitating acute withdrawal if given too early). Low-dose induction (microdosing/Bernese protocol) for patients on high-dose fentanyl.

**Q:** What are the CANMAT low-risk alcohol use guidelines (LRDG)?

**A:** Low-risk drinking: ≤2 standard drinks/day (≤10/week for women, ≤15/week for men), with ≤3 per occasion — updated 2023 Canada guidance: >2 drinks/week = moderate risk, >6/week = high risk

CAMH/CCSA 2023 Canadian alcohol guidance: new evidence shows even low levels of alcohol increase cancer risk (breast, colorectal, liver). No safe level for cancer. Revised thresholds: 0-2 drinks/week = low risk; 3-6/week = moderate risk; 7+/week = high risk.

**Q:** What is CAGE screening for alcohol use disorder?

**A:** Cut down, Annoyed by criticism, Guilty, Eye-opener. >=2 positive = likely alcohol use disorder

AUDIT more sensitive (10 questions). DSM-5 AUD: >=2 of 11 criteria in 12 months. Mild(2-3), moderate(4-5), severe(>=6). Withdrawal: benzodiazepines (CIWA protocol).

**Q:** What are the CAGE and AUDIT screening tools for alcohol use disorder?

**A:** CAGE: Cut down (tried to?), Annoyed (by criticism?), Guilty (about drinking?), Eye-opener (morning drink?). >=2 positive = high suspicion for AUD. AUDIT (Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test): 10 items scoring 0-40. 8-15: hazardous drinking. 16-19: harmful drinking. >=20: possible dependence. AUDIT-C (first 3 items): screening version, score >=4 men / >=3 women is positive.

CAGE is quick but less sensitive for early/hazardous drinking. AUDIT is the gold standard screening tool recommended by CCSA (Canadian Centre on Substance Abuse). AUDIT is more sensitive and specific than CAGE. Canadian primary care guidelines recommend universal alcohol screening.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for substance use disorder?

**A:** >=2 of 11 criteria in 12 months: (1) larger amounts/longer than intended, (2) persistent desire/unsuccessful efforts to cut down, (3) excessive time obtaining/using/recovering, (4) craving, (5) failure to fulfill role obligations, (6) continued use despite social problems, (7) given up activities, (8) use in hazardous situations, (9) continued despite physical/psychological problems, (10) tolerance, (11) withdrawal. Severity: mild (2-3), moderate (4-5), severe (>=6).

DSM-5 eliminated the abuse/dependence distinction, creating a single spectrum. Craving was added as a new criterion. The dimensional severity approach guides treatment intensity. Canadian clinical practice guidelines use DSM-5 criteria for all substance use disorders.

**Q:** What is the management of alcohol withdrawal syndrome?

**A:** CIWA-Ar (Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment) guides treatment. Mild (CIWA <10): supportive, thiamine. Moderate (10-18): benzodiazepine (diazepam 10-20mg or lorazepam 2-4mg) on symptom-triggered protocol. Severe (>20) or seizures: aggressive benzodiazepine loading, ICU consideration. Prevention of Wernicke: thiamine 200-500mg IV BEFORE glucose. Phenobarbital for refractory cases. Nutritional support: thiamine, folate, magnesium.

Symptom-triggered protocols (benzodiazepines given based on CIWA score) are superior to fixed-schedule dosing (less medication, shorter treatment). Lorazepam is preferred in liver disease (no active metabolites). Alcohol withdrawal seizures peak at 24-48h. DT peak at 48-72h with 5-15% mortality if untreated.

**Q:** What are the pharmacological treatments for alcohol use disorder?

**A:** First-line: naltrexone 50mg/day PO or 380mg IM monthly (opioid antagonist, reduces craving/heavy drinking-contraindicated if opioid use). Acamprosate 666mg TID (GABA modulator, supports abstinence-safe in liver disease). Second-line: topiramate, gabapentin. Disulfiram 250mg/day (aldehyde dehydrogenase inhibitor-aversive therapy, requires motivation). Adjunct: thiamine supplementation.

CCSA and CRISM (Canadian Research Initiative on Substance Misuse) guidelines recommend naltrexone or acamprosate as first-line pharmacotherapy combined with psychosocial interventions. Naltrexone is particularly effective for reducing heavy drinking days. Canadian availability of injectable naltrexone (Vivitrol) is limited.

**Q:** What is the approach to opioid use disorder treatment in Canada?

**A:** First-line: opioid agonist therapy (OAT). Buprenorphine-naloxone (Suboxone): first-line per CRISM, can be initiated in office (micro-dosing or traditional induction), partial agonist, safer in overdose. Methadone: full agonist, requires daily witnessed ingestion initially, designated prescriber. Slow-release oral morphine (Kadian): second-line. Adjuncts: naltrexone (after detox only), psychosocial interventions, harm reduction.

Canadian Opioid Use Disorder Guidelines (CRISM) recommend buprenorphine-naloxone as first-line. OAT reduces mortality by 50%, reduces illicit opioid use, HIV/HCV transmission, and criminal activity. Naloxone kits are widely distributed in Canada for overdose reversal. Safe consumption sites are operating in several Canadian cities.

**Q:** What is the management of acute opioid overdose?

**A:** Signs: respiratory depression (RR <12), miosis, decreased LOC. ABCs first. Naloxone: 0.4-2mg IV/IM/SC/IN, repeat q2-3min (max 10mg). Onset: 2 min IV, 5 min IM. Duration: 30-90 min (shorter than most opioids-monitor for renarcotization). If IV access unavailable: intranasal naloxone 4mg. Observe >=2h (longer for long-acting opioids). Consider intubation if no response to naloxone.

Canadian opioid crisis: >7000 deaths annually. Take-home naloxone programs are available across all provinces. Naloxone can precipitate withdrawal in opioid-dependent patients-titrate to adequate respirations, not full consciousness. Fentanyl overdoses may require higher naloxone doses.

**Q:** What is the approach to cannabis use disorder?

**A:** Prevalence: ~7% of Canadian cannabis users meet criteria for CUD. Assessment: frequency, amount, method, age of onset, functional impairment. No approved pharmacotherapy for CUD. Treatment: CBT, motivational enhancement therapy (MET), contingency management. Cannabis hyperemesis syndrome: cyclic vomiting, hot bathing behavior, capsaicin cream. Psychosis risk: dose-dependent, especially with high-THC products and early-onset use.

Canada legalized recreational cannabis in 2018. CCSA Lower-Risk Cannabis Use Guidelines recommend: delay use until after 25, avoid high-THC products, avoid smoking, avoid frequent use. Cannabis use in adolescence increases psychosis risk 2-4x (particularly with daily use of high-potency products).

**Q:** What is the approach to benzodiazepine use disorder and deprescribing?

**A:** Gradual taper is essential (abrupt cessation causes seizures, delirium, death). Taper: switch to long-acting agent (diazepam) if on short-acting, reduce 10-25% every 1-2 weeks. Slower at lower doses. Total taper: 8-16 weeks minimum. Adjuncts: CBT for insomnia, pregabalin for anxiety during taper. Monitor for protracted withdrawal (anxiety, insomnia lasting months).

CCSA and Choosing Wisely Canada recommend deprescribing benzodiazepines in chronic users, especially elderly. Canadian Deprescribing Network provides guidelines. Benzodiazepine withdrawal syndrome: anxiety, insomnia, irritability, tremor, sensory hypersensitivity, psychosis, seizures. Risk of seizure increases with high dose and long duration.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and management of stimulant intoxication and withdrawal?

**A:** Intoxication (cocaine/amphetamine): euphoria, hyperactivity, tachycardia, hypertension, hyperthermia, dilated pupils, agitation, psychosis, seizures, chest pain (cocaine-coronary vasospasm). Management: benzodiazepines (NOT beta-blockers for cocaine-unopposed alpha stimulation), cooling, cardiac monitoring. Withdrawal: crash (fatigue, hypersomnia, dysphoria, increased appetite). No specific pharmacotherapy for stimulant use disorder.

Cocaine chest pain: treat as ACS but avoid beta-blockers (may worsen coronary vasospasm). Use benzodiazepines and nitroglycerin. Canadian stimulant use is rising (especially methamphetamine in Western Canada). Contingency management has best evidence for stimulant use disorder but limited availability in Canada.

**Q:** What is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome and how is it prevented?

**A:** Wernicke encephalopathy (acute): triad of confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia (nystagmus, 6th nerve palsy). Only 10-16% have classic triad. Caused by thiamine (B1) deficiency. Treatment: thiamine 200-500mg IV TID for 3-5 days (ALWAYS give BEFORE glucose). Korsakoff syndrome (chronic): anterograde and retrograde amnesia, confabulation. Often irreversible. Prevention: thiamine supplementation for ALL alcohol-dependent patients.

Wernicke is a medical emergency with 20% mortality if untreated. Oral thiamine is poorly absorbed in alcoholics-IV is essential. Give thiamine before glucose in all malnourished/alcohol patients to prevent precipitating Wernicke. Korsakoff develops in 80% of untreated Wernicke. Canadian guidelines recommend routine thiamine supplementation for all admitted alcohol-dependent patients.

**Q:** What is the harm reduction approach to substance use in Canada?

**A:** Key principles: pragmatic acceptance that substance use exists, focus on reducing harm without requiring abstinence. Canadian programs: needle exchange programs, supervised consumption sites (supervised injection sites), naloxone distribution, drug checking services, safer supply programs, housing-first approaches.

Harm reduction is a pillar of Canadian drug policy per CCSA and Canadian federal health policy. InSite (Vancouver) was the first supervised consumption site in North America. Supreme Court of Canada upheld its legality in 2011. Safe supply prescribing (prescribed opioids to replace illicit supply) is expanding in Canada.

**Q:** What is the management of nicotine dependence?

**A:** Fagerström Test for Nicotine Dependence assesses severity. First-line pharmacotherapy: varenicline (most effective, partial nicotinic agonist), NRT (patch + gum/lozenge combination), bupropion. Combination NRT = long-acting (patch) + short-acting (gum/inhaler/lozenge). Counseling + pharmacotherapy is most effective. Varenicline: start 1 week before quit date, 0.5mg x 3 days, 0.5mg BID x 4 days, then 1mg BID x 12 weeks. Extend to 24 weeks for relapse prevention.

CAN-ADAPTT (Canadian Action Network for Advancement and Prevention of Treatment of Tobacco) provides evidence-based guidelines. Smoking is the leading cause of preventable death in Canada. All healthcare encounters should include smoking status assessment and brief intervention (5 As: Ask, Advise, Assess, Assist, Arrange).

**Q:** What is motivational interviewing and its application in substance use?

**A:** MI: collaborative, person-centered communication style for strengthening motivation to change. Principles: express empathy (reflective listening), develop discrepancy (between current behavior and goals), roll with resistance (avoid arguing), support self-efficacy. Stages of Change: precontemplation, contemplation, preparation, action, maintenance, relapse.

MI is the most widely taught brief intervention technique in Canadian medical education. Evidence supports MI across substance use disorders, medication adherence, and health behavior change. A single MI session can reduce harmful alcohol use. FRAMES approach: Feedback, Responsibility, Advice, Menu, Empathy, Self-efficacy.

**Q:** What is the approach to concurrent disorders (substance use + mental illness)?

**A:** Integrated treatment: address both simultaneously (sequential treatment leads to poor outcomes). Common combinations: AUD + depression, cannabis + psychosis, opioids + PTSD. Principles: stabilize acute intoxication/withdrawal first, then address both concurrently. Prefer medications effective for both conditions (e.g., SSRI for depression + AUD). Avoid benzodiazepines in patients with SUD.

50% of people with mental illness have a comorbid substance use disorder. Canadian guidelines (CCSA, CRISM) recommend integrated concurrent disorder programs. Many Canadian mental health acts allow involuntary admission for psychiatric emergency even when substance-related. Substance-induced mood/psychotic disorders typically resolve within 1 month of abstinence.

**Q:** What are the medical complications of chronic alcohol use?

**A:** GI: fatty liver, alcoholic hepatitis, cirrhosis, pancreatitis, esophageal varices, Mallory-Weiss tears. Neuro: Wernicke-Korsakoff, peripheral neuropathy, cerebellar degeneration, alcohol-related dementia. Hematologic: macrocytic anemia (folate deficiency, direct toxicity), thrombocytopenia. Cardiac: alcoholic cardiomyopathy, holiday heart (AF). Other: increased cancer risk (oropharyngeal, esophageal, liver, breast, colorectal).

CCSA Low-Risk Alcohol Drinking Guidelines (updated 2023): no amount of alcohol is completely safe. New guidance: 0 drinks/week = no risk, 1-2 = low risk, 3-6 = moderate risk, 7+ = increasingly high risk. This represents a significant shift from previous Canadian guidelines that suggested "moderate" drinking was safe.

**Q:** What are the methadone vs buprenorphine/naloxone protocols for opioid use disorder?

**A:** Methadone: full agonist, daily supervised dispensing initially, slow titration (fatal in overdose), long QT risk. Buprenorphine: partial agonist, can prescribe for take-home, safer in OD, must be in mild withdrawal to start (COWS >=12)

CRISM guidelines. Buprenorphine preferred first-line (safety profile, less supervised dosing). Methadone for buprenorphine failures or high-tolerance patients. Injectable opioid agonist therapy (iOAT) for severe refractory OUD.

**Q:** What are the CCSA-recommended pharmacotherapy options for opioid use disorder?

**A:** First-line: buprenorphine/naloxone (Suboxone) — can be prescribed in community settings, safer profile. Second-line: methadone — requires specialized prescriber authorization, daily supervised dosing initially. Third-line: slow-release oral morphine (Kadian). Injectable hydromorphone or diacetylmorphine for treatment-refractory patients. Naltrexone (oral or injectable) for highly motivated patients.

CRISM Canadian guidelines recommend opioid agonist therapy (OAT) as first-line. Buprenorphine is preferred over methadone due to lower overdose risk, fewer drug interactions, and ability to prescribe in primary care (micro-dosing initiation gaining acceptance). Treatment retention reduces mortality by 50%. All patients on OAT should receive naloxone kits.

**Q:** What is the CIWA-Ar scale and how does it guide alcohol withdrawal management?

**A:** Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol (revised): 10-item scale scoring 0-67. Assesses nausea, tremor, anxiety, agitation, paroxysmal sweats, tactile/auditory/visual disturbances, headache, orientation.
