Score <10: mild (supportive care). 10-18: moderate (consider benzodiazepine). >18: severe (requires benzodiazepine, consider ICU). Score >15: risk of seizures/DTs.

CIWA-Ar enables symptom-triggered benzodiazepine dosing, which reduces total benzodiazepine dose and treatment duration compared to fixed-schedule dosing. CCSA guidelines recommend diazepam or chlordiazepoxide for alcohol withdrawal. Seizures typically occur 12-48 hours after last drink; DTs at 48-96 hours. Mortality of untreated DTs is 15-20%.

**Q:** What is the recommended approach to benzodiazepine tapering?

**A:** Switch to long-acting agent (diazepam) if on short-acting. Reduce dose by 10-25% every 1-2 weeks initially, then slower (5-10% reductions) for the last 25%. Total taper duration: 6-12 weeks minimum (longer for long-term use). Manage withdrawal symptoms with non-pharmacologic strategies, consider adjuncts (gabapentin, hydroxyzine, melatonin for insomnia).

Canadian Deprescribing Network provides a benzodiazepine deprescribing algorithm. Abrupt cessation risks withdrawal seizures, especially with short-acting agents. The equivalence table (diazepam 5 mg = lorazepam 1 mg = clonazepam 0.5 mg = alprazolam 0.5 mg) guides conversion. CBT for insomnia should replace benzodiazepine use long-term.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria and management of cannabis use disorder?

**A:** DSM-5: >=2 of 11 criteria within 12 months (craving, tolerance, withdrawal, impaired control, social impairment, risky use, continued despite problems). Cannabis withdrawal: irritability, anxiety, insomnia, decreased appetite, depressed mood (onset 1-2 days, peaks at 1 week). Management: CBT + motivational enhancement therapy. No approved pharmacotherapy.

Cannabis use disorder affects ~9% of users (17% of those starting in adolescence). Since legalization in Canada (2018), ED visits for cannabis-related presentations have increased. Cannabis hyperemesis syndrome (cyclic vomiting, hot shower relief) is increasingly recognized. CCSA recommends Canada's Lower-Risk Cannabis Use Guidelines for harm reduction.

**Q:** What should be included in naloxone kit education and who should receive one?

**A:** Who: all patients on opioids (prescribed or non-prescribed), their family/friends, people who use drugs, first responders. Education: recognize overdose signs (unresponsive, slow/absent breathing, pinpoint pupils, cyanosis), call 911, administer naloxone (IM 0.4 mg or intranasal 4 mg), rescue breathing, repeat naloxone q2-3 min if no response, recovery position.

Good Samaritan Drug Overdose Act (Canadian federal law) protects anyone calling 911 for an overdose from simple possession charges. Naloxone kits are available free at most Canadian pharmacies without prescription. CRISM recommends naloxone distribution as part of comprehensive OUD management. Naloxone duration is shorter than most opioids — monitor for re-sedation.

**Q:** What are the differences between methadone and buprenorphine/naloxone for OUD treatment?

**A:** Methadone: full mu-agonist, higher overdose risk, daily witnessed dosing initially (carries progressing to bi-weekly/monthly), QTc prolongation, more drug interactions, requires specialized authorization. Buprenorphine/naloxone: partial agonist (ceiling effect = safer), can prescribe from office, home dosing sooner, precipitated withdrawal if started too early, less sedating.

CRISM guidelines recommend buprenorphine as first-line over methadone. Buprenorphine micro-dosing (Bernese method) allows initiation without full withdrawal. Methadone is preferred when buprenorphine is insufficient (severe OUD, high tolerance). Both reduce mortality, criminal activity, and HIV transmission. Pregnancy: methadone or buprenorphine (without naloxone) monotherapy.

**Q:** What pharmacotherapy options exist for alcohol use disorder?

**A:** First-line: naltrexone 50 mg daily (or monthly injectable 380 mg) — reduces heavy drinking days. Acamprosate 666 mg TID — supports abstinence maintenance. Second-line: topiramate, gabapentin. Disulfiram (Antabuse) — aversion therapy, limited evidence, requires high motivation and supervision. Nalmefene (as-needed) available in some jurisdictions.

CCSA and CANMAT recommend naltrexone or acamprosate as first-line. Naltrexone is contraindicated in patients on opioids. Acamprosate is safe in hepatic impairment (renal excretion) — useful for patients with liver disease. Pharmacotherapy should be combined with psychosocial interventions (motivational interviewing, CBT, AA/mutual aid).

**Q:** What is stimulant use disorder management approach?

**A:** No approved pharmacotherapy for cocaine or methamphetamine use disorder. Treatment is psychosocial: contingency management (most effective for stimulant use), CBT, community reinforcement approach, motivational interviewing. Manage psychiatric comorbidities (depression, psychosis). Harm reduction: safer supply programs in some Canadian cities.

Stimulant-related deaths are rising in Canada alongside the opioid crisis. No medication has shown consistent benefit in RCTs for stimulant use disorder, though trials of lisdexamfetamine, topiramate, and mirtazapine are ongoing. Contingency management provides tangible rewards for negative urine screens. Psychosis management may require short-term antipsychotics.

**Q:** What is the Canadian tobacco cessation pharmacotherapy approach?

**A:** First-line: varenicline (Champix) — most effective single agent (OR 2.9 for abstinence), NRT (patch + short-acting form = combination NRT), bupropion. Combination NRT is as effective as varenicline. Start varenicline 1-2 weeks before quit date, 12-week course (can extend to 24 weeks). All should include counselling.

CAN-ADAPTT Canadian smoking cessation guidelines recommend pharmacotherapy for all smokers ready to quit. Varenicline does NOT increase neuropsychiatric events per EAGLES trial. NRT is safe in cardiovascular disease. E-cigarettes/vaping are not recommended as cessation aids by Canadian guidelines due to uncertain long-term safety and youth uptake concerns.

**Q:** What are the key features of fetal alcohol spectrum disorder (FASD)?

**A:** Requires confirmed or suspected prenatal alcohol exposure. Features: facial dysmorphology (smooth philtrum, thin upper lip, short palpebral fissures), growth deficiency, CNS dysfunction (microcephaly, executive function deficits, ADHD-like symptoms, learning disabilities, poor adaptive functioning). Canadian diagnostic guidelines use a multidisciplinary approach.

Canada has the highest rate of FASD in the developed world. The 2015 Canadian diagnostic guidelines use a standardized multidisciplinary assessment. FASD is a leading preventable cause of intellectual disability. No safe amount of alcohol in pregnancy has been established. Canadian guidelines recommend universal screening for alcohol use in prenatal care.

**Q:** Buprenorphine is used for opioid use disorder but has a 'ceiling effect' for analgesia and respiratory depression. What pharmacodynamic property explains this?

**A:** Buprenorphine is a partial agonist at the mu-opioid receptor. Partial agonists can only elicit a submaximal effect regardless of concentration — this ceiling effect limits both therapeutic and toxic effects compared to full agonists like morphine.

Partial agonists have reduced intrinsic efficacy compared to full agonists; they cannot produce the maximal receptor response even at saturating concentrations. This property makes buprenorphine safer than full agonists in opioid use disorder treatment.

**Q:** What are the recommended maximum daily opioid doses for patients with chronic non-cancer pain according to Canadian opioid guidelines?

**A:** Restrict to <90 MME/day (morphine milligram equivalents); ideally <50 MME/day, especially at starting dose. Patients already on ≥90 MME/day should be encouraged to taper collaboratively to the lowest effective dose.

Canadian opioid guidelines (CMAJ 2017; Busse et al.) emphasize optimizing non-opioid therapy first, restricting doses to minimize harm, and using slow collaborative tapers. Evidence shows that opioid tapering in chronic pain patients improves pain in ~80% of cases.

**Q:** What approach is recommended for patients with chronic non-cancer pain who have serious challenges tapering opioids?

**A:** A formal multidisciplinary pain management program is recommended for patients who cannot taper on their own.

Canadian national opioid guidelines emphasize collaborative, slow tapering approaches. When standard tapering fails, multidisciplinary programs that address biopsychosocial factors improve outcomes more effectively than dose adjustments alone.

## Suicide Risk Assessment

**Q:** A 45-year-old man expresses a wish to die. What are the key elements of suicide risk assessment?

**A:** SADPERSONS or Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS): assess ideation (passive/active/plan/intent/means), history of attempts, protective factors, psychiatric diagnosis, social support, current stressors, access to means (especially firearms, medications)

CANMAT and CPA (Canadian Psychiatric Association) guidelines: all suicidal patients require structured risk assessment. High-risk features: plan + intent + means, prior attempt, male sex, older age, hopelessness, access to lethal means. Risk assessment guides level of care: safety planning, voluntary/involuntary hospitalization.

**Q:** What are the key epidemiological facts about suicide in Canada?

**A:** ~4500 suicide deaths annually (Canada); highest rates in men 45-65 and Indigenous Canadians; men 3x more likely to die by suicide; women 3x more likely to attempt; firearms are the most lethal method (85% case fatality)

PHAC (Public Health Agency of Canada) suicide statistics: suicide is the 2nd leading cause of death for ages 15-34. Indigenous Canadians have 2-3x higher rates. Means restriction (reducing access to firearms and medications) is one of the most evidence-based suicide prevention strategies.

**Q:** What is the difference between suicidal ideation, intent, and plan?

**A:** Ideation: thoughts of death or self-harm; Intent: decision/resolution to act on ideation; Plan: specific method, time, and place identified — all three together = highest risk (requires immediate intervention and likely hospitalization)

C-SSRS distinguishes passive ideation ("I wish I were dead"), active ideation without plan, active with plan but no intent, active with intent and plan. Risk level guides intervention intensity. Safety planning and means restriction are key interventions before hospitalization.

**Q:** What is a safety plan and how does it differ from a no-harm contract?

**A:** Safety plan: personalized crisis management plan — warning signs, coping strategies, social contacts, professional resources, means restriction; Evidence-based. No-harm contract: "I will not hurt myself" promise — NOT evidence-based and may provide false reassurance

CANMAT guidelines: safety planning (Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention) is evidence-based and superior to no-harm contracts. Key elements: warning signs, internal coping strategies, social distractors, social supports to contact, crisis resources (distress lines), means restriction.

**Q:** Under what circumstances can a physician involuntarily admit a patient with suicidal ideation in Canada?

**A:** When patient has a mental disorder (not just suicidal ideation alone in some provinces), poses serious risk of physical harm to self or others, AND requires hospitalization for treatment — governed by provincial Mental Health Acts (e.g., Form 1 in Ontario, Form H1 in BC)

Each province has its own Mental Health Act for involuntary psychiatric admission. In Ontario, Form 1 (72h detention) can be completed by any physician who has examined the patient. Criteria must be met: serious mental disorder + imminent serious harm to self/others + needs hospitalization.

**Q:** What are the modifiable risk factors for suicide that should be targeted in treatment?

**A:** Hopelessness (strongest predictor), depression severity, untreated pain, access to lethal means (especially firearms), social isolation, substance use, poor treatment adherence — all are modifiable

CANMAT suicide prevention guidelines: reducing hopelessness is the strongest therapeutic target. Treating the underlying mental illness is essential. Means restriction: discuss safe storage of firearms and medications with family. Suicide-specific interventions: CBT-SP (CBT for suicide prevention), DBT, CAMS (Collaborative Assessment and Management of Suicidality).

**Q:** What are the protective factors that reduce suicide risk?

**A:** Social support (family, community), reasons for living (children, religious beliefs, pets), engaged in mental health treatment, problem-solving skills, positive therapeutic alliance, cultural/spiritual beliefs that discourage suicide

CANMAT guidelines: protective factors help balance risk assessment and guide individualized safety planning. Connectedness to family and community is a strong protective factor — especially in First Nations communities where community-based prevention programs are effective. Hope is a key therapeutic target.

**Q:** A 70-year-old man lost his wife 3 months ago and now expresses passive death wish. He drinks heavily and has given away his possessions. What is his risk level and management?

**A:** High risk — multiple risk factors: older male, recent loss, alcohol use, passive death wish, giving away possessions (preparatory behaviors); requires urgent psychiatric assessment and likely hospitalization

Giving away possessions and saying goodbyes are preparatory behaviors indicating high near-term risk. Older men are a high-risk group — often present to GPs rather than psychiatrists. Alcohol use markedly increases immediate suicide risk. CANMAT guidelines recommend hospitalization for high-risk patients.

**Q:** What is the evidence for lithium in suicide prevention?

**A:** Lithium reduces suicide and suicidal behavior by 60-80% in bipolar disorder and recurrent unipolar depression — the strongest anti-suicidal pharmacological agent; mechanism includes serotonergic effects and may reduce impulsivity

CANMAT Bipolar Guidelines: lithium is the only pharmacological agent with proven anti-suicidal properties. This is not true of valproate or other mood stabilizers. When starting lithium for bipolar disorder, the anti-suicidal benefit should be explicitly discussed with the patient.

**Q:** What is the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) and how is it used clinically?

**A:** C-SSRS: 2 categories — ideation severity (passive wish to be dead, active ideation, ideation with plan, ideation with intent) and behavior (preparatory acts, interrupted attempt, aborted attempt, actual attempt) — standardized risk assessment tool

CANMAT recommends C-SSRS as the standard clinical suicide risk assessment tool. C-SSRS has been validated in emergency departments, inpatient, and outpatient settings. Identifies individuals at risk more reliably than clinical interview alone. Free online training available.

**Q:** What is postvention and why is it important?

**A:** Postvention: support provided to survivors (family, friends, colleagues) after a suicide death — reduces grief complications, survivor guilt, and secondary PTSD; also involves connecting to resources and reducing suicide contagion

CANMAT/CAMH: suicide survivors have 3x higher risk of dying by suicide. Postvention programs for schools, workplaces, and communities are evidence-based. Media guidelines (Reporting on Suicide): avoid detailed method descriptions, romanticization, and sensationalism — reduce contagion effect.

**Q:** What is passive suicidal ideation and how does it differ from active suicidal ideation?

**A:** Passive: wishes to be dead or "not wake up" — "I wish I were dead" without intent to act; Active: actual thoughts of killing oneself, with or without specific plan or intent

Clinical assessment of passive vs. active ideation guides management. Passive ideation may escalate to active ideation — requires monitoring and treatment of underlying disorder. Both require empathic inquiry without minimization. Passive ideation alone does not typically warrant involuntary hospitalization if protective factors are strong.

**Q:** How should suicide risk be documented in the medical record?

**A:** Document: specific ideation content, presence/absence of plan and intent, means identified, history of attempts, protective factors, risk level assessment, clinical reasoning for management decision, plan and follow-up — comprehensive documentation supports continuity of care and medicolegal protection

CMPA (Canadian Medical Protective Association) guidelines: complete documentation of suicide risk assessment and rationale for management decisions is a medicolegal requirement. Include that means restriction was addressed. Document collateral information sources. Document capacity assessment if involuntary admission considered.

**Q:** What are the evidence-based psychotherapies for suicidal patients?

**A:** CBT for suicide prevention (CBT-SP), Dialectical Behavior Therapy (DBT) — especially for BPD, Collaborative Assessment and Management of Suicidality (CAMS), Brief Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (BCBT-MIL)

CANMAT: DBT (Marsha Linehan) is the gold standard for BPD with suicidal behavior — reduces attempts by 50%. CAMS is a therapeutic framework for collaborative suicide risk assessment and management. CBT-SP reduces attempts in adolescents. All approaches involve hope installation and reasons for living.

**Q:** What is physician-assisted dying (MAID) and how does it differ from suicide?

**A:** MAID (Medical Assistance in Dying): legal in Canada, for adults with a serious irremediable medical condition causing intolerable suffering — patient-requested, physician-administered or patient self-administered; NOT the same as suicide

Bill C-14 (2016) and C-7 (2021) legalized and expanded MAID in Canada. Eligibility: ≥18 years, serious irremediable illness, grievous/intolerable suffering (physical or psychological), capable of informed consent. Mental illness as sole underlying condition: planned eligibility from 2024. CANMAT provides guidance on mental health MAID requests.

**Q:** What is the SAD PERSONS suicide risk assessment?

**A:** Sex(male), Age(<19/>45), Depression, Previous attempt, Ethanol, Rational thinking loss, Social supports lacking, Organized plan, No spouse, Sickness(chronic)

Higher score = higher risk. Key high-risk factors: prior attempt (strongest predictor), male, access to lethal means, social isolation, hopelessness. Always assess intent, plan, means, protective factors.

**Q:** What is the SAD PERSONS scale for suicide risk assessment?

**A:** S: Sex (male), A: Age (<19 or >45), D: Depression, P: Previous attempt, E: Ethanol/drug abuse, R: Rational thinking loss (psychosis), S: Social supports lacking, O: Organized plan, N: No spouse, S: Sickness (chronic illness). Each scores 1 point. 0-2: may be safe to discharge with follow-up. 3-4: close follow-up. 5-6: strongly consider hospitalization. 7-10: hospitalize.

While SAD PERSONS is widely taught, its predictive validity is limited. Canadian guidelines recommend comprehensive risk assessment over sole reliance on scales. Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) is increasingly used in Canadian EDs. No scale replaces clinical judgment.

**Q:** What are the key risk factors for completed suicide?

**A:** Strongest: previous suicide attempt (single strongest predictor), psychiatric illness (MDD, bipolar, schizophrenia, BPD, SUD), male sex (3:1 completed suicide ratio M:F), access to lethal means (especially firearms). Other: older age, chronic pain/illness, recent discharge from psychiatric care, family history, social isolation, recent loss, Indigenous identity (3x rate in Canada).

In Canada, suicide is the 9th leading cause of death and 2nd leading cause in youth (15-24). Canadian Armed Forces members and Indigenous peoples have significantly elevated rates. Means restriction (e.g., safe firearm storage, bridge barriers) is one of the most effective population-level interventions.

**Q:** What is the approach to acute suicide risk assessment in the ED?

**A:** (1) Ensure safety (remove means, 1:1 observation). (2) Assess: suicidal ideation (active vs passive), plan (specific, lethal, available means), intent, timeline. (3) Prior attempts (method, lethality, rescue). (4) Risk factors and warning signs. (5) Protective factors (reasons for living, children, religious beliefs, social support, future-oriented thinking). (6) Formulation: low vs moderate vs high risk. (7) Disposition: safety plan + follow-up vs psychiatric consultation vs voluntary/involuntary admission.

No suicide risk assessment tool is predictive enough to determine disposition alone. Canadian psychiatric emergency guidelines emphasize clinical formulation. The "4 Ds" of high risk: desire, determined plan, dangerous method, deterrents absent. Asking about suicide does NOT increase risk-it opens dialogue.

**Q:** What is a safety plan and how does it differ from a no-harm contract?

**A:** Safety plan (Stanley-Brown): (1) Warning signs that crisis is developing, (2) Internal coping strategies (relaxation, distraction), (3) People/social settings that provide distraction, (4) People to contact for help (friends, family), (5) Professionals/agencies to contact (crisis lines, ED), (6) Making environment safe (means restriction). NO-HARM CONTRACTS are NOT recommended-no evidence of efficacy and may provide false reassurance.

Safety plans are evidence-based and reduce suicide attempts by 50% (Stanley et al, 2018). Canadian guidelines recommend safety planning for all patients with suicidal ideation. Crisis Services Canada 988 (national crisis line). Canadian Association for Suicide Prevention provides resources.

**Q:** What are the Canadian legal frameworks for involuntary psychiatric admission?

**A:** Each province has its own Mental Health Act. Common criteria: the person has a mental disorder AND is at risk of serious harm to self or others AND is unwilling/unable to consent to voluntary admission. Two physician certificates are typically required for extended hold. Initial police/physician hold: typically 72 hours for assessment. Patient has right to legal counsel and review board hearing.

In Ontario: Form 1 (72h assessment by physician), Form 3 (2-week certificate of involuntary admission), Form 4 (additional certificate for renewal). Criteria vary: some provinces include "deterioration criterion" (likely to deteriorate without treatment). Capacity to consent is assessed separately from certification under mental health legislation.

**Q:** What are the warning signs of imminent suicide risk?

**A:** Acute warning signs: talking about wanting to die, looking for means (guns, pills, heights), giving away possessions, saying goodbye, increased substance use, withdrawing from activities/people, dramatic mood change (calm after being depressed), researching methods online, making final arrangements.

Canadian Suicide Prevention Service (988) emphasizes recognizing acute warning signs. A sudden improvement in mood in a previously severely depressed patient may indicate they have made the decision to die and feel relieved. This should heighten, not decrease, concern.

**Q:** How should clinicians respond to a patient who discloses suicidal ideation?

**A:** (1) Remain calm and non-judgmental, (2) Validate feelings without reinforcing hopelessness, (3) Ask directly about plan, intent, means, and timeline-be specific, (4) Assess protective factors, (5) Determine level of risk, (6) Create safety plan collaboratively, (7) Means restriction counseling, (8) Document thoroughly, (9) Arrange appropriate follow-up or disposition. NEVER dismiss or minimize disclosure.

CMPA (Canadian Medical Protective Association) recommends thorough documentation of suicide risk assessments. Asking about suicide is a clinical duty-omission may constitute negligence. Canadian guidelines recommend that all healthcare providers receive suicide risk assessment training (Applied Suicide Intervention Skills Training-ASIST).

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of suicide in special populations in Canada?

**A:** Indigenous peoples: 3x national rate, Inuit youth 11x. LGBTQ+ youth: 4x more likely to attempt. Physicians: elevated risk compared to general population. Elderly men: highest rate of completed suicide per capita. Veterans/military: elevated risk. Post-discharge psychiatric patients: highest risk period is first 2 weeks after discharge.

Canadian federal government has identified Indigenous suicide as a public health crisis. Culturally appropriate, community-led interventions are essential. The "Hope for Wellness" helpline provides culturally competent crisis support for Indigenous peoples. Transition from inpatient to outpatient care is a critical high-risk period.

**Q:** What medications are associated with increased vs decreased suicide risk?

**A:** Increased risk: antidepressants in youth <25 (black box warning-monitor closely first 4 weeks), isotretinoin (controversial), corticosteroids, mefloquine, varenicline (warning removed but monitor). Decreased risk: lithium (only medication with strong anti-suicidal evidence-30% reduction), clozapine (reduces suicidality in schizophrenia-InterSePT trial), ketamine/esketamine (rapid anti-suicidal effect).

Health Canada black box warning on all antidepressants for age <25 mandates close monitoring (weekly for first 4 weeks). Lithium anti-suicidal effect is independent of mood-stabilizing effect. Clozapine is FDA-approved specifically for suicide risk reduction in schizophrenia. Canadian guidelines recommend lithium consideration when suicide risk is a prominent concern.

**Q:** What is the approach to self-harm (non-suicidal self-injury)?

**A:** NSSI: deliberate self-inflicted tissue damage without suicidal intent (cutting, burning, scratching). Distinct from suicide attempt but NSSI is a risk factor for eventual suicide. Assessment: function (emotion regulation, self-punishment, communication), frequency, severity. Treatment: DBT (most evidence), CBT, emotion regulation skills. Treat underlying conditions (BPD, depression). Do NOT assume it is "just attention-seeking."

NSSI prevalence is 15-25% of adolescents and 4% of adults. Canadian Emergency Department guidelines recommend compassionate, non-judgmental care. NSSI increases future suicide risk 3-6x. Social contagion (peer influence) is a recognized phenomenon, particularly in adolescent inpatient settings.

**Q:** What is the physician duty to report and duty to protect in suicidal patients?

**A:** Confidentiality may be broken when there is imminent risk of harm to self or others (per CMA Code of Ethics and provincial legislation). Duty to protect: warn identifiable third parties at risk (Tarasoff-type duty). Documentation requirements: risk assessment, clinical reasoning, safety plan, disposition rationale. Duty to follow up: arrange timely post-discharge care.

CMPA guidance emphasizes that adequate documentation of clinical reasoning protects against liability. Canadian courts have held physicians liable for inadequate suicide risk assessment. The standard is reasonable clinical judgment, not prediction of suicide. Collegial consultations should be documented.

**Q:** What is the approach to suicide bereavement and postvention?

**A:** Postvention: organized response after a suicide to promote healing and prevent further suicides (suicide contagion). Components: immediate crisis response for family/close contacts, screening for at-risk individuals, memorial guidance (avoid glorification), support groups. Bereaved individuals have elevated suicide risk (peer and family). Clinician self-care after patient suicide (50% of psychiatrists experience patient suicide during career).

Canadian Mental Health Association provides suicide bereavement resources. Media guidelines for reporting suicide (avoid details of method, avoid sensationalization) reduce imitative suicides. Schools should have postvention protocols. Clinicians experiencing patient suicide should access peer support-CMPA provides assistance.

**Q:** What are the protective factors against suicide and how are they incorporated into risk assessment?

**A:** Individual: problem-solving skills, coping abilities, reasons for living, fear of death/pain, future-oriented thinking, help-seeking behavior. Social: strong family connections, social support, children in the home, therapeutic alliance. Community: access to mental health services, cultural/religious beliefs against suicide. These factors modulate risk but do NOT eliminate it in the presence of strong risk factors.

Canadian suicide risk assessment frameworks (e.g., CARMS: Columbia, Assessment, Risk, Mitigation, Safety plan) integrate protective factors into formulation. Protective factors should be actively strengthened as part of the safety plan. Reasons for living (Linehan inventory) can be used therapeutically.

**Q:** What is the management of suicide risk in adolescents?

**A:** Assessment adapted for age: collateral from parents/school, assess social media involvement, bullying, LGBTQ+ identity/discrimination, academic stress. Treatment: CBT, DBT-A (adolescent adaptation), family therapy. Pharmacotherapy: fluoxetine has best risk-benefit profile (only SSRI consistently showing efficacy in adolescent MDD). Close monitoring during SSRI initiation. Safety: means restriction counseling with parents, school-based interventions.

Youth suicide is the second leading cause of death in Canadian adolescents. Kids Help Phone (1-800-668-6868) provides 24/7 support. CPS recommends universal screening for depression/suicidality in adolescents. Social media use and cyberbullying are emerging risk factors. School-based gatekeeper training programs (e.g., safeTALK) are widely implemented in Canada.

**Q:** What are the ethical considerations in end-of-life requests and distinguishing from suicidality?

**A:** Medical Assistance in Dying (MAID) is legal in Canada (Bill C-14, expanded C-7). Eligibility: >=18, capable of consent, serious and incurable illness/condition, in advanced state of irreversible decline, enduring suffering. Mental illness as sole condition: currently under legislative review/moratorium in Canada. MAID requests require 2 independent physician assessments. Suicidality from mental illness is NOT equivalent to a MAID request.

Canada is one of few countries where MAID is available for conditions where death is not reasonably foreseeable (Track 2). The extension of MAID to mental illness as sole condition was postponed to 2027. Clinicians must carefully differentiate MAID requests from suicidal ideation driven by treatable mental illness.

**Q:** What is the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) and how is it used?

**A:** Standardized tool assessing suicidal ideation severity (5 levels: wish to be dead, nonspecific active thoughts, active ideation without plan, active ideation with plan, active ideation with plan and intent) and suicidal behaviour (actual attempt, interrupted attempt, aborted attempt, preparatory acts). Guides risk stratification and intervention level.

C-SSRS is recommended by Canadian Association for Suicide Prevention and used in many Canadian EDs. It provides a common language for suicide risk communication between providers. Any endorsement of active ideation with plan and intent (level 4-5) warrants immediate safety intervention. The tool differentiates ideation from behaviour, guiding appropriate response.

**Q:** What are the essential components of a suicide safety plan?

**A:** 1) Warning signs (triggers, thoughts, moods, behaviours). 2) Internal coping strategies (distraction, self-soothing). 3) Social contacts for distraction. 4) People to ask for help (family, friends). 5) Professionals/agencies to contact (crisis line, therapist, ED). 6) Making the environment safe (means restriction). Documented, accessible, reviewed regularly.

Stanley-Brown Safety Planning is evidence-based and recommended over no-suicide contracts. Canadian Mental Health Commission supports safety planning as a brief intervention. The plan should be collaborative, patient-driven, and stored where accessible (wallet, phone). Studies show safety planning reduces suicidal behaviour by 50% in the 6 months following ED visit.

**Q:** What is means restriction and why is it the most effective suicide prevention strategy?

**A:** Removing or limiting access to lethal means (firearms, medications, bridges, pesticides). Most effective because: suicide attempts are often impulsive (70% within 1 hour of decision), method substitution is uncommon, reducing access to the planned method reduces overall suicide rate. Examples: medication lockboxes, safe firearm storage, bridge barriers.

Means restriction is the only suicide prevention strategy with Level 1 evidence. In Canada, firearm-related suicides account for 15% of deaths — safe storage counselling is recommended. Canadian guidelines advise asking about access to firearms and medications during risk assessment. Prescribing smaller quantities of lethal medications (TCAs, lithium) is a clinical application.

**Q:** What are the key components of a post-suicide attempt assessment?

**A:** Assess: lethality of attempt (method, medical severity), intent (did they expect to die, precautions against discovery, suicide note), current suicidal ideation (ongoing wish to die?), precipitants, access to means, psychiatric diagnosis, substance use, previous attempts, social supports, protective factors. Disposition: admit if ongoing ideation/plan/intent, inadequate supports, or high-lethality attempt.

The first 30 days post-attempt carry highest re-attempt risk (100x general population). Canadian guidelines recommend follow-up within 72 hours of ED discharge. The transition from ED to outpatient is the highest-risk period. Caring contacts (follow-up calls/texts) reduce re-attempts. Document risk assessment and rationale for disposition thoroughly.

**Q:** What are the unique risk factors for youth suicide?

**A:** Bullying/cyberbullying, social media/contagion effect, LGBTQ2S+ identity (especially with family rejection), Indigenous youth (3-5x higher rate), impulsivity, substance use (cannabis, alcohol), romantic breakup, academic pressure, previous self-harm, family history of suicide, access to means, recent discharge from psychiatric care.

Suicide is the second leading cause of death in Canadian youth aged 15-24. Indigenous youth have the highest rates, particularly in remote communities. The Canadian Paediatric Society recommends universal screening in clinical settings. Trevor Project evidence supports that family acceptance reduces LGBTQ2S+ suicide risk by 50%. Postvention (support after a peer suicide) prevents contagion.

**Q:** What protective factors against suicide should be assessed and strengthened?

**A:** Strong social connections (family, friends, community), reasons for living (children, religious beliefs, future plans), problem-solving skills, access to mental health care, restricted access to lethal means, cultural connectedness (especially for Indigenous peoples), employment/purpose, therapeutic alliance, treatment engagement.

Canadian suicide prevention frameworks emphasize building protective factors alongside reducing risk. The Assembly of First Nations and Inuit Tapiriit Kanatami highlight cultural continuity and self-determination as protective for Indigenous communities. Protective factors should be documented in risk assessments and actively reinforced in safety plans.

**Q:** What is the role of the 988 Suicide Crisis Helpline in Canada?

**A:** 988 launched in November 2023 as Canada's national suicide crisis helpline (call or text). Provides immediate support from trained crisis responders 24/7, in English and French. Connects callers to local crisis services. Designed to reduce barriers to accessing help and divert appropriate calls from 911/EDs.

The 988 line is operated by the Canadian Mental Health Association with PHAC funding. It complements existing provincial crisis lines and the Crisis Text Line (text HOME to 686868). Emergency physicians should include 988 in discharge safety plans. Healthcare providers should familiarize themselves with local crisis resources for warm handoffs.

**Q:** How should a physician document a suicide risk assessment?

**A:** Document: risk factors identified, protective factors, current suicidal ideation (frequency, duration, plan, intent, means access), mental status exam, collateral information obtained, clinical formulation of risk level (acute vs chronic, low/moderate/high), safety plan reviewed, means restriction discussed, disposition rationale, and follow-up plan.

Documentation of suicide risk assessment is both a clinical and medicolegal necessity. Canadian Medical Protective Association (CMPA) recommends documenting the clinical reasoning behind decisions, not just the outcome. Risk level should be described as a clinical judgment (not a prediction). Regular reassessment and updated documentation are expected standards of care.

**Q:** What is postvention and why is it important after a patient suicide?

**A:** Postvention: organized response after a suicide to support those affected (family, peers, healthcare team, community). Includes bereavement support, monitoring at-risk individuals (contagion effect), debriefing for healthcare providers, mortality review, and community-level response (school protocols after student suicide).

Each suicide directly affects an estimated 135 people. Healthcare providers are also affected — physician mental health support should be offered after patient suicide. Canadian guidelines recommend structured postvention protocols in schools and workplaces. The contagion effect is real, particularly among youth, requiring media guidelines and targeted monitoring.

**Q:** What are the warning signs that suggest imminent suicide risk requiring emergency intervention?

**A:** Expressing intent to die or kill oneself, actively searching for means (buying firearms, stockpiling medications), giving away possessions, saying goodbye, sudden calmness after a period of depression (may indicate decision made), writing a suicide note, increased substance use, social withdrawal, dramatic mood changes, insomnia/agitation, hopelessness, feeling like a burden.

The IS PATH WARM mnemonic summarizes warning signs: Ideation, Substance abuse, Purposelessness, Anxiety/agitation, Trapped, Hopelessness, Withdrawal, Anger, Recklessness, Mood change. Any patient expressing imminent intent should not be left alone and requires emergency psychiatric evaluation. Canadian Emergency Department guidelines recommend direct observation until assessment is complete.

**Q:** What are the four key functions of an emergency psychiatric assessment?

**A:** 1. Rule out medical cause of psychiatric symptoms. 2. Determine if psychiatric consult is needed. 3. Assess for safety issues (suicidal ideation, homicidal ideation). 4. Determine if patient is certifiable (present test: risk to self/others; future test: apparent mental illness).

Psychiatric emergencies require a systematic approach that prioritizes excluding organic causes (e.g., delirium, metabolic, structural) before attributing symptoms to primary psychiatric illness. Certification under mental health legislation enables involuntary treatment when safety is threatened.

**Q:** What epidemiological facts about suicide are important for MCCQE1?

**A:** Attempted suicide: females > males. Completed suicide: males > females. Suicide is the second leading cause of death in people under 24 years. Significantly increased incidence among marginalized communities, particularly Indigenous peoples and 2SLGBTQQIA+ Canadians.

Women attempt suicide more often but use less lethal methods; men use more lethal means resulting in higher completion rates. Indigenous Canadians and sexual/gender minority youth have disproportionately high suicide rates due to systemic social determinants including colonization and discrimination.

**Q:** What are the SAD PERSONS risk factors for suicide?

**A:** S – Sex (male), A – Age >45 yr, D – Depression, P – Previous attempts, E – Ethanol use, R – Rational thinking loss, S – Suicide in family, O – Organized plan, N – No spouse/support system, S – Serious illness.

The SAD PERSONS scale provides a structured mnemonic for suicide risk stratification. High-risk features include active intent with organized plan and access to lethal means, which are indications for admission. The Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale is another validated tool used in Canadian EDs.

**Q:** When should a suicidal patient be admitted from the ED?

**A:** Admit if there is: active intent and organized plan, or access to lethal means. Patient may require certification under local mental health legislation if unwilling to stay voluntarily. Even willing patients at high risk should be certified depending on local regulations. Do NOT start long-term psychiatric medications in the ED.

Certification under provincial mental health legislation (e.g., Ontario Mental Health Act) allows involuntary admission when a patient poses imminent risk. The standard is a two-part test: present risk and likely mental disorder. Intoxicated patients should be reassessed for suicidality once sober.

## General

**Q:** What is the SAD PERSONS scale used for?

**A:** Suicide risk assessment

SAD PERSONS is a validated scale to assess suicide risk in emergency psychiatric evaluations

**Q:** What medications are used for agitation in psychiatric emergencies?

**A:** Lorazepam 2mg PO/IM/SL or Olanzapine 5-10mg PO/IM or Haloperidol 5mg PO/IM

Offer medication to patients voluntarily when possible; both benzodiazepines and antipsychotics are effective

**Q:** What assessment scale is used to evaluate suicide risk?

**A:** SAD PERSONS scale or Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale

These validated scales help quantify suicide risk by assessing factors like sex, age, depression, previous attempts, organized plan, no social support, and specific suicidal ideation.

**Q:** When is LP indicated in psychiatric presentation?

**A:** When anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis is suspected

Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis presents with psychiatric symptoms and requires CSF analysis for diagnosis (anti-NMDA receptor antibodies).

**Q:** What is a critical safety concern with TCAs?

**A:** Narrow therapeutic window, lethal in overdose

TCAs are dangerous in overdose due to cardiac toxicity and can be lethal even with relatively small ingestions.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of SNRIs?

**A:** Block serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake

SNRIs (e.g., venlafaxine, desvenlafaxine) inhibit reuptake of both serotonin and norepinephrine.

**Q:** What is a major concern with TCAs in overdose?

**A:** Narrow therapeutic window, lethal in overdose

TCAs have a narrow therapeutic index and can be fatal in relatively small overdoses due to cardiac toxicity.

**Q:** What is the major safety concern with TCAs?

**A:** Narrow therapeutic window, lethal in overdose

TCAs have a narrow therapeutic index, making overdose potentially fatal due to cardiac toxicity and CNS depression.

## Psychiatry

**Q:** A 7-year-old boy is brought in by his parents for frequent angry outbursts, arguing with adults, and deliberately annoying his siblings. These behaviors have been present for 8 months. He is spiteful at least twice per month. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**A:** Oppositional Defiant Disorder (ODD)

ODD is characterized by a pattern of negativistic, hostile, and defiant behaviour lasting ≥6 months with ≥4 symptoms including angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behavior, and vindictiveness. The frequency must exceed normative behavior for age, gender, and culture.

**Q:** A 14-year-old boy has a history of bullying, initiating fights, cruelty to animals, and truancy. He was caught shoplifting. What diagnosis requires ≥3 criteria in the past 12 months and ≥1 in the past 6 months?

**A:** Conduct Disorder (CD)

CD involves a repetitive pattern of behavior violating the rights of others and age-appropriate social norms. It includes aggression to people/animals, destruction of property, deceitfulness/theft, and rule violations. 50% of children with CD develop antisocial personality disorder as adults.

**Q:** A patient on lithium 900 mg/d presents with severe nausea, vomiting, ataxia, slurred speech, and tremor. Lithium level is 2.3 mmol/L. What is the most appropriate management?

**A:** Discontinue lithium, IV normal saline, and hemodialysis

Lithium toxicity is a clinical diagnosis that can occur even at therapeutic levels. Hemodialysis is indicated when lithium level is >2 mmol/L, or if there is coma, shock, severe dehydration, or failure to respond to treatment within 24 hours. Common precipitants include NSAIDs, thiazides, ACEIs, and dehydration.

**Q:** Which mood stabilizer requires very slow titration due to the risk of Stevens-Johnson Syndrome, and is first-line for bipolar I depression?

**A:** Lamotrigine (Lamictal)

Lamotrigine requires very slow dose titration to reduce the risk of Stevens-Johnson Syndrome, a potentially life-threatening skin reaction. It is first-line monotherapy for bipolar I depression and bipolar disorder maintenance, but has limited efficacy in preventing mania.

**Q:** Which atypical antipsychotic is most effective for treatment-resistant schizophrenia but requires weekly CBC monitoring for the first 6 months due to risk of agranulocytosis?

**A:** Clozapine (Clozaril)

Clozapine is the gold standard for treatment-resistant schizophrenia, with approximately 50% of patients benefiting. It carries a 1% risk of agranulocytosis requiring ongoing CBC monitoring (weekly x 6 months, then q2 weeks). Other serious side effects include metabolic syndrome, cardiomyopathy, severe constipation, and seizures.

**Q:** What are the three benzodiazepines safe to use in patients with impaired liver function, and why?

**A:** Lorazepam, Oxazepam, and Temazepam (LOT) - they undergo glucuronidation only and have no active metabolites

LOT benzodiazepines are preferred in liver disease because they are metabolized by direct glucuronidation (phase II metabolism) rather than oxidative (phase I) hepatic metabolism. This means they do not produce active metabolites and their clearance is less affected by hepatic dysfunction.

**Q:** In Ontario, what is a Community Treatment Order (CTO) and what are the criteria for a physician to issue one?

**A:** A CTO orders a person with serious mental disorder to receive treatment in the community. Criteria: prior psychiatric hospitalization (>30 days over ≥2 hospitalizations in past 3 years or previous CTO in past 3 years), community treatment plan, physician examination within 72 hours, patient ability to comply, and consent.

CTOs are designed for patients who experience a pattern of psychiatric admission, stabilization, release, treatment non-adherence, destabilization, and readmission. They are valid for 6 months and are consent-based. If violated, a Form 47 can be issued for police to bring the patient for examination.

**Q:** What are the key components of a psychiatric Mental Status Exam (MSE)?

**A:** AASEPTIC: Appearance and behavior, Attitude, Speech, Emotion (mood and affect), Perception, Thought content and process, Insight and judgment, Cognition.

The MSE is analogous to the physical exam. Mood is subjective (patient's own words), affect is objective. The spectrum of affect ranges from full to restricted to blunted to flat. Always assess suicidal ideation as part of every encounter.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for schizophrenia?

**A:** ≥2 symptoms for significant portion of 1 month (at least one must be delusions, hallucinations, or disorganized speech): delusions, hallucinations, disorganized speech, catatonic behavior, negative symptoms. Functional decline. Continuous signs ≥6 months. Rule out mood disorders, substances, and medical conditions.

Prevalence is 0.3-0.7%. The dopamine hypothesis explains positive symptoms (mesolimbic excess) and negative symptoms (prefrontal deficit). Over time: 1/3 improve, 1/3 stable, 1/3 worsen. 5-6% die by suicide.

**Q:** How do you differentiate brief psychotic disorder, schizophreniform disorder, and schizophrenia by duration?

**A:** Brief psychotic disorder: <1 month. Schizophreniform disorder: 1-6 months. Schizophrenia: >6 months.

Duration is the key differentiating factor. Brief psychotic disorder has good prognosis and is self-limiting. One-third of schizophreniform patients recover; two-thirds progress to schizophrenia.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for a major depressive episode (MDE)?

**A:** ≥5 symptoms for ≥2 weeks, must include depressed mood or anhedonia. MSIGECAPS: Mood depressed, Sleep changes, Interest decreased, Guilt/worthlessness, Energy decreased, Concentration decreased, Appetite changes, Psychomotor changes, Suicidal ideation.

MDD has a lifetime prevalence of 11% in Canada with peak at 15-25 years (F:M=2:1). First-line treatment includes SSRIs/SNRIs plus psychotherapy. ECT is the fastest and most effective treatment for severe or treatment-resistant cases.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for a manic episode?

**A:** ≥1 week of elevated/expansive/irritable mood with increased energy, plus ≥3 symptoms (4 if irritable only): GST PAID - Grandiosity, Sleep decreased, Talkative/pressured speech, Pleasurable activities with painful consequences, Activity increased, Ideas (flight of), Distractibility.

A full manic episode differentiates Bipolar I from Bipolar II (which has only hypomania). Mania requires marked functional impairment or hospitalization. Hypomania lasts ≥4 days without psychotic features or severe impairment.

**Q:** What is the key difference between Bipolar I and Bipolar II disorder?

**A:** Bipolar I requires at least one manic episode (MDE not required). Bipolar II requires at least one hypomanic episode plus at least one MDE, with no history of mania.

Bipolar II is not a 'milder' form - patients spend 50% of time depressed vs 32% in Bipolar I. Bipolar II is often missed with an average delay of a decade before diagnosis. Monotherapy with antidepressants should be avoided due to switch risk.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for bipolar depression?

**A:** Lithium, lurasidone, quetiapine, or lamotrigine (the 4 Ls: Lithium, Lamotrigine, Lurasidone, Seroquel). Antidepressants are second-line and must be used with a mood stabilizer.

Monotherapy with antidepressants is avoided in bipolar depression due to risk of switching to mania. Quetiapine is first-line for Bipolar II depression. Lithium has demonstrated efficacy in preventing suicide completions.

**Q:** What is postpartum depression and how does it differ from 'baby blues'?

**A:** Baby blues: transient mood instability in 50-80% of mothers, onset 2-4 days postpartum, lasts up to 10 days, no treatment needed. Postpartum depression: MDE with onset during pregnancy or within 4 weeks of delivery, lasts 2-6 months, occurs in 3-6%, may include psychotic features.

Risk factors include prior mood disorder, psychosocial stressors, and lack of social support. Treatment includes psychotherapy (CBT/IPT) and SSRIs. ECT is considered for severe or psychotic presentations. Untreated depression impacts child cognitive development and attachment.

**Q:** What is the SAD PERSONS mnemonic for suicide risk factors?

**A:** Sex (male), Age >60, Depression, Previous attempts, Ethanol/substance abuse, Rational thinking loss, Social support lacking, Organized plan, No spouse/partner, Sickness (chronic/serious illness).

The best predictor of completed suicide is a prior attempt. Most common psychiatric disorders associated with completed suicide are mood disorders and alcohol use disorders. Attempted:completed ratio is 20:1 overall (100:1 in young, 4:1 in elderly).

**Q:** What is the approach to a suicidal patient in the emergency department?

**A:** Assess ideation (passive vs active), plan, intent, access to means, past attempts, protective factors. Higher risk (plan + intent + access): hospitalize (Form 1 if refusing). Lower risk (no active plan): safety plan, address underlying disorders, ensure follow-up.

Asking about suicide does not increase risk. Poor correlation exists between clinical impression and actual attempt frequency. Document clinical rationale for management decisions. Patients with alcohol issues often see suicidality resolve with abstinence.

**Q:** What are the types of delusions?

**A:** Persecutory: others trying to cause harm. Reference: ordinary events have personal significance. Erotomanic: another person is in love with you. Grandiose: special powers/talents. Religious: receiving divine instructions. Somatic: physical disorder/defect. Nihilistic: things don't exist.

Delusions are fixed, false beliefs not amenable to change despite conflicting evidence. Bizarre delusions involve impossible situations (alien abduction). Non-bizarre delusions involve plausible situations (being followed). Obsessions differ from delusions in being ego-dystonic.

**Q:** What is the schizoaffective disorder diagnostic criteria?

**A:** A) Uninterrupted illness with major mood episode concurrent with Criterion A of schizophrenia. B) Delusions or hallucinations for ≥2 weeks in absence of mood symptoms. C) Mood symptoms present for majority of total illness duration.

Schizoaffective disorder lies between schizophrenia and mood disorders. Criterion B distinguishes it from mood disorder with psychotic features. Treatment combines antipsychotics, mood stabilizers, and antidepressants.

**Q:** What is the Generalized Anxiety Disorder medical workup?

**A:** Routine: vitals, physical exam, CBC, electrolytes, thyroid function, glucose, ECG. Additional as indicated: extended electrolytes, vitamin B12, β-HCG, folate, CXR.

The differential diagnosis of anxiety includes hyperthyroidism, pheochromocytoma, cardiac arrhythmias, pulmonary embolism, and substance intoxication/withdrawal. Always rule out medical and substance-related causes before diagnosing a primary anxiety disorder.

**Q:** What is the significance of duration of untreated psychosis (DUP)?

**A:** Longer DUP is associated with worse outcomes: poorer treatment response, fewer remissions, poorer social functioning, and more severe negative symptoms.

Meta-analyses show DUP is a potentially modifiable prognostic factor. Early intervention programs aim to reduce DUP. DUP was not associated with positive symptoms, neurocognitive function, or quality of life in studies.

**Q:** What is the management of treatment-resistant schizophrenia?

**A:** Clozapine is the gold standard for treatment-resistant schizophrenia.

Treatment resistance is defined as failure to respond to adequate trials of ≥2 antipsychotics. Long-acting injectable antipsychotics reduce relapse and rehospitalization compared to oral alternatives. Adjunctive agents include mood stabilizers for aggression and ECT.

**Q:** What are the good prognostic factors for schizophrenia?

**A:** Late onset, acute onset, identifiable precipitant, shorter prodrome, female gender, married, good premorbid functioning, good cognitive function, no family history, presence of affective symptoms, absence of structural brain abnormalities, good drug response.

Over time, 1/3 of schizophrenia patients improve, 1/3 remain the same, and 1/3 worsen. Positive symptoms typically respond to treatment, while negative symptoms and cognitive deficits tend to be most persistent.

**Q:** What is the difference between persistent depressive disorder and MDD?

**A:** Persistent depressive disorder: depressed mood for most days for ≥2 years (≥1 year in children) with ≥2 of: appetite changes, sleep changes, low energy, low self-esteem, poor concentration, hopelessness. MDD: ≥5 MSIGECAPS symptoms for ≥2 weeks.

Persistent depressive disorder has a lifetime prevalence of 2-3%. It can coexist with MDE ('double depression'). Combination of psychotherapy and pharmacotherapy may be most efficacious. In children, mood can be irritable rather than depressed.

**Q:** What screening questions should be included in every psychiatric assessment?

**A:** Depression: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless? Anxiety: Do you feel anxious or worry about things? Psychosis: Do you see or hear things others don't? Mania: Have you felt euphoric with lots of energy and decreased need for sleep? Suicidality: Have you had thoughts of harming or killing yourself?

These screening questions cover the major psychiatric disorders. Substance use and medical causes should always be ruled out before considering psychiatric diagnoses. Every encounter must include a suicide risk assessment.

**Q:** What is the typical response timeline to antidepressant treatment?

**A:** Physical symptoms improve at 2 weeks, mood/cognition improve by 4 weeks. If no improvement after 4 weeks at maximum tolerated dose, alter regimen.

First-line for moderate or greater MDD: SSRIs/SNRIs, with escitalopram, mirtazapine, sertraline, and venlafaxine showing evidence of superiority. For non-response, options include dose optimization, switching, or augmentation with aripiprazole or quetiapine.

**Q:** What is the tetrad of neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS)?

**A:** Mental status changes (usually first), fever, rigidity, and autonomic instability.

NMS is a psychiatric emergency caused by strong dopamine blockade, most common with high-potency and depot antipsychotics. Labs show elevated CPK, leukocytosis, and myoglobinuria. Treatment includes discontinuing antipsychotics, hydration, cooling, dantrolene, and bromocriptine.

**Q:** What is the treatment for neuroleptic malignant syndrome?

**A:** Discontinue the antipsychotic, supportive care (hydration, cooling blankets), dantrolene (muscle relaxant), and bromocriptine (dopamine agonist).

NMS develops over 24-72 hours and carries a 5% mortality rate. Risk factors include sudden dose increases, starting new drugs, dehydration, and male sex. It is essential to distinguish NMS from serotonin syndrome.

**Q:** Which antipsychotic is considered most effective for treatment-resistant schizophrenia?

**A:** Clozapine.

All other antipsychotics are considered equally effective, but clozapine uniquely demonstrates superior efficacy in treatment-resistant schizophrenia. Its use is limited by the risk of agranulocytosis (1%), requiring regular CBC monitoring (weekly for 6 months, then biweekly).

**Q:** What are the main side effects that distinguish typical from atypical antipsychotics?

**A:** Typical antipsychotics have higher risk of EPS and tardive dyskinesia; atypical antipsychotics have more metabolic side effects (weight gain, diabetes, dyslipidemia).

Both generations are equally effective except clozapine. The risk of weight gain follows the order: clozapine > olanzapine > quetiapine > risperidone. Choice of agent should consider the patient's side effect profile and prior response.

**Q:** What is the order of onset for antidepressant effects?

**A:** Neuro-vegetative/physical symptoms improve in 1-3 weeks; emotional/cognitive symptoms improve in 2-6 weeks.

During the first 2 weeks of therapy, patients may have improved energy but persistent suicidal ideation, placing them at increased risk. This is especially concerning in children and adolescents, requiring close monitoring early in treatment.

**Q:** Which SSRI is safest in pregnancy and breastfeeding?

**A:** Sertraline.

Sertraline has the most evidence supporting safety in pregnancy. Fluoxetine is the most activating SSRI and is most used in children/adolescents. Citalopram and escitalopram have the fewest drug interactions. Paroxetine should be avoided in pregnancy due to cardiac defect risk.

**Q:** What symptoms characterize serotonin syndrome?

**A:** Nausea, diarrhea, palpitations, diaphoresis, restlessness, confusion, lethargy, progressing to myoclonus, hyperthermia, rigor, and hypertonicity.

Serotonin syndrome results from overstimulation of the serotonergic system, often from drug combinations (SSRI + MAOI, SSRI + SNRI, MAOI + meperidine). Treatment involves discontinuing the medication and emergency supportive care. It must be distinguished from NMS.

**Q:** Which antidepressants are most likely to cause discontinuation syndrome?

**A:** Paroxetine, fluvoxamine, and venlafaxine (drugs with shortest half-lives).

Symptoms begin within 1-3 days of cessation and include anxiety, insomnia, irritability, nausea, dizziness, and flu-like symptoms. Fluoxetine rarely causes discontinuation syndrome due to its long half-life. Treatment is to restart the antidepressant and taper slowly.

**Q:** What are the therapeutic levels and monitoring requirements for lithium?

**A:** Acute mania: 1.0-1.25 mmol/L; maintenance: 0.8-1.0 mmol/L (adult), 0.6-0.8 mmol/L (geriatric). Monitor levels every 5-7 days until therapeutic, then monthly, then every 2-3 months. Monitor thyroid and creatinine every 6 months.

Lithium toxicity is a clinical diagnosis as toxicity can occur at therapeutic levels. Common causes include overdose, sodium/fluid loss, NSAIDs, diuretics, and ACEIs. Signs include severe GI symptoms, cerebellar signs, and cerebral signs.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of lithium toxicity?

**A:** GI (severe nausea/vomiting/diarrhea), cerebellar (ataxia, slurred speech), and cerebral (drowsiness, tremor, seizures, coma).

Management includes discontinuing lithium, monitoring levels and electrolytes, IV saline, and hemodialysis if level >2 mmol/L or in cases of coma, shock, severe dehydration, or deterioration. NSAIDs, thiazides, ACEIs, and metronidazole decrease lithium clearance.

**Q:** What is the Canadian guideline for acute psychosis treatment in the emergency setting?

**A:** Haloperidol 5 mg IM + lorazepam 2 mg IM; or loxapine 25 mg PO/IM + lorazepam 2 mg IM; or olanzapine 2.5-10 mg; or risperidone 2 mg.

The goal is rapid tranquilization to ensure patient and staff safety. Olanzapine can be given PO, IM, or as oral quick dissolve, though the quick dissolve form has the same time to peak as regular PO (4-6 hours).

**Q:** What are the four key problem areas addressed by Interpersonal Therapy (IPT)?

**A:** 1. Grief and loss, 2. Role transitions, 3. Conflict, 4. Interpersonal deficits.

IPT is indicated for mood disorders and typically involves 12-20 weekly sessions. It is ideal for individuals with depression or bipolar disorder who have some insight and difficult social functioning, but not for those with severe psychotic process, personality disorders, or comorbid substance use.

**Q:** What are the four skills taught in Dialectical Behavioural Therapy (DBT)?

**A:** Mindfulness, emotional regulation, interpersonal effectiveness, and distress tolerance.

DBT combines CBT techniques with Buddhist Zen mindfulness practices. It is primarily indicated for borderline personality disorder and involves four components: individual therapy, group skills training, phone consultations, and a consultation team. Treatment typically lasts 1 year.

**Q:** What is the duration of a Form 1 (Application for Psychiatric Assessment) in Ontario?

**A:** 72 hours after hospitalization.

Form 1 is signed by any physician within 7 days of examining the patient and is used when patients are thought to be in imminent danger of harming themselves or others, or when they are incapable of self-care. The patient does not have the right to a Review Board hearing on Form 1 alone.

**Q:** What criteria must be met for a Community Treatment Order (CTO) in Ontario?

**A:** Prior psychiatric hospitalization (cumulative >30 days over >2 hospitalizations in past 3 years, or previous CTO), a community treatment plan, physician examination within 72 hours, ability to comply, rights advisor consultation, and patient/substitute decision-maker consent.

CTOs are valid for 6 months and are consent-based. If violated, a Form 47 allows police to bring the patient for examination. CTOs are intended for patients with serious mental disorders who experience cycles of admission, stabilization, treatment discontinuation, and readmission.

**Q:** What did the CATIE trial conclude about first-generation vs. second-generation antipsychotics?

**A:** First and second-generation antipsychotics did not differ in overall effectiveness. Perphenazine was the most cost-effective drug.

The CATIE trial randomized 1460 patients with chronic schizophrenia to perphenazine, olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone, or ziprasidone for up to 18 months. Individual clinical circumstances impacted drug effectiveness, highlighting the importance of patient factors in prescribing.

**Q:** What did the STAR*D trial show about depression treatment?

**A:** Cumulative remission rate was 67% across up to 4 treatment steps. Remission rates decreased with each successive step (36.8%, 30.6%, 13.7%, 13.0%), and patients requiring more steps had higher relapse rates.

STAR*D demonstrated that patients with persistent MDD benefit from trying successive treatment strategies, but the likelihood of remission decreases with each additional step. This supports aggressive initial treatment and early augmentation or switching strategies.

**Q:** What distinguishes ODD from Conduct Disorder in children?

**A:** ODD: pattern of negativistic/hostile/defiant behavior (angry mood, argumentative, vindictive) for ≥6 months without violation of others' rights. CD: pattern violating rights of others and social norms (aggression to people/animals, property destruction, deceitfulness, rule violation) for ≥12 months.

ODD prevalence is 2-16% and usually appears in preschool. 50% of children with CD develop ASPD as adults. Poor prognosis in CD includes early onset, high frequency/variety of behaviors, comorbid ADHD, and early substance misuse. ODD is superseded by disruptive mood dysregulation disorder if both criteria are met.

**Q:** What benzodiazepines are preferred for patients with impaired liver function?

**A:** LOT: Lorazepam, Oxazepam, Temazepam — these have no first-pass liver metabolism.

Benzodiazepines potentiate GABA binding to its receptors, decreasing neuronal activity. They should be used for limited periods to avoid tolerance and dependence. Side effects include drowsiness, cognitive impairment, falls, and respiratory depression. Flumazenil is the specific antagonist for benzodiazepine overdose.

**Q:** A 35-year-old woman with a history of two prior depressive episodes presents with depressed mood for 3 weeks. She asks about the risk of recurrence if she recovers. What is the approximate risk of recurrence after 2 depressive episodes?

**A:** Approximately 70% risk of recurrence after 2 episodes (50% after 1 episode, 90% after 3 episodes).

Depression recurrence risk increases with each episode. After 1 episode there is a 50% chance, after 2 episodes 70%, and after 3 episodes 90%. This underscores the importance of maintenance therapy in recurrent depression.

**Q:** Which first-line antidepressant is recommended for youth with depression, and which SSRI is specifically NOT recommended for this population?

**A:** Fluoxetine is the first-line SSRI for youth. Paroxetine is NOT recommended for youth (controversial increased suicidality risk).

Fluoxetine has the most evidence for safety and efficacy in pediatric depression. Paroxetine is avoided due to concerns about increased suicidal ideation in younger patients. The mnemonic M-SIGECAPS can help screen for major depression.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for primary insomnia, and when should pharmacotherapy be considered?

**A:** First-line: cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) including sleep hygiene, relaxation therapy, stimulus control, and sleep restriction. Pharmacotherapy (short-term <14 days) is used to supplement CBT only.

CBT is superior to pharmacotherapy for long-term insomnia management. If medications are needed, options include short-acting benzodiazepines (used cautiously), zopiclone, zolpidem, melatonin, or sedating antidepressants (trazodone).

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacotherapy class for depression in adults?

**A:** SSRIs (e.g., sertraline, escitalopram, fluoxetine). First-line for youth is fluoxetine specifically.

SSRIs are preferred due to their favorable side-effect profile compared to TCAs or MAOIs. Paroxetine is not recommended for youth. The mnemonic M-SIGECAPS helps recall depression criteria.

**Q:** What is the risk of depression recurrence after 1, 2, and 3 episodes?

**A:** 50% after 1 episode, 70% after 2 episodes, 90% after 3 episodes.

Depression recurrence risk increases significantly with each episode. Up to 40% of first episodes resolve spontaneously within 6-12 months. This guides the decision on duration of maintenance therapy.

**Q:** What components are included in the Identifying Data section of a psychiatric history?

**A:** Name, age, gender (preferred pronouns), living situation, marital/relationship status, children, source of income/support, occupation, drug/therapy coverage, and current mental health supports.

Identifying data provides essential context for understanding a patient's social determinants of health and support systems. Clarifying preferred pronouns is essential for respectful, affirming care. In Canada, knowledge of drug/therapy coverage impacts treatment planning as mental health services vary by province.

**Q:** What are the 5 key screening questions for major psychiatric disorders in a clinical encounter?

**A:** 1) Depression/hopelessness, 2) Anxiety/worry, 3) Psychosis (seeing/hearing things others don't), 4) Mania (euphoria, talkativeness, energy, decreased sleep need), 5) Suicidal/self-harm ideation.

These screening questions form a rapid psychiatric review of systems. A positive response to any screen warrants further detailed exploration. The mnemonic DAMPS (Depression, Anxiety, Mania, Psychosis, Suicide) can be used. In Canada, suicide risk assessment is mandatory in all psychiatric encounters.

**Q:** What is the mnemonic AASEPTIC for the Mental Status Examination?

**A:** A - Appearance and behaviour; A - Attitude; S - Speech and affect; E - Emotion (mood and affect); P - Perception; T - Thought content and process; I - Insight and judgment; C - Cognition.

The MSE is analogous to the physical exam — it focuses on current signs, affect, behaviour, and cognition. It is documented after history taking and provides objective data on the patient's current mental state. Each domain contributes to diagnosis and treatment planning.

**Q:** Distinguish between mood and affect in the Mental Status Examination.

**A:** Mood = subjective emotional state (in patient's own words). Affect = objective emotional state inferred from emotional responses to stimuli, described in terms of quality, range, stability, and mood congruence.

Affect spectrum from normal to abnormal: Full > Restricted > Blunted > Flat. Quality includes: euthymic, depressed, elevated, anxious, irritable. Affect stability ranges from fixed to labile. Mood-affect incongruence (e.g., laughing while describing a sad event) can indicate psychosis or other disorders.

**Q:** What is the difference between a hallucination and an illusion in psychiatry?

**A:** Hallucination: sensory perception in the absence of appropriate external stimuli (similar in quality to a true perception). Illusion: misperception of a REAL external stimulus (e.g., mistaking a coat on a rack as a person).

The most common type of hallucination is auditory. Tactile, visual, olfactory, and gustatory hallucinations also occur. Auditory hallucinations are classic in schizophrenia; visual hallucinations suggest organic causes (delirium, substance use). Illusions are not pathological in isolation — context matters.

**Q:** What is the difference between depersonalization and derealization?

**A:** Depersonalization: change in self-awareness where the person feels unreal, distant, or detached from their own body, and/or unable to feel emotion. Derealization: feeling that the world/outer environment is unreal.

Both are dissociative experiences. Depersonalization = 'I feel unreal' (self-referential). Derealization = 'The world feels unreal' (external). They often co-occur and can be symptoms of depersonalization/derealization disorder, PTSD, panic disorder, or substance use. May also occur briefly in healthy individuals under stress.

**Q:** Define and distinguish the following thought process abnormalities: circumstantial, tangential, flight of ideas, and loosening of associations.

**A:** Circumstantial: indirect/delayed, eventually returns to the point. Tangential: speech does not return to the original point. Flight of ideas: rapidly skipping from one idea to another with marginal connections (mania). Loosening of associations/derailment: illogical shifting between topics with no clear connection.

These thought process abnormalities reflect disruption of the normal linear, goal-directed flow of thought. Flight of ideas is associated with mania/hypomania. Loosening of associations (derailment) is a hallmark of schizophrenia. Tangential thinking is more pathological than circumstantial. Word salad (jumble of words lacking meaning) represents the most severe form.

**Q:** What is the key feature that differentiates an obsession from a delusion?

**A:** Obsessions are ego-dystonic (unwanted, distressing, patient recognizes them as products of their own mind). Delusions are ego-syntonic (consistent with patient's sense of self, believed to be true).

This distinction is clinically important for diagnosis. In OCD, patients recognize obsessions as irrational and unwanted, causing significant distress. In psychotic disorders, delusions are believed with full conviction. Cannot be corrected by logic or reason. Obsessions also cannot be stopped by logic but patient retains insight that they are products of their own mind.

**Q:** List the types of delusions and their definitions.

**A:** Persecutory: belief others are trying to cause harm. Reference: interpreting ordinary events as specifically directed at oneself. Erotomania: belief that another person is in love with you. Grandiose: belief of having special powers/talents. Religious: belief of receiving instructions/powers from a higher being. Somatic: belief of having a physical disorder/defect. Nihilistic: belief that things do not exist or are not real.

Delusions are fixed false beliefs held despite incontrovertible evidence to the contrary, and are inconsistent with the patient's cultural or religious background. Persecutory delusions are the most common type. First-rank symptoms of schizophrenia include thought insertion, withdrawal, and broadcasting.

**Q:** What are the first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia involving thought?

**A:** Thought insertion (belief that thoughts are being placed into one's mind), thought withdrawal (belief that thoughts are being removed from one's mind), and thought broadcasting (belief that one's thoughts are being transmitted to others).

Schneider's first-rank symptoms were historically important for diagnosing schizophrenia. While no longer required for diagnosis in DSM-5, they remain clinically significant. All three are delusional in nature — the patient believes an external agent is controlling their thoughts. These are distinct from thought process abnormalities.

**Q:** What is the MMSE scoring system for cognitive impairment?

**A:** Total score out of 30. Score ≥26: normal. Score 20-25: mild impairment. Score 10-19: moderate impairment. Score <10: severe impairment.

The MMSE assesses: orientation (time and place), memory (immediate and delayed recall), attention and concentration, language (comprehension, reading, writing, repetition, naming), and spatial ability (intersecting pentagons). The Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA) is more sensitive for mild cognitive impairment. Both are standard screening tools in Canada.

**Q:** What does the biopsychosocial formulation approach involve in psychiatric assessment?

**A:** Three domains: Biological, Psychological, and Social. For each category, consider predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating, and protecting (4Ps) factors.

The biopsychosocial model is the foundation of modern psychiatric formulation. The 4Ps framework helps structure understanding: predisposing factors (vulnerabilities), precipitating factors (triggers), perpetuating factors (what maintains the illness), and protecting factors (resilience, supports). This guides comprehensive treatment planning beyond just pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** What is the ratio of attempted to completed suicide, and how does it vary by age?

**A:** Overall: attempted:completed = 20:1. Younger persons: 100:1. Older persons: 4:1. Sex: M:F = 1:4 for attempts, but 3:1 for completed suicide.

Older adults have a much higher ratio of completed to attempted suicides because they use more lethal means, are more isolated, and have less physiological reserve. Males complete suicide more often despite attempting less frequently, also due to use of more lethal methods. The second most common cause of death in ages 15-24 is suicide.

**Q:** What is the best predictor of completed suicide?

**A:** A history of previous suicide attempt(s) is the best predictor of completed suicide.

While there is poor correlation between clinical impression of suicide risk and frequency of attempts, a prior attempt remains the single strongest risk factor for eventual completion. The most common psychiatric disorders associated with completed suicide are mood disorders (15% lifetime risk in depression) and alcohol use disorders (15% lifetime risk). Schizophrenia carries a 10-15% risk.

**Q:** What are the epidemiological risk factors for suicide in Canada regarding race/ethnicity and demographics?

**A:** Higher rates in: White individuals and Indigenous peoples in Canada. Widowed/divorced > married. Living alone; no children <8 years old in household. Age: highest completion rates in persons >75 yr; second most common cause of death for ages 15-24.

Indigenous peoples in Canada have significantly elevated suicide rates, particularly among youth, reflecting the ongoing impacts of colonialism, intergenerational trauma, and systemic inequities. This is a critical MCCQE1 topic. Protective factors include social connectedness, cultural identity, and access to culturally safe mental health services.

**Q:** What psychiatric disorders carry the highest lifetime risk of suicide?

**A:** Mood disorders: 15% lifetime risk in depression (higher in bipolar disorder). Alcohol use disorder: 15% lifetime risk. Schizophrenia: 10-15% risk. Eating disorders: 5% lifetime risk.

These are the conditions associated with the highest absolute risk of suicide completion. Mixed drug misuse, panic disorder, and personality disorders (borderline, antisocial) also significantly elevate risk. When assessing a patient with these diagnoses, routine suicide screening is mandatory in every clinical encounter in Canada.

**Q:** What clinical symptoms are specifically associated with elevated suicide risk?

**A:** Hopelessness, anhedonia, insomnia, severe anxiety, impaired concentration, psychomotor agitation, and dissociation.

These symptoms, when present in a patient with suicidal ideation, significantly elevate short-term risk. Hopelessness is particularly important as it predicts intent.
