Insomnia and severe anxiety are modifiable risk factors that can be targeted acutely. Psychomotor agitation increases impulsivity, making access to means particularly dangerous.

**Q:** What is the difference between passive and active suicidal ideation?

**A:** Passive ideation ('death wish'): patient would rather not be alive but has no active plan (e.g., 'I would rather not wake up,' 'I would not mind if a car hit me'). Active ideation: patient actively thinks about killing themselves (e.g., 'I think about killing myself').

Distinguishing passive from active ideation is essential for risk stratification. Even passive ideation warrants thorough assessment. Active ideation, especially with a plan and intent, represents higher risk. The clinician should never avoid asking about suicide — research shows asking does NOT give the idea or incentive to die.

**Q:** What are the key elements to assess when evaluating suicidal ideation?

**A:** Plan, intent, past attempts (number, lethality, outcome), access to means, time and place, provocative factors, protective factors (what keeps them alive), final arrangements, practiced/aborted attempts, ambivalence, and onset/frequency/control over ideation.

A thorough suicidal ideation assessment includes intended lethality ('What do you think would happen?'), access to means ('How will you get a gun?'), and protective factors ('What keeps you alive?'). Ambivalence is important to explore — the fact that a patient came for help often reflects some ambivalence about dying.

**Q:** In Ontario, what form is completed for involuntary psychiatric admission and what form must be given to the patient?

**A:** Form 1 is completed by the physician for involuntary admission. The patient must be given Form 30 to notify them of their admission.

Under the Ontario Mental Health Act, Form 1 (Application for Psychiatric Assessment) allows detention of a person for up to 72 hours for psychiatric assessment. The patient must receive Form 30 (Rights Advice) informing them of their rights. The process varies by province in Canada, making this a jurisdiction-specific MCCQE1 topic.

**Q:** What assessment factors distinguish a high-risk from a low-risk suicide attempt requiring hospitalization?

**A:** HIGH risk (hospitalization strongly considered): active plan + intention to act + access to lethal means + recent social stressors + symptoms of psychiatric disorder. LOW risk: not actively suicidal, no active plan, no access to lethal means.

For low-risk patients, management focuses on: discussing protective factors, promoting survival skills, and addressing underlying problems (psychiatric disorders, psychosocial stressors). For high-risk patients: do not leave patient alone, remove dangerous objects, consider capacity assessment if patient wants to leave. Proper documentation of clinical reasoning is essential.

**Q:** What features of a suicide attempt indicate higher lethality and risk?

**A:** Isolated setting (vs. others present), planned (vs. impulsive), no substance intoxication, brought in by another person (vs. self-presentation), long time lag to ED, expected to die, disappointment at surviving (vs. remorse), and escalation to more lethal means.

Assessment of a suicide attempt must include all these dimensions to properly stratify ongoing risk. Reaction to survival is particularly important: disappointment or self-blame at surviving indicates ongoing intent, whereas guilt/remorse suggests some ambivalence and may indicate lower ongoing risk. Escalating lethality of means across attempts predicts future completion.

**Q:** What are the key considerations when assessing mental status exam thought process versus thought content?

**A:** Thought PROCESS/FORM = HOW the patient thinks (coherence, stream, organization — e.g., goal-directed, circumstantial, tangential, loose associations). Thought CONTENT = WHAT the patient thinks (suicidal/homicidal ideation, delusions, obsessions, preoccupations).

This distinction is fundamental to the MSE. Process abnormalities reflect the form or flow of thinking (e.g., derailment in schizophrenia, flight of ideas in mania). Content abnormalities reflect specific themes (e.g., persecutory delusions, suicidal ideation). Both must be assessed separately and documented in every psychiatric encounter.

**Q:** What is the historical multiaxial model of DSM and what replaced it in DSM-5?

**A:** Historical model had 5 axes: I (psychiatric diagnoses), II (personality disorders/intellectual disability), III (medical conditions), IV (psychosocial/environmental issues), V (GAF 0-100). DSM-5 replaced this by simply listing all relevant psychiatric and medical diagnoses together.

While the multiaxial model is no longer officially used, many clinicians still employ it as an organizing framework for psychiatric assessment. The Global Assessment of Functioning (GAF) scale from Axis V has been replaced in DSM-5 by the WHO Disability Assessment Schedule (WHODAS). Understanding both old and new frameworks is important for MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for diagnosing Panic Disorder?

**A:** A. Recurrent unexpected panic attacks. B. At least one attack followed by ≥1 month of: (1) persistent concern/worry about more attacks or their consequences, OR (2) significant maladaptive change in behaviour related to attacks. C. Not attributable to a substance or medical condition. D. Not better explained by another mental disorder.

Panic disorder has a lifetime prevalence of 5% (one of top 5 reasons to see a family physician in Canada), with M:F ratio 1:2-3. The key distinguishing feature is recurrent UNEXPECTED panic attacks plus at least one month of anticipatory anxiety or behavioural change.

**Q:** What are the first-line pharmacological agents for Panic Disorder, and what are key prescribing considerations?

**A:** First-line: SSRIs (fluoxetine, citalopram, escitalopram, paroxetine, sertraline, fluvoxamine) and SNRIs (venlafaxine XR). Key points: start low/go slow; anxiety disorders often require higher doses and longer duration than depression; full response may take up to 12 weeks; treat for up to 1 year after symptom resolution. Benzodiazepines are 2nd line (short-term, lowest effective dose).

Patients should be warned about expected side effects before initiation to prevent non-adherence. The paradox of SSRIs/SNRIs is that they can initially worsen anxiety, which is why starting low and slow is essential. The 'start low, go slow, aim high' mnemonic is key.

**Q:** What psychological treatment is recommended for Panic Disorder and what does it involve?

**A:** Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT) is the psychological treatment of choice, involving: (1) graduated exposure to unpleasant sensations of arousal (interoceptive exposure) for experiential disconfirmation of fears; (2) cognitive restructuring addressing underlying beliefs about panic attacks; (3) relaxation techniques (visualization, box-breathing); and (4) psychoeducation.

CBT combined with pharmacotherapy can be very effective for panic disorder. The exposure component targets the fear of fear itself (interoceptive conditioning), while cognitive restructuring challenges catastrophic misinterpretations of bodily sensations.

**Q:** What is the prognosis for Panic Disorder?

**A:** 85% can achieve good results; 10-20% continue with significant symptoms. Long-term, 65% achieve remission. Clinical course is chronic but episodic, often waxing and waning with psychosocial stressors.

Panic disorder has a relatively good prognosis with treatment, though a subset of patients remains significantly impaired. Common comorbidities include depression and agoraphobia, which can worsen prognosis.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Agoraphobia?

**A:** A. Marked fear/anxiety about ≥2 of 5 situations: (1) public transportation, (2) open spaces, (3) enclosed places, (4) standing in line/crowd, (5) being outside home alone. B. Fear of being unable to escape or get help if panic-like symptoms occur. C. Situations almost always provoke fear. D. Situations avoided, require companion, or endured with intense anxiety. E. Fear disproportionate to actual danger. F. Persistent, typically ≥6 months. G. Clinically significant distress/impairment. H-I. Not explained by medical condition or other mental disorder.

Important: Agoraphobia is diagnosed irrespective of panic disorder. If criteria for both are met, BOTH diagnoses should be assigned. This is a change from DSM-IV where agoraphobia was a subtype of panic disorder.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD)?

**A:** A. Excessive anxiety/worry occurring more days than not for ≥6 months about multiple events/activities. B. Difficult to control worry. C. ≥3 of 6 symptoms (≥1 day in past 6 months): (1) restlessness/keyed up, (2) easy fatigue, (3) difficulty concentrating/mind going blank, (4) irritability, (5) muscle tension, (6) sleep disturbance. D. Clinically significant distress/impairment. E. Not due to substance/medical condition. F. Not better explained by another mental disorder. Mnemonic: CFRIME (Concentration, Fatigue, Restlessness, Irritability, Muscle tension, Excessive worry).

GAD has a 1-year prevalence of 1-4% and lifetime prevalence of 6%, with M:F = 1:2. In primary care, 70% initially present with physical symptoms as the main concern. Bimodal age of onset: before 20 or middle adulthood.

**Q:** What are the first-line treatments for Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD)?

**A:** Pharmacological first-line: SSRIs (escitalopram, sertraline, paroxetine), SNRIs (venlafaxine XR, duloxetine), pregabalin. Psychological: CBT (cognitive restructuring, muscle relaxation, mindfulness). Lifestyle: avoid caffeine and alcohol, sleep hygiene. Benzodiazepines are 2nd line. Beta-blockers are NOT recommended for GAD.

Pregabalin is a first-line option for GAD (unlike panic disorder where it is not first-line). Beta-blockers are useful only for situational/performance anxiety, not GAD. This distinction is commonly tested.

**Q:** What defines Social Anxiety Disorder and what is its epidemiology?

**A:** Marked and persistent (>6 months) fear of social or performance situations where one is exposed to unfamiliar people or possible scrutiny, with fear of being negatively evaluated (humiliated, embarrassed, or rejected). Situations are avoided or endured with intense anxiety causing significant distress/impairment. Lifetime prevalence: 8-12%; M:F ratio approximately equal.

Social anxiety disorder (formerly social phobia) is distinct from shyness due to the severity of impairment. Unlike many anxiety disorders, the sex ratio is approximately equal. Treatment: CBT, SSRIs, and SNRIs; second-line: benzodiazepine, MAOI, pregabalin.

**Q:** What are the types and diagnostic criteria for Specific Phobias?

**A:** Types: (1) animal/insect, (2) environment (heights, storms), (3) blood/injection/injury, (4) situational (airplane, closed spaces), (5) other (loud noise, clowns). Criteria: marked fear/anxiety about specific object/situation; almost invariably provokes immediate fear (may present as panic attack); actively avoided or endured with intense anxiety; fear out of proportion and persistent (≥6 months); person recognizes it as excessive; significant functional impact. Lifetime prevalence: 10-13%.

Treatment is predominantly psychological (psychoeducation, CBT with in vivo and virtual exposure therapy). Medications play a minimal role in specific phobias, distinguishing them from social anxiety disorder and panic disorder where pharmacotherapy is commonly used.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder (OCD)?

**A:** A. Presence of obsessions (recurrent intrusive thoughts/urges/images causing anxiety + attempts to ignore/suppress/neutralize them) and/or compulsions (repetitive behaviours or mental acts aimed at reducing anxiety but not connected realistically to what they prevent). B. Obsessions/compulsions are time-consuming (>1 h/day) OR cause clinically significant distress/impairment. C. Not attributable to substance/medical condition. D. Not better explained by another mental disorder. Specifiers: with good/fair/poor/absent insight; tic-related.

OCD has a lifetime prevalence of 3%, mean age of onset 20 years. Most common obsessions: contamination, pathological doubt, harm, somatic, symmetry, religious. Most common compulsions: checking, washing, repeating, ordering, counting. Rate in first-degree relatives is higher than general population.

**Q:** What is the pharmacological treatment for OCD, and what are key prescribing considerations?

**A:** First-line: SSRIs (require 12-16 weeks for response, need higher therapeutic doses than for depression, relatively benign side effects); clomipramine (TCA). Adjunctive antipsychotics (risperidone, aripiprazole) for refractory OCD. Neurosurgery (anterior cingulotomy) or neurostimulation for severe refractory OCD (50-70% response rate). ECT for comorbid severe depression.

The delay in OCD response to SSRIs (12-16 weeks vs. 4-6 weeks for depression) and the need for higher doses are clinically important. Clomipramine is as effective as SSRIs but has more side effects. Refractory OCD may warrant neurosurgical intervention.

**Q:** What is Exposure and Response Prevention (ERP) in OCD treatment?

**A:** ERP is the CBT approach for OCD that involves: exposure to feared situations using various techniques (imaginal exposure, systematic desensitization, flooding) COMBINED WITH preventing the compulsive behaviours. Cognitive strategies include challenging underlying beliefs. ERP is the gold-standard psychological treatment for OCD.

The 'response prevention' component is critical: the patient must resist performing the compulsion after exposure, which allows anxiety to naturally habituate. Without response prevention, exposure alone may not be effective as the compulsion provides escape and prevents new learning.

**Q:** What are the key features of Body Dysmorphic Disorder (BDD)?

**A:** Preoccupation with ≥1 perceived flaws in physical appearance not observed by others; repetitive behaviours (mirror checking, excessive grooming, skin picking, reassurance seeking) or mental acts (comparing self to others) related to appearance; causes clinically significant distress or functional impairment. Mean age of onset: 15 years. High rate of suicidal ideation and attempts. Comorbidity with MDD, social anxiety disorder, and OCD. Treatment: SSRIs + CBT specific to BDD.

BDD must be distinguished from eating disorders (where preoccupation centres on weight/shape) and OCD (though they share features). Muscle dysmorphia is a specifier. The high suicide risk makes early recognition and treatment critical.

**Q:** What are the key features of Hoarding Disorder and how is it distinguished from OCD?

**A:** Hoarding: persistent difficulty discarding possessions regardless of value; need to save items; discarding creates distress; results in clutter compromising living areas; causes clinically significant distress/impairment. Onset in teens, worsens over time, more common in older populations, large genetic component. Treatment: CBT specific to hoarding. Distinguished from OCD: hoarding lacks obsessions/compulsions per se; items are valued (ego-syntonic); treatment response to SSRIs is poor.

Rule out: brain injury, cerebrovascular disease, Prader-Willi syndrome, OCD, MDD (low energy), psychotic disorder (delusions), neurocognitive disorder, ASD (restricted interests). The clutter may be reduced with 3rd party intervention but this does not address the underlying disorder.

**Q:** What distinguishes Trichotillomania and Excoriation Disorder, and how are they treated?

**A:** Trichotillomania: recurrent hair pulling causing hair loss (scalp, eyebrows, eyelashes); repeated attempts to stop; causes distress/impairment. Treatment: CBT (habit reversal training), SSRIs, 2nd gen antipsychotics, N-acetylcysteine, lithium. Excoriation: recurrent skin picking causing lesions; repeated attempts to stop; causes distress/impairment. Treatment: similar to trichotillomania. Both: rule out dermatological conditions and body dysmorphic disorder.

Both are classified as OCD-related disorders. Habit reversal training (a specific CBT technique) is the primary psychological treatment for both. N-acetylcysteine (a glutamate modulator) has evidence for both conditions and is unique among psychiatric medications.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for PTSD?

**A:** A. Exposure to actual/threatened death, serious injury, or sexual violence (directly, witnessed, learned about, or repeated extreme exposure). B. ≥1 intrusion symptom: intrusive memories, nightmares, flashbacks, intense distress at cues, physiological reactions to cues. C. ≥1 avoidance: of memories/thoughts/feelings or external reminders. D. ≥2 negative cognition/mood changes: amnesia for trauma, negative beliefs, distorted blame, persistent negative emotion, diminished interest, estrangement, inability for positive emotions. E. ≥2 arousal/reactivity changes: irritability/angry outbursts, recklessness, hypervigilance, exaggerated startle, concentration problems, sleep disturbance. F. >1 month duration. G. Clinically significant distress/impairment. H. Not due to substance/medical condition.

The mnemonic TRAUMA helps: Traumatic event, Re-experience, Unable to function, More than a month, Arousal increased. Specifiers: with dissociative symptoms (depersonalization or derealization), or with delayed expression (full criteria not met until ≥6 months after event).

**Q:** What are the epidemiology and key comorbidities of PTSD in Canada?

**A:** Lifetime prevalence in Canada: 9%; F:M = 2:1. Age of onset: mid-late 20s. Risk factors: severity, duration, and proximity to trauma. Most common trauma forms: unexpected death of someone close, sexual assault, serious illness/injury to someone close, physical assault by partner/caregiver. 75% have another comorbid psychiatric disorder; increased suicide risk 2-3x. DDx includes bipolar disorder, borderline personality disorder, and acute stress disorder (3 days to 1 month post-trauma).

The sex difference in PTSD (women are 2x more likely than men) is partly explained by higher rates of sexual trauma in women, which carries higher PTSD risk per event than combat. The 2-3x increased suicide risk makes PTSD a serious public health concern.

**Q:** What are the three stages of PTSD/trauma recovery and what are the first-line treatments?

**A:** Stage 1 (Safety and Stabilization): emotional regulation techniques, medications, manage substance use. Stage 2 (Remembrance and Mourning): exposure to traumatic memories, working through distorted thoughts/relational patterns/grief. Stage 3 (Reconnection and Integration): exposure therapy, building new future and identity. Psychotherapy: CBT and EMDR are first-line. Pharmacotherapy first-line: fluoxetine, paroxetine, sertraline, venlafaxine XR (50-80% response but residual symptoms common). Prazosin for nightmares. Benzodiazepines used with extreme caution, not recommended acutely post-trauma.

EMDR (Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing) is explicitly recommended as a first-line treatment alongside trauma-focused CBT. Benzodiazepines are potentially harmful in acute post-trauma settings as they may impair fear extinction and increase risk of developing PTSD.

**Q:** How does Acute Stress Disorder differ from PTSD?

**A:** Acute Stress Disorder: symptoms persist for 3 days to 1 month after trauma exposure, similar symptoms to PTSD. PTSD: requires >1 month duration (criterion F). Acute Stress Disorder may be a precursor to PTSD. The key distinguishing feature is duration: <1 month = Acute Stress Disorder; >1 month = PTSD.

This distinction is clinically important for coding and prognosis. Not all patients with Acute Stress Disorder develop PTSD. Early psychological support (not debriefing) is recommended for those exposed to trauma. Critical incident debriefing is no longer recommended as it may worsen outcomes.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for Adjustment Disorder?

**A:** A. Emotional or behavioural symptoms in response to identifiable stressor(s) within 3 months of stressor onset. B. Clinically significant: marked distress in excess of what would be expected, OR significant impairment in social/occupational functioning. C. Stress-related disturbance does not meet criteria for another mental disorder and is not merely an exacerbation of pre-existing mental disorder. D. Symptoms do not represent normal bereavement. E. Once stressor terminates, symptoms do not persist >6 additional months. Specifiers: with depressed mood, anxiety, mixed anxiety/depression, conduct disturbance, mixed disturbance of conduct/emotions, unspecified.

Adjustment disorder prevalence is 2-8%, F:M = 2:1. Stressors can be single, multiple, recurrent, continuous, or developmental. This diagnosis should be considered when symptoms are present but do not meet full criteria for another disorder, and are clearly related to an identifiable stressor.

**Q:** What distinguishes normal grief/bereavement from Major Depressive Disorder (MDD)?

**A:** Symptoms suggesting MDD rather than normal grief: (1) guilt about things other than actions at time of death, (2) thoughts of death other than feeling they should have died with deceased, (3) morbid preoccupation with worthlessness, (4) marked psychomotor retardation, (5) prolonged marked functional impairment, (6) hallucinatory experiences other than hearing the voice/transiently seeing the image of the deceased, (7) dysphoria pervasive and independent of thoughts of deceased (no mood reactivity). After 12 months, persistent yearning/longing → consider persistent complex bereavement disorder. If MDD criteria are met in context of bereavement, MDD is STILL diagnosed.

This is a key change from DSM-IV: the 'bereavement exclusion' for MDD has been removed in DSM-5. If a patient meets full MDD criteria, even during bereavement, they receive an MDD diagnosis. Normal grief should NOT be treated with antidepressants as it is important to allow the mourning process.

**Q:** What are risk factors for poor bereavement outcomes?

**A:** Risk factors for poor bereavement outcome: (1) poor social supports, (2) prior history of depression or psychiatric disorder, (3) lack of preparation for death, (4) highly dependent relationship with deceased, (5) high initial distress, (6) other concurrent stresses and losses, (7) death of a child, (8) traumatic or unexpected loss, (9) history of separation anxiety. Consequence: significant increase in morbidity and mortality acutely following the loss. Loneliness is the most common symptom that continues to persist in normal bereavement.

Bereavement support should include assessment for secondary mental health conditions (PTSD, depression, suicidal ideation, substance use, cardiovascular illness). Grief therapy (individual or group) is indicated for those needing additional support or experiencing complex grief.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Delirium?

**A:** A. Disturbance in attention (reduced ability to direct, focus, sustain, shift attention) AND awareness (reduced orientation to environment). B. Develops over short period (hours to days), represents a change from baseline, and fluctuates in severity. C. Additional disturbance in cognition (memory deficit, disorientation, language, visuospatial ability, or perception). D. Not better explained by pre-existing neurocognitive disorder; not in context of severely reduced arousal (coma). E. Evidence from history/exam/labs that it is a direct physiological consequence of another medical condition, substance intoxication/withdrawal, or toxin.

The Confusion Assessment Method (CAM) is highly sensitive and specific for delirium diagnosis. CAM requires: (1) acute onset and fluctuating course, PLUS (2) inattention, PLUS either (3) disorganized thinking OR (4) altered level of consciousness.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features and subtypes of Delirium?

**A:** Features: (1) disturbance of attention (distractibility, disorientation to time/place/rarely person), (2) sleep/wake disturbance, (3) psychotic-like symptoms (visual hallucinations are organic until proven otherwise, delusions, illusions), (4) affective symptoms (anxiety, fear, depression, irritability, anger, euphoria, apathy), (5) shifts in psychomotor activity. Fluctuation over the course of the day is expected. Subtypes: hyperactive (30%), hypoactive (24%), mixed (46%). Note: collateral history is essential due to fluctuating symptoms.

Visual hallucinations in delirium are a red flag for organic etiology. The hypoactive subtype (lethargic, withdrawn) is commonly missed and has a worse prognosis. Mixed delirium is the most common subtype. Collateral history from nursing staff and family is critical.

**Q:** What are the major risk factors for Delirium?

**A:** Risk factors include: polypharmacy (especially anticholinergics, serotonergic drugs, psychoactive drugs—e.g., Cogentin, Benadryl, benzodiazepines, opioids, corticosteroids); infection; dehydration; malnutrition; immobility; bladder catheters; hospitalization (10-56% incidence); frail/surgical patients; previous delirium; nursing home residents (60% incidence); old age (especially males); severe illness (cancer, AIDS); recent anesthesia/surgery; brain vulnerability (pre-existing neurologic/neurocognitive disorder, substance use disorder, past psychiatric illness).

The mnemonic I WATCH DEATH covers delirium etiologies: Infections, Withdrawal, Acute metabolic, Trauma, CNS pathology, Hypoxia, Deficiencies, Endocrinopathies, Acute vascular, Toxins, Heavy metals. Drug toxicity accounts for approximately 30% of all delirium cases.

**Q:** What is the Confusion Assessment Method (CAM) for diagnosing Delirium?

**A:** CAM requires features 1 AND 2, PLUS either 3 OR 4: (1) Acute onset and fluctuating course, (2) Inattention, (3) Disorganized thinking, (4) Altered level of consciousness. The CAM is highly sensitive and specific for diagnosing delirium. Features favouring delirium over psychosis: visual/tactile hallucinations, acute onset, no previous history of psychosis, cognitive impairment, recent medical illness/medication changes.

The CAM is the most widely validated and used tool for delirium detection in clinical settings. Features favouring psychosis over delirium include: auditory hallucinations that are structured and consistent, personal reference, gradual onset (unless substance-induced), and history of prodrome.

**Q:** What is the management of Delirium?

**A:** Step 1 - Identify/manage underlying cause: identify and treat cause immediately; stop non-essential medications; maintain nutrition/hydration/electrolyte balance; bowel care. Step 2 - Optimize environment: quiet, well-lit room; optimize hearing/vision; protect sleep; orient frequently (calendar, clock); avoid frequent room transfers or staff changes; falls prevention; safe mobility; minimize restraints/lines/catheters; calm supportive approach. Step 3 - Pharmacotherapy: low-dose high-potency antipsychotics (haloperidol 0.5-1 mg IM/IV q1-2h elderly; 0.5-2 mg PO q4-6h). Alternatives: risperidone (less sedating), olanzapine (more sedating), quetiapine (EPS-sensitive), aripiprazole (no QTc prolongation). Benzodiazepines ONLY for alcohol/substance withdrawal delirium.

All neuroleptics prolong QT interval and decrease seizure threshold. Patients with Parkinson's disease or Lewy body dementia are at high risk of EPS. An ECG should be obtained before starting antipsychotics. Antipsychotics treat agitation but do NOT treat the underlying acute brain state.

**Q:** What investigations should be ordered for a patient with suspected Delirium?

**A:** Standard bloodwork: CBC with differential, electrolytes (including Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺, PO₄³⁻), glucose, BUN, creatinine, TSH/T4, LFTs, vitamin B12, folate, albumin, toxicology screen; if indicated: blood cultures, infectious serologies (HIV, VDRL, Hep B/C). Standard imaging: CXR and CT head (especially if focal neurological deficit, acute status change, anticoagulant use, acute incontinence, gait abnormality, history of cancer). Standard urinalysis: urine dip; if indicated: urine drug screen, urine C&S. If indicated: lumbar puncture and EEG (typical finding: generalized slowing).

Drug toxicity accounts for approximately 30% of delirium cases, making medication review essential. EEG is not routinely ordered but can help rule out underlying seizures or postictal states. MRI head is indicated for negative CT when subacute stroke or multifocal inflammatory lesions are suspected.

**Q:** How does Delirium differ from Dementia and Depression-associated cognitive impairment?

**A:** Onset: Delirium = acute (hours-days); Dementia = gradual/stepwise; Depression = subacute. Duration: Delirium = days-weeks; Dementia = months-years; Depression = variable. Level of consciousness: Delirium = fluctuating (hyperactive/hypoactive); Dementia = normal; Depression = normal. Attention: Delirium = impaired; Dementia = not initially affected; Depression = difficulty concentrating. Memory: Delirium = marked recent loss; Dementia = recent then remote loss; Depression = recent. Hallucinations: Delirium = visual (common, frightening/bizarre); Dementia = variable (vacuous/bland); Depression = less common (auditory if present, self-deprecatory).

This triad distinction is highly tested. Key differentiating features: delirium fluctuates (acute, reversible); dementia is progressive (irreversible, gradual); depression is subacute with pervasive depressed mood. Delirium up to 50% 1-year mortality after episode.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for Major Neurocognitive Disorder (Dementia)?

**A:** A. Evidence of significant cognitive decline from previous level in ≥1 cognitive domain (complex attention, executive function, learning/memory, language, perceptual-motor, social cognition) based on: (1) concern of individual, informant, or clinician of significant decline, AND (2) substantial impairment in cognitive performance (preferably by standardized neuropsychological testing). B. Cognitive deficits interfere with independence in everyday activities (at minimum, requiring assistance with complex IADLs). C. Not exclusively in context of delirium. D. Not better explained by another mental disorder.

Mild neurocognitive disorder (MCI) differs from major NCD: deficits do not interfere with independence in everyday activities and impairments are mild-moderate. The MoCA score of 18-25 suggests mild NCD; <18 suggests major NCD. Prevalence: 5% in >65 years, 35-50% in >85 years.

**Q:** What are the '7 As of Dementia' and the most common causes?

**A:** 7 As: (1) Amnesia (memory loss), (2) Aphasia (language loss), (3) Apraxia (loss of ability to carry out learned movements), (4) Agnosia (failure to recognize objects through senses), (5) Anosognosia (not knowing what one does not know), (6) Apathy (loss of initiative), (7) Alteration in personality. Most common causes: Alzheimer's disease (>50%); vascular dementia (~15-30%); Lewy body disease (~25%); frontotemporal degeneration (~5%).

Alzheimer's disease: predominantly memory/learning, insidious onset, gradual progression. Frontotemporal: early language or behavioural changes (apathy/disinhibition), onset often before age 65. Lewy body: early executive/attention changes, well-formed visual hallucinations (e.g., rabbits), autonomic impairment, Parkinson-type EPS not responsive to levodopa. Vascular: executive dysfunction > memory, abrupt onset, stepwise progression.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are used for Alzheimer's Disease?

**A:** To slow AD: (1) Cholinesterase inhibitors (donepezil 5-10 mg once daily, rivastigmine, galantamine) for mild to severe disease. (2) NMDA receptor antagonist (memantine 5 mg once daily to 10 mg BID) for moderate to severe disease. To manage behavioural disturbances: low-dose atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine 2.5-10 mg/d, quetiapine 25-200 mg/d, risperidone 0.25-3 mg/d). Trazodone (25-100 mg) for night-time agitation. 'Start low and go slow' — effective doses can be 1/3 to 1/2 of regular adult doses. Reassess pharmacotherapy every 3 months.

Cholinesterase inhibitors work by increasing acetylcholine availability in areas of cholinergic deficiency. Memantine (NMDA antagonist) addresses glutamate excitotoxicity. Discontinue cognitively impairing medications (anticholinergics, benzodiazepines, Z-drugs). Physicians must inform the Ministry of Transportation about patients unable to drive safely.

**Q:** What is the definition of Substance Use Disorder (SUD) and what are its key components?

**A:** SUD is a neurobiological disorder involving compulsive drug seeking and taking despite adverse consequences, with loss of control over drug use (the '3 Cs': Compulsive use, Consequences, loss of Control). It is possible to have SUD without physiological dependence. Forms of dependence: (1) behavioural (substance-seeking activities, pathological use patterns), (2) physical (physiologic withdrawal effects or tolerance), (3) cognitive (continuous/intermittent cravings to avoid dysphoria or achieve desired effects). Usually chronic with relapsing-remitting course.

DSM-5 identifies 10 substance classes: alcohol, caffeine, cannabis, hallucinogens (PCP), inhalants, opioids, sedatives/hypnotics/anxiolytics, stimulants (amphetamines, cocaine), tobacco, and other. Behavioural addictions (e.g., gambling) are also included. The lifetime prevalence of SUD in Canada is 21.6%.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of Substance Use Disorders in Canada and their relationship with mental health?

**A:** Lifetime prevalence of SUD in Canada: 21.6% (12-month: 10.1%). Alcohol use disorder: 18.1% lifetime, 3.2% 12-month. Cannabis use disorder: 6.8% lifetime, 1.3% 12-month. Other substances: 4.0% lifetime, 0.7% 12-month. 47% of those with SUD have mental health problems. 29% of those with a mental health disorder have SUD. 47% of those with schizophrenia and 25% of those with an anxiety disorder have a SUD.

The bidirectional relationship between SUD and mental health is striking. The very high rate of SUD in schizophrenia (47%) reflects self-medication of psychotic symptoms. Data were collected before cannabis legalization in Canada, so cannabis use disorder rates may be higher now.

**Q:** What is the neurobiological mechanism underlying substance addiction?

**A:** Almost all drugs and activities related to dependence directly or indirectly increase dopamine release from ventral tegmental area (VTA) neurons synapsing onto the nucleus accumbens — known as the brain's 'reward pathway.' This action contributes to rewarding properties of substances. With repeated use, substances modulate signaling pathways that further encourage their use, contributing to addictive potential.

The mesolimbic dopamine pathway (VTA → nucleus accumbens) is the core of the reward system. Different substances activate this pathway through different mechanisms: opioids via disinhibition of dopamine neurons; cocaine/amphetamines via direct dopamine reuptake inhibition; alcohol via GABA and opioid systems. Understanding this is key to designing pharmacological treatments.

**Q:** A 32-year-old woman presents with 8 months of worry about work performance, difficulty controlling her worry, fatigue, muscle tension, and poor sleep. She drinks 3 cups of coffee daily. Her PHQ-9 is 6 (mild). What is the most appropriate first step in management?

**A:** Lifestyle modification first: reduce caffeine intake (a key modifiable factor for GAD) and improve sleep hygiene. Then initiate CBT (cognitive restructuring, relaxation techniques, mindfulness) and, if pharmacotherapy needed, start with an SSRI (escitalopram or sertraline) or SNRI (venlafaxine XR or duloxetine). Beta-blockers are NOT recommended for GAD. Benzodiazepines are 2nd line.

This patient has GAD (>6 months worry, difficult to control, ≥3 associated symptoms, causing impairment). Caffeine exacerbates anxiety and is a key lifestyle factor. The mild depression likely represents comorbidity. Starting with lifestyle modification before medications demonstrates appropriate clinical reasoning.

**Q:** A 70-year-old man on the surgical ward post-hip replacement develops acute confusion, agitation, and visual hallucinations of animals in his room. He is pulling at his IV lines and trying to get out of bed. What is the immediate management?

**A:** 1. Identify and treat underlying cause (rule out infection, electrolyte imbalance, medication toxicity, hypoxia, urinary retention, constipation—common post-surgical delirium causes). Stop non-essential medications. 2. Optimize environment: quiet, well-lit room, family present for reassurance, frequent reorientation, falls prevention. 3. If agitation cannot be managed non-pharmacologically, use low-dose haloperidol 0.5-1 mg IM/IV q1-2h in elderly (monitor QT interval and EPS). Obtain ECG before antipsychotics. Avoid benzodiazepines (worsen delirium unless alcohol/sedative withdrawal).

Post-surgical delirium is very common (especially post-hip fracture surgery). Visual hallucinations are organic until proven otherwise. Antipsychotics treat agitation but NOT the underlying delirium. Physical restraints should be minimized. Do NOT use benzodiazepines for delirium agitation unless there is alcohol or benzodiazepine withdrawal.

**Q:** How does the Mini-Cog (rapid cognitive assessment) work, and what MoCA scores suggest Mild vs. Major Neurocognitive Disorder?

**A:** Mini-Cog: (1) 3-word immediate recall, (2) Clock drawing to '10 past 11', (3) 3-word delayed recall. MoCA scoring: 18-25 = suggestive of mild neurocognitive disorder (MCI); <18 = suggestive of major neurocognitive disorder (dementia). Note: many false positives exist with MoCA — clinical context is important. MoCA is more sensitive than MMSE for detecting mild cognitive impairment.

The Mini-Cog is useful for rapid bedside screening. The clock drawing test (placing hands at 10 past 11) assesses executive function, visuospatial ability, and comprehension. The MoCA was developed at McGill University and is widely used in Canada. Education level affects scores.

**Q:** A patient with a 3-week history of intrusive thoughts about harming her baby, which she finds distressing and attempts to suppress, along with compulsive checking behaviours (2 hours/day) interfering with function. What is the diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Diagnosis: OCD (perinatal/postpartum presentation) — the intrusive harm-related obsessions and checking compulsions meet DSM-5 criteria, with >1 hour/day and functional impairment. Treatment: (1) CBT with ERP (first-line psychological treatment); (2) SSRIs (12-16 weeks for response, higher doses than for depression). Key consideration: distinguish from postpartum psychosis (ego-syntonic command hallucinations to harm baby) — OCD obsessions are ego-dystonic (distressing, unwanted).

Harm obsessions (fear of hurting loved ones) are among the most common obsession types in OCD. They are ego-dystonic: the patient does not want to act on them. This is critically different from postpartum psychosis, where command hallucinations may be ego-syntonic and carry risk of infanticide. Reassurance about the nature of OCD obsessions is an important part of psychoeducation.

**Q:** What are the first-line pharmacological treatments for chronic insomnia disorder according to DSM-5 guidelines?

**A:** Non-benzodiazepines are first-line: 'z-drugs' (e.g., zopiclone), lemborexant, and low-dose doxepin. CBT for insomnia (CBT-I) is also first-line (non-pharmacological).

Canadian guidelines favour CBT-I as the most effective long-term treatment. Z-drugs and dual orexin receptor antagonists (lemborexant) are preferred over benzodiazepines due to lower dependence risk. Low-dose doxepin targets histamine receptors for sleep maintenance.

**Q:** A patient presents with recurrent irresistible sleep attacks, sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by laughter, and vivid hallucinations upon falling asleep. What is the diagnosis and classic tetrad?

**A:** Narcolepsy. Classic tetrad: (1) sleep attacks (irresistible need to sleep), (2) cataplexy (sudden loss of tone evoked by strong emotion, no LOC), (3) hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations, (4) sleep paralysis.

Narcolepsy is caused by loss of orexin (hypocretin)-producing neurons. Cataplexy is pathognomonic — it is REM-related muscle atonia intruding into wakefulness triggered by strong emotions like laughter. Management includes amphetamines, modafinil, and sodium oxybate.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for restless legs syndrome (RLS)?

**A:** Dopamine agonists and benzodiazepines are first-line. Iron should be replaced if ferritin is low. Medications exacerbating symptoms should be modified.

RLS is characterized by an uncomfortable urge to move legs at rest, worse at night, relieved by movement. Low ferritin (even without frank anemia) is a reversible cause. Dopamine agonists (e.g., pramipexole, ropinirole) address the underlying dopaminergic deficit.

**Q:** What distinguishes REM sleep behaviour disorder (RBD) from NREM parasomnias, and what is first-line management?

**A:** RBD: arousal during REM with vocalization/complex motor behaviours; rapid orientation on awakening; associated with neurodegenerative disease. NREM: amnesia for episodes, difficult to arouse. Management: melatonin (first-line), clonazepam; discontinue causative medications (TCAs, SSRIs, SNRIs).

RBD occurs when REM atonia is lost, allowing acting out of dreams. It is a prodrome of alpha-synucleinopathies (Parkinson's, Lewy body dementia, MSA). Unlike sleepwalking, patients with RBD have full orientation on awakening and recall their dreams.

**Q:** How is Gender Dysphoria defined in DSM-5, and what are the three main components of management?

**A:** Gender Dysphoria: marked incongruence between experienced/expressed gender and sex assigned at birth, causing distress. Management: (1) supportive psychotherapy/mental health counselling, (2) hormone therapy (feminizing: estrogen + anti-androgen; masculinizing: testosterone), (3) gender-affirming surgery.

Canadian guidelines emphasize gender-affirming care, beginning with using the patient's preferred pronouns and language. Practitioners should recognize patients as experts in their own experiences. Social and institutional supports (peer groups, documentation for legal name/gender changes) are also important.

**Q:** What is the distinction between a paraphilia and a paraphilic disorder?

**A:** Paraphilia: intense, persistent (≥6 months) sexual arousal from non-normative stimuli — not inherently a disorder. Paraphilic disorder: the paraphilia causes distress/functional impairment to the individual, OR its realization entails harm or risk of harm to others.

DSM-5 separates the presence of atypical sexual arousal (paraphilia) from pathological impact. Paraphilic disorders require clinical intervention; management includes anti-androgens (medroxyprogesterone, leuprolide), high-dose SSRIs (e.g., fluoxetine), behaviour modification, and psychotherapy.

**Q:** List the DSM-5 subtypes of paraphilic disorders.

**A:** Voyeuristic, exhibitionistic, frotteuristic, sexual masochism, sexual sadism, pedophilic, fetishistic, transvestic, other specified/unspecified paraphilic disorder.

Paraphilic disorders tend to begin in childhood/early adolescence, are more common in men (95%), and rarely present via self-referral — usually through legal or interpersonal conflict. Most patients have more than one paraphilia. Disorders tend to be chronic but decrease with age.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Anorexia Nervosa?

**A:** A. Restriction of energy intake leading to significantly low body weight for age/sex/developmental trajectory. B. Intense fear of weight gain OR persistent behaviour interfering with weight gain despite low weight. C. Disturbance in body weight/shape experience, undue influence on self-evaluation, or lack of recognition of seriousness of low weight.

All three criteria (A, B, C) must be met. Specifiers include restricting vs. binge-eating/purging type and severity by BMI: mild ≥17, moderate 16–16.99, severe 15–15.99, extreme <15 kg/m². Importantly, intense fear (Criterion B) can be manifested by behaviours even without expressed fear.

**Q:** What are the criteria for medical ward admission in a patient with Anorexia Nervosa?

**A:** <65% of standard body weight (<85% in adolescents), hypovolemia requiring IV fluids, heart rate <40 bpm, abnormal serum chemistry (low K⁺, Mg²⁺, PO₄³⁻, or high creatinine), or active suicidality.

Psychiatric hospitalization for eating disorders is rarely acute unless there is a concurrent psychiatric emergency. Medical admission thresholds reflect life-threatening physiological compromise. Agreed-upon target weight should be set on admission with reassurance it won't be surpassed.

**Q:** What is refeeding syndrome, what electrolyte abnormality is central, and how is it prevented?

**A:** Refeeding syndrome: life-threatening metabolic response to refeeding in severely malnourished patients causing severe fluid/electrolyte shifts. Central electrolyte: hypophosphatemia. Complications: CHF, cardiac arrhythmias, delirium, death. Prevention: slow refeeding, gradual nutritional increase, supplemental phosphorus, close electrolyte and cardiac monitoring.

Refeeding causes a shift from fat to carbohydrate metabolism, triggering insulin release that drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium intracellularly. Hypophosphatemia is the hallmark. Monitoring and gradual reintroduction of calories (starting low at ~20 kcal/kg/day) is essential.

**Q:** What is the prognosis for Anorexia Nervosa?

**A:** Only ~50% make a full recovery. Adolescent onset has better prognosis than adult onset. With treatment, 70% achieve ≥85% of expected weight; ~57% resume normal menstrual function. Mortality is ~7% (starvation, cardiac/metabolic complications, suicide).

AN has the highest mortality of any psychiatric disorder. Poor prognostic factors include adult onset, chronicity, binge-purge type, comorbid depression or substance use. Eating abnormalities and psychiatric symptoms are common and persistent even with partial recovery.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Bulimia Nervosa?

**A:** A. Recurrent binge-eating (large amount + sense of lack of control). B. Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviours (self-induced vomiting, laxatives, diuretics, fasting, excessive exercise). C. Both occur ≥once/week for 3 months. D. Self-evaluation unduly influenced by body shape/weight. E. Not occurring exclusively during AN episodes.

Severity is based on compensatory behaviour frequency: mild (1–3/week), moderate (4–7/week), severe (8–13/week), extreme (≥14/week). Treatment: SSRIs (fluoxetine has most evidence), CBT, family therapy. Medical admission if significant electrolyte abnormalities present.

**Q:** What physical signs are associated with bulimia nervosa due to self-induced vomiting?

**A:** Russell's sign (knuckle callus), dental enamel loss/caries, parotid gland enlargement, perioral skin irritation, mouth ulcers, periocular and palatal petechiae, metabolic alkalosis with hypokalemia.

Russell's sign (calluses on dorsum of hand/knuckles) results from repeated contact with teeth during self-induced vomiting. Parotid hypertrophy is from repeated vomiting. Electrolyte findings: vomiting causes hyponatremia, hypokalemia, hypochloremia, low H⁺ → metabolic alkalosis. Laxatives cause metabolic acidosis.

**Q:** How does the electrolyte pattern differ between self-induced vomiting and laxative abuse in bulimia nervosa?

**A:** Vomiting: ↓Na⁺, ↓K⁺, ↓Cl⁻, ↓H⁺ → metabolic alkalosis + hypokalemia. Laxatives: ↓Na⁺, ↓K⁺, ↓Cl⁻, ↑H⁺ → metabolic acidosis.

Vomiting loses gastric acid (HCl), causing metabolic alkalosis. Laxative abuse causes loss of bicarbonate-rich intestinal secretions, leading to a non-anion gap metabolic acidosis. Distinguishing these patterns helps identify purging method and guides management.

**Q:** What differentiates Binge-Eating Disorder from Bulimia Nervosa?

**A:** Binge-Eating Disorder: binge eating WITHOUT compensatory behaviours; dieting usually follows binge-eating (in BN, dysfunctional dieting precedes binge-eating). BED: F:M=2:1. BN: binge-eating with compensatory behaviours; self-evaluation unduly influenced by body shape/weight.

Key differential: absence of compensatory behaviours in BED. BED patients often feel disgust/guilt after binges. AN binge-purge type differs by having significantly low body weight. Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder does not involve body image disturbances. BED treatment is primarily CBT.

**Q:** What is Avoidant/Restrictive Food Intake Disorder (ARFID), and how does it differ from Anorexia Nervosa?

**A:** ARFID: feeding/eating disturbance with persistent failure to meet nutritional needs (weight loss, nutritional deficiency, enteral feeding dependence) WITHOUT body image disturbance. Onset typically in infancy. AN: significantly low weight WITH body image disturbance. ARFID not explained by lack of available food or culturally sanctioned practice.

ARFID lacks the fear of weight gain and body image distortion central to AN. It may be driven by sensory sensitivities, fear of choking/vomiting, or lack of interest in food. Risk factors include anxiety disorders and GI conditions. Treatment: psychoeducation, behaviour modification, psychotherapy.

**Q:** What are the general criteria that define a Personality Disorder?

**A:** An enduring pattern of inner experience and behaviour that: (1) deviates markedly from cultural expectations in ≥2 of: cognition, affect, interpersonal functioning, impulse control; (2) is inflexible and pervasive; (3) stable and established by adolescence/early adulthood (not sudden onset); (4) causes significant distress or functional impairment.

Personality disorders are not episodic — their stability over time and early onset distinguish them from Axis I disorders. The mainstay of treatment is psychotherapy (CBT, IPT, psychodynamic, DBT). Pharmacotherapy targets comorbid conditions only. Establishing therapeutic boundaries and showing empathy are key clinical skills.

**Q:** Describe the three Clusters of Personality Disorders and their key characteristics.

**A:** Cluster A ('Mad'): odd, eccentric — paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal; familial association with psychotic disorders. Cluster B ('Bad'): dramatic, emotional, impulsive — borderline, narcissistic, antisocial, histrionic; familial association with mood disorders. Cluster C ('Sad'): anxious, fearful — avoidant, obsessive-compulsive, dependent; familial association with anxiety disorders.

Cluster A uses defense mechanisms of intellectualization, projection, and magical thinking. Cluster B uses denial, acting out, regression, splitting, projective identification. Cluster C uses isolation, avoidance, and hypochondriasis. Personality disorders with familial associations to psychotic disorders: schizotypal, antisocial, borderline.

**Q:** What is the mnemonic for Paranoid Personality Disorder and what are its diagnostic criteria?

**A:** Mnemonic: SUSPECT. Requires 4+ of: Suspicious (exploitation/deception without basis), Unforgiving (bears grudges), Spousal infidelity suspected without justification, Perceives attacks/counterattacks quickly, Enemies or friends (preoccupied with trustworthiness), Confiding feared, Threats interpreted in benign remarks. Must rule out psychotic disorder.

Prevalence 1–4%. Paranoid PD differs from paranoid schizophrenia by lacking true delusions/hallucinations. Patients appear angry and hostile, blame others for problems. They are unlikely to seek treatment voluntarily. Psychotherapy (building trust) is the mainstay.

**Q:** What is the mnemonic for Schizotypal Personality Disorder and what are its key features?

**A:** Mnemonic: ME PECULIAR. Requires 5+ of: Magical thinking, Experiences unusual perceptions (body illusions), Paranoid ideation, Eccentric behaviour/appearance, Constricted/inappropriate affect, Unusual thinking/speech (vague, stereotyped), Lacks close friends, Ideas of reference, Anxiety in social situations (related to paranoid fears, does not diminish with familiarity). Prevalence 4–5%.

Schizotypal PD has a familial association with schizophrenia and may represent a milder form on the schizophrenia spectrum. Unlike schizoid PD, patients are INTERESTED in relationships but have difficulty forming them. Unlike schizophrenia, no true psychosis. Rule out pervasive developmental disorders.

**Q:** How do Schizoid, Schizotypal, and Schizophrenia differ in terms of thought content and social relationships?

**A:** Schizoid: organized thought, no psychosis; SOLITARY with NO desire for relationships. Schizotypal: organized but vague/circumstantial thought, no psychosis (but ideas of reference, paranoid ideation, magical thinking); INTERESTED in relationships but UNABLE to form them. Schizophrenia: disorganized thought, psychosis; socially marginalized but NOT by choice.

Key distinction: schizoid patients don't WANT relationships; schizotypal patients WANT but can't have them; schizophrenia patients are marginalized involuntarily. Schizotypal overlaps most with schizophrenia spectrum. Schizoid has the most restricted/flat affect.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Borderline Personality Disorder (mnemonic IMPULSIVE) and what is the hallmark treatment?

**A:** Mnemonic: IMPULSIVE (5+ required): Impulsive (≥2 self-damaging ways), Mood/affect instability, Paranoia/dissociation under stress, Unstable self-image, Labile intense relationships (idealization/devaluation), Suicidal gestures/self-harm, Inappropriate anger, Voiding abandonment (frantic efforts), Emptiness. Treatment: Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT). 10% suicide rate.

BPD has a strong correlation with childhood sexual abuse and emotionally invalidating environments. Splitting (seeing things as all-good or all-bad) is the hallmark defense mechanism. DBT combines CBT, mindfulness, distress tolerance, and interpersonal effectiveness. More dramatic behaviour tends to diminish with age.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Antisocial Personality Disorder (ASPD) and what age requirement is needed?

**A:** Mnemonic: CORRUPT (3+ required): Cannot conform to law (repeated illegal acts), Obligations ignored (irresponsible), Reckless disregard for safety, Remorseless, Underhanded (deceitful/conning), Planning insufficient (impulsive), Temper (irritable/aggressive). Requirements: patient must be ≥18; evidence of Conduct Disorder before age 15. Prevalence: M 2–4%, F 0.5–1%.

ASPD cannot be diagnosed before age 18, but conduct disorder must be present before age 15. History of trauma/abuse is common. Patients may appear charming initially. Disregard for others' rights is pervasive. Mainstay is psychotherapy; treatment resistance is common.

**Q:** What differentiates Avoidant Personality Disorder from Schizoid Personality Disorder?

**A:** Avoidant PD: WANTS relationships but avoids them due to FEAR of rejection, criticism, embarrassment; hypersensitive to criticism; socially inhibited. Schizoid PD: has NO DESIRE for relationships and genuinely prefers solitude; not driven by fear but by genuine disinterest.

Avoidant PD is Cluster C (anxious/fearful) with familial association with anxiety disorders. Schizoid is Cluster A (odd/eccentric). The key question: is the social withdrawal driven by fear (avoidant) or preference (schizoid)? Avoidant PD also overlaps with social anxiety disorder — avoidant PD is more pervasive and ego-syntonic.

**Q:** How does Obsessive-Compulsive Personality Disorder (OCPD) differ from OCD?

**A:** OCPD: ego-syntonic (consistent with self-image); preoccupation with orderliness/perfectionism/control; obsessional thinking without compulsions, rituals, or anxiety; rigid, inflexible. OCD: ego-dystonic (intrusive, unwanted thoughts); obsessions + compulsions; causes anxiety; patient recognizes thoughts as senseless.

OCPD (Cluster C) features perfectionism that hampers task completion, inability to discard worthless objects, devotion to work excluding leisure, inflexibility in ethics/morality, and reluctance to delegate. Requires 4+ of: SCRIMPER. Highly comorbid with mood/anxiety and eating disorders.

**Q:** What are the key features of Dependent Personality Disorder?

**A:** Pervasive and excessive need to be cared for, excessive fear of separation, clinging/submissive behaviour. Mnemonic RELIANCE (5+ required): Reassurance needed for everyday decisions, Expressing disagreement difficult, Life responsibilities assumed by others, Initiating projects difficult, Alone feels helpless, Nurturance sought excessively, Companionship sought urgently, Exaggerated fears of caring for self. Prevalence 0.5–0.6%.

Management involves setting a regulated treatment schedule (regular, brief visits) and being firm about boundaries between visits. Encourage the patient to engage in own problem-solving. The goal is gradually increasing autonomy and self-efficacy.

**Q:** What are the four attachment styles from the Strange Situation experiment and their associated parenting styles?

**A:** Secure (loving, consistently available caregiver): explores freely, distressed at departure, happy at return. Insecure avoidant (rejecting, unavailable caregiver): ignores caregiver, little emotion. Insecure ambivalent/resistant (inconsistent caregiver): clingy but inconsolable, angry. Disorganized (frightening, dissociated, sexualized caregiver; history of trauma/loss): simultaneous approach/avoidance.

The caretaker's attachment style is the best predictor of their child's attachment style. Separation anxiety is normal between 10–18 months. The Strange Situation stresses the child by removing the caregiver; the child's behaviour during reunion (not separation) is what classifies attachment type.

**Q:** How does Major Depressive Disorder present differently in children versus adults?

**A:** In children: irritable mood may replace depressed mood (key diagnostic difference); insomnia (not hypersomnia); somatic complaints more prominent; irritability, boredom, academic deterioration, social withdrawal. In adolescents: hypersomnia more common; substance misuse. Both: anhedonia, low self-esteem, low motivation.

First-line SSRI in pediatric MDD is fluoxetine; 2nd line: escitalopram, sertraline, citalopram. Close follow-up is essential when starting SSRIs in adolescents to monitor for increased suicidal ideation. Lifetime prevalence: pre-pubertal 1–2% (F:M=1:1); adolescents 4–18% (F:M=2:1).

**Q:** What is Disruptive Mood Dysregulation Disorder (DMDD) and how does it differ from Bipolar Disorder in children?

**A:** DMDD: severe, developmentally inappropriate recurrent temper outbursts ≥3x/week with persistently irritable mood between outbursts; onset before age 10; present ≥12 months; diagnosed between ages 6–18; NO manic/hypomanic episodes. Bipolar: episodic mood changes with mania/hypomania. DMDD supersedes ODD if both criteria are met.

DMDD was introduced in DSM-5 to reduce inappropriate bipolar diagnoses in children. The key distinguishing feature is the ABSENCE of discrete manic/hypomanic episodes and the CHRONIC irritability between outbursts. Comorbidities: ADHD, anxiety, depressive disorders.

**Q:** What is Separation Anxiety Disorder in children and what is its association with school refusal?

**A:** Excessive, developmentally inappropriate anxiety about separation from attachment figures or home; distress for ≥4 weeks; persistent worry about losing attachment figures; reluctance to go places, be alone, or sleep alone; nightmares involving separation. Often associated with school refusal and comorbid MDD.

Normal separation anxiety peaks at 10–18 months. Pathological separation anxiety persists beyond the developmentally expected period or is disproportionate. School refusal is a common presentation. Treatment: CBT, psychoeducation, SSRIs if needed. Distinguish from social anxiety (fear of scrutiny) and general school avoidance.

**Q:** What is Selective Mutism and what are its key diagnostic features?

**A:** Consistent failure to speak in specific social situations where speaking is expected, despite speaking in other situations, for ≥1 month. The disturbance interferes with educational/occupational achievement or social communication. Not due to lack of language knowledge or communication disorder.

Selective mutism is classified as an anxiety disorder in DSM-5. It most commonly presents in early childhood and is associated with social anxiety disorder. Diagnosis requires that the failure to speak is not better explained by a communication disorder (e.g., stuttering) or comfort with another language.

**Q:** What is the HEEADSSS interview and when is it used?

**A:** A structured psychosocial interview for adolescents covering: Home environment, Education/Employment, Eating, Activities, Drugs, Sexuality, Safety, Suicide/depression. Used to assess psychosocial well-being and screen for risk factors in adolescent patients.

HEEADSSS is the standard adolescent psychosocial interview in Canadian practice. It allows confidential exploration of sensitive topics in a systematic way. Starting with less sensitive topics (Home, Education) builds rapport before addressing more sensitive domains (Drugs, Sexuality, Suicide).

**Q:** What are the physiologic complications of AN starvation affecting the cardiovascular system and endocrine system?

**A:** CVS: arrhythmias, CHF (from electrolyte disturbances and muscle wasting), bradycardia (HR <40 bpm can be admission criterion), QTc prolongation, starvation edema. Endocrine: primary or secondary amenorrhea, cold intolerance, decreased T3/T4, increased GH, decreased LH/FSH, decreased estrogen and testosterone.

The athletic triad (disordered eating, osteoporosis, amenorrhea) overlaps with AN. Elevated GH and decreased T3/T4 are adaptive responses to starvation. Cardiac complications (including sudden death from arrhythmia) are the leading cause of death in AN alongside suicide.

**Q:** What skin and musculoskeletal findings are associated with starvation in Anorexia Nervosa?

**A:** Skin: dry skin, lanugo hair (fine downy hair), hair loss/thinning, brittle nails, yellow skin (hypercarotenemia). MSK: osteoporosis secondary to hypogonadism (40% of patients), osteopenia (50%), pedal/periorbital edema from decreased albumin.

Lanugo hair is a characteristic feature of starvation — it represents reversion to a fetal hair pattern as a thermoregulatory response. Yellow skin from high carotene occurs because carotene metabolism is impaired in AN. Bone loss begins early and may be irreversible, making early treatment critical.

**Q:** What lab values are expected in starvation (Anorexia Nervosa)?

**A:** Decreased: RBCs, WBCs, LH, FSH, estrogen, testosterone. Increased: GH, cholesterol. Dehydration: increased BUN. Electrolytes: hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, hypophosphatemia.

Elevated GH in starvation is a paradox — it is an adaptive response to maintain glucose. Elevated cholesterol occurs due to decreased estrogen (which normally promotes hepatic LDL uptake). Leukopenia can mask infections. Electrolyte abnormalities are dangerous and drive admission decisions.

**Q:** What are the good and poor prognostic factors for Bulimia Nervosa?

**A:** Good prognostic factors: onset before age 15, achieving healthy weight within 2 years of treatment. Poor prognostic factors: later age of onset, previous hospitalizations, individual and familial disturbance. Outcomes: 60% good, 30% intermediate, 10% poor. Mortality: ~2% per decade. Relapsing/remitting course.

BN has significantly better prognosis than AN (mortality ~2%/decade vs. ~7% lifetime for AN). The relapsing/remitting nature means long-term follow-up is important. SSRIs (especially fluoxetine at higher doses) and CBT are the mainstays of treatment.

**Q:** What is the SCOFF questionnaire and what does it screen for?

**A:** SCOFF is a 5-item eating disorder screening tool with 78% sensitivity and 88% specificity: Do you make yourself Sick? Do you worry you've lost Control? Have you lost more than One stone (6.35 kg) in 3 months? Do you believe yourself to be Fat when others say you are too thin? Would you say that Food dominates your life?

2+ positive answers suggest a likely eating disorder. SCOFF is a validated screening tool used in primary care in Canada. It does not diagnose specific eating disorders but identifies patients needing further assessment. It covers key features of both AN (Fat, One stone) and BN (Sick, Control).

**Q:** What is the narcissistic personality disorder mnemonic and how does it present clinically?

**A:** Mnemonic: GRANDIOSE (5+ required): Grandiose sense of self-importance, Requires excessive admiration, Arrogant, Needs to be special (and associate with special people), Dreams of success/power/beauty, Interpersonally exploitative, Others (lacks empathy), Sense of entitlement, Envious. Prevalence ~6%. More in males.

Narcissistic PD features a fragile sense of self beneath the grandiose exterior. Patients are often high-functioning professionally but have severe interpersonal difficulties. They are sensitive to perceived criticism (narcissistic injury) and may react with rage. Treatment is challenging due to ego-syntonic nature.

**Q:** What is the treatment for bipolar disorder in adolescents and what makes it challenging?

**A:** Lack of research specific to adolescents — treatment extrapolated from adult guidelines. Pharmacotherapy: mood stabilizers (lithium, anticonvulsants) and/or antipsychotics (risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, aripiprazole). Psychotherapy: CBT and family-focused therapy (psychoeducation + communication skills + problem-solving). Often misdiagnosed in adolescents.

Adolescent bipolar disorder more commonly presents with mixed features and psychotic symptoms than adult-onset. Rapid onset of depression, psychomotor retardation, and affective illness in the family suggest bipolar. Family-focused therapy is specifically designed for bipolar disorder and combines multiple therapeutic components.

**Q:** What are the features distinguishing Histrionic Personality Disorder (mnemonic ACTRESSS)?

**A:** Mnemonic ACTRESSS (5+ required): Appearance used to attract attention, Centre of attention (uncomfortable otherwise), Theatrical, Relationships believed more intimate than they are, Easily influenced, Seductive behaviour, Shallow expression of emotions (rapidly shifting), Speech (impressionistic and vague). Prevalence 2%. Diagnosed more in females but equal prevalence in studies.

Histrionic PD (Cluster B) involves attention-seeking, dramatic, flamboyant behaviour with difficulty forming meaningful relationships. Defense mechanisms: denial, regression. Patients are often sexually inappropriate and easily influenced. Differentiate from borderline PD (which has self-harm, identity disturbance) and narcissistic PD (which focuses on superiority rather than attention-seeking).

**Q:** What is the prognosis and course of childhood anxiety disorders, and when is pharmacotherapy used?

**A:** With treatment, up to 80% will not meet criteria for anxiety disorder at 3-year follow-up, but up to 30% will meet criteria for another psychiatric disorder. Better prognosis: later onset, fewer comorbidities, early treatment, maintained school attendance/peer relationships, absence of social anxiety. Pharmacotherapy: SSRIs if CBT insufficient (meta-analysis shows SSRIs/SNRIs beneficial but benefit is small and disorder-specific; larger effect for anxiety than depression).

The cited meta-analysis (JAMA Psychiatry 2017) found SSRIs/SNRIs more effective than placebo in youth, with larger effect sizes for anxiety disorders (g=0.56) than depressive disorders (g=0.20). However, severe adverse events were significantly more common with active treatment vs. placebo, requiring careful monitoring.

**Q:** What is the athletic triad and which eating disorder is it classically associated with?

**A:** Athletic Triad: disordered eating + osteoporosis + amenorrhea (or reproductive dysfunction). Classically associated with Anorexia Nervosa, particularly the restricting subtype. All three components are interrelated through low energy availability → hormonal dysregulation → bone loss.

The female athlete triad is now termed Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport (RED-S) in Canadian sports medicine guidelines. Low energy availability impairs the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, causing amenorrhea and reduced estrogen, which drives bone loss. Early recognition and treatment are critical to prevent irreversible osteoporosis.

**Q:** What is the SCRIMPER mnemonic for OCPD diagnostic criteria?

**A:** SCRIMPER (4+ required): Stubborn, Cannot discard worthless objects, Rule/detail obsessed (to point activity is lost), Inflexible in morality/ethics/values, Miserly, Perfectionistic to extent it hampers task completion, Excludes leisure due to devotion to work, Reluctant to delegate. Prevalence 2.1–7.9%.

OCPD is ego-syntonic — patients see their perfectionism and rigidity as virtues, not problems. This contrasts with OCD which is ego-dystonic. OCPD is highly comorbid with mood disorders, anxiety disorders, and eating disorders. Psychotherapy is the mainstay; CBT addresses cognitive rigidity.

**Q:** What is the management of circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders?

**A:** Melatonin (primary pharmacological treatment), bright light phototherapy, and modafinil if severe. Behavioural interventions include chronotherapy (gradual shifting of sleep schedule).

Circadian rhythm disorders result from misalignment between the endogenous circadian clock and the desired sleep-wake schedule (e.g., delayed sleep phase, shift work, jet lag). Melatonin administration timing relative to the circadian phase is key: it is most effective when given at the target sleep time in the new schedule.

**Q:** What medications can cause or exacerbate REM sleep behaviour disorder?

**A:** TCAs (tricyclic antidepressants), SSRIs, and SNRIs can cause or worsen REM sleep behaviour disorder. Management includes discontinuing the causative medication if possible, plus melatonin or clonazepam.

These antidepressants suppress REM sleep acutely but upon discontinuation or dose changes, REM rebound with loss of REM atonia can occur. Identification and removal of causative medications is the first step in management of drug-induced RBD.

**Q:** What is the first-line SSRI for adolescent MDD and what monitoring is required?

**A:** First-line: fluoxetine. Second-line: escitalopram, sertraline, citalopram. Monitoring: close follow-up for all adolescents starting SSRIs to monitor for increased suicidal ideation or behaviour (black box warning in Canada for antidepressants in patients <24 years old).

Fluoxetine has the strongest evidence base for pediatric MDD. The black box warning reflects the finding of increased suicidal ideation (not completed suicide) in trials. This does not mean SSRIs should be withheld — untreated depression itself increases suicide risk. Psychotherapy + pharmacotherapy has best evidence for severe depression.

**Q:** What are the common causes of lithium toxicity?

**A:** Overdose, sodium/fluid loss, concurrent medical illness, initiation of NSAIDs, steroids, diuretics, or ACE inhibitors (can cause nephropathy and diabetes insipidus in some patients).

Lithium has a narrow therapeutic index and toxicity can occur even at 'therapeutic' levels. Renal clearance of lithium parallels sodium reabsorption, so anything reducing sodium/volume (dehydration, diuretics, NSAIDs, ACEi) raises lithium levels dangerously.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of lithium toxicity, organized by system?

**A:** GI: severe nausea/vomiting, diarrhea. Cerebellar: ataxia, slurred speech, lack of coordination. Cerebral: drowsiness, myoclonus, tremor, upper motor neuron signs, seizures, delirium, coma.

Cerebellar and cerebral signs are the hallmark of lithium neurotoxicity. The GI symptoms often precede neurological symptoms. Recognition is clinical because toxicity can occur at 'therapeutic' serum levels.

**Q:** When is hemodialysis indicated for lithium toxicity?

**A:** Lithium level >2 mmol/L, coma, shock, severe dehydration, failure to respond to treatment after 24 hours, or clinical deterioration.

Lithium is dialyzable due to its small molecular weight and lack of protein binding. Hemodialysis rapidly removes lithium and is life-saving in severe toxicity. IV saline is first-line for milder cases to restore volume and promote renal lithium excretion.

**Q:** What is the management of lithium toxicity?

**A:** 1) Discontinue lithium for several days and restart at lower dose once levels non-toxic. 2) Monitor serum lithium, creatinine, BUN, electrolytes. 3) IV normal saline. 4) Hemodialysis if Li >2 mmol/L, coma, shock, severe dehydration, failure to respond after 24h, or deterioration.

IV saline restores extracellular volume and promotes renal excretion of lithium. Monitoring renal function is critical as lithium can cause nephropathy. Hemodialysis is the definitive treatment for severe toxicity.

**Q:** What are the indications for anxiolytics (e.g., benzodiazepines)?

**A:** Short-term treatment of anxiety disorders, insomnia, alcohol withdrawal (including delirium tremens), barbiturate withdrawal, akathisia due to antipsychotics, seizure disorders, musculoskeletal disorders, agitation/aggression in acute mania or psychosis.

Anxiolytics decrease anxiety regardless of cause; they can mask or alleviate symptoms. They are generally reserved for short-term use to avoid tolerance and dependence.

**Q:** What are the relative contraindications to anxiolytics?

**A:** Major depression (except as adjunct), history of drug/alcohol misuse, pregnancy/breastfeeding (caution), myasthenia gravis (relative CI for benzodiazepines), risk of falls.

Myasthenia gravis is a specific relative contraindication for benzodiazepines because their muscle relaxant and sedating effects can worsen neuromuscular blockade. Benzodiazepines are CNS depressants and can worsen depression.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of benzodiazepines versus buspirone?

**A:** Benzodiazepines: potentiate binding of GABA to its receptors, resulting in decreased neuronal activity. Buspirone: partial agonist of 5-HT1A receptors.

Benzodiazepines act at the GABA-A receptor chloride channel complex to enhance inhibitory neurotransmission. Buspirone's different mechanism explains why it has no cross-tolerance with benzodiazepines and why its anxiolytic effect is delayed (2 weeks onset).

**Q:** What are the side effects of benzodiazepines?

**A:** CNS: drowsiness, cognitive impairment, reduced motor coordination (falls), memory impairment. Dependence, tolerance, withdrawal. Decreased respiratory drive. Increased risk of MVAs.

All benzodiazepines are sedating and share these side effects regardless of half-life. They are particularly dangerous in the elderly due to risk of falls and cognitive impairment. They potentiate alcohol and other CNS depressants.

**Q:** Describe benzodiazepine withdrawal: symptoms, onset, and management.

**A:** Low-dose withdrawal: tachycardia, HTN, panic, rebound insomnia, anxiety, impaired memory/concentration, perceptual disturbances. High-dose/rapid withdrawal: hyperpyrexia, seizures, death. Onset: 1-2d (short-acting), 2-4d (long-acting). Management: taper slowly; may switch to a long-acting benzodiazepine.

Benzodiazepine withdrawal is similar to but less severe than alcohol withdrawal, but can be fatal with high-dose or abrupt cessation. Abrupt withdrawal from >50 mg diazepam equivalents/day can cause autonomic hyperactivity, seizures, delirium, and arrhythmias.

**Q:** When is benzodiazepine overdose dangerous or potentially fatal?

**A:** When combined with alcohol, other CNS depressants, opioids, or TCAs. Benzodiazepine overdose alone is common but rarely fatal.

The synergistic CNS and respiratory depression from combining benzodiazepines with opioids, alcohol, or other sedatives dramatically increases the risk of fatal respiratory depression. Flumazenil is the benzodiazepine antagonist (Anexate) used for suspected overdose.

**Q:** What is the benzodiazepine antagonist and what is it used for?

**A:** Flumazenil (Anexate) — used for suspected benzodiazepine overdose to reverse sedation.

Flumazenil competitively antagonizes benzodiazepines at the GABA-A receptor. It can precipitate withdrawal seizures in benzodiazepine-dependent patients and has a shorter half-life than most benzodiazepines so re-sedation can occur.

**Q:** What are the advantages of buspirone (Buspar) over benzodiazepines for GAD?

**A:** Non-sedating, no interaction with alcohol, does not alter seizure threshold, not prone to abuse. Primary use: GAD. Onset of action: 2 weeks.

Buspirone's 5-HT1A partial agonist mechanism provides anxiolysis without the dependency, sedation, or cross-tolerance issues of benzodiazepines. The 2-week delay in onset is a clinical consideration — benzodiazepines may be used short-term during this bridge period.

**Q:** Which benzodiazepines undergo no first-pass liver metabolism and are preferred in liver disease?

**A:** Lorazepam (Ativan), oxazepam (Serax), and temazepam — remembered by the mnemonic 'LOT'. Lorazepam and oxazepam are most commonly used for alcohol withdrawal in liver disease.

Most benzodiazepines are hepatically metabolized by oxidation (CYP450), which is impaired in liver disease. LOT drugs are conjugated directly by glucuronidation, which is better preserved. Lorazepam and oxazepam are preferred for alcohol withdrawal in cirrhosis.

**Q:** What are Z-drugs, and how do they compare to benzodiazepines?

**A:** Non-benzodiazepine sedatives for short-term insomnia: zopiclone (Imovane), eszopiclone (Lunesta), zolpidem (Sublinox). Anecdotally provide more restful sleep. Similar side effect profile and dependency risk to benzodiazepines. Should not be used long-term.

Z-drugs act on the same GABA-A receptor as benzodiazepines but with more selectivity. Despite being marketed as safer, they share similar risks of dependence, cognitive impairment, and falls, especially in the elderly.

**Q:** What is the first-line long-acting benzodiazepine for seizure prevention and panic disorder?

**A:** Clonazepam (Rivotril) — dose 0.25-4 mg/d, half-life 18-50 hours. Used for seizure prevention, akathisia, GAD, and panic disorder.

Clonazepam's long half-life provides stable blood levels suitable for seizure prophylaxis and panic disorder. Its high potency and anticonvulsant properties make it distinct from other benzodiazepines.

**Q:** Which short-acting benzodiazepines should generally be avoided and why?

**A:** Alprazolam (Xanax) — should be avoided due to high dependency rate. Temazepam (Restoril) and flurazepam (Dalmane) — should be avoided. Triazolam (Halcion) — shortest half-life, can cause rebound insomnia.

Short-acting benzodiazepines with high potency (like alprazolam) have higher addiction liability and more pronounced rebound anxiety/insomnia between doses. Flurazepam has active metabolites with very long half-life, increasing fall/sedation risk.

**Q:** What is ECT (electroconvulsive therapy) and what is the usual treatment course?
