**A:** ECT is the fastest and most effective acute treatment for depression. It involves induction of a generalized seizure using electrical pulse through scalp electrodes under general anesthesia with muscle relaxant. Usual course: 6-12 treatments, 2-3 per week.

Modern ECT is safe and controlled. General anesthesia and muscle relaxants prevent the physical convulsions seen in early ECT. Unilateral electrode placement causes less memory impairment than bilateral but may be less effective.

**Q:** What are the indications for ECT?

**A:** Treatment-resistant depression (unipolar, bipolar I, bipolar II); depression with psychotic/catatonic features; depression when medications unsafe or rapid response needed (cachexia, severe dehydration, frail elderly, high suicide risk, pregnancy); refractory/severe/life-threatening catatonia; treatment-resistant schizophrenia, acute symptoms, catatonia, NMS history; refractory/severe mania; personal/family history of good ECT response.

ECT is underutilized due to stigma but is highly effective. It is particularly valuable when medications are contraindicated (pregnancy) or when rapid response is essential (severe suicidality, severe dehydration from refusal to eat/drink).

**Q:** What are the adverse effects and contraindications of ECT?

**A:** Adverse effects: anesthesia risk, memory loss (retrograde and/or anterograde, usually resolves by 6-9 months), transient headaches, transient myalgias. No absolute contraindications. Relative: increased ICP, recent (<4 wk) hemorrhagic stroke, recent (<2 wk) MI.

Memory loss is the most concerning side effect for patients. Bilateral ECT causes more memory loss than unilateral. The absence of absolute contraindications means ECT can be considered even in high-risk medical patients when benefits outweigh risks.

**Q:** How does unilateral ECT compare to bilateral ECT?

**A:** Unilateral ECT causes less memory loss than bilateral ECT, but may not be as effective.

Right unilateral ECT (dominant temporal and parietal regions are avoided) reduces cognitive side effects. Bilateral ECT has greater antidepressant efficacy but at the cost of more pronounced and potentially persistent memory impairment.

**Q:** What is repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation (rTMS) and what are its indications?

**A:** rTMS is non-invasive production of focal electrical currents in select brain circuits using magnetic induction. Strong evidence for treatment-resistant depression and pain disorders. Possibly efficacious for anxiety, PTSD, eating disorders, substance use disorders. Not as effective as ECT for depression.

rTMS avoids general anesthesia and systemic side effects of ECT. It is outpatient-based with sessions over several weeks. Common side effects: transient local discomfort, hearing issues, cognitive changes. Rare: seizure, syncope, mania induction.

**Q:** What is deep brain stimulation (DBS) and what psychiatric conditions is it used for?

**A:** DBS involves placement of small electrode leads in specific brain areas to alter neuronal signaling. Most evidence for OCD; some evidence for treatment-resistant MDD. Response rates (>50% symptom reduction) of 40-70%. Still experimental.

DBS is used when all other treatments have failed. The electrodes can be placed in areas such as the nucleus accumbens or subgenual cingulate cortex. It is reversible and adjustable, unlike ablative procedures.

**Q:** What is vagus nerve stimulation (VNS) and when is it used in psychiatry?

**A:** Direct intermittent electrical stimulation of the left cervical vagus nerve via implanted pulse generator. Used for chronic, recurrent MDD with poor response to previous therapy and ECT. Slow onset; approximately 30% response rate at 1 year.

VNS is a last-resort treatment for highly treatment-resistant depression. The left vagus nerve is chosen because right VNS can cause cardiac effects. The slow onset and modest response rate mean it is rarely used but can be valuable in refractory cases.

**Q:** What is phototherapy (light box therapy), and what are its indications and adverse effects?

**A:** Bright light exposure (10,000 lux) for 30 min daily within the first hour of awakening. Indications: seasonal affective disorder (SAD), non-seasonal depression (augmentation), some sleep disorders. Adverse effects: mania induction, reaction with photosensitizing drugs or photosensitive conditions.

Phototherapy acts on the suprachiasmatic nucleus to reverse pathological circadian rhythm alterations associated with SAD. Morning exposure is critical to properly phase-advance the circadian clock. It is a first-line treatment for SAD.

**Q:** What is the role of aerobic exercise in psychiatric illness?

**A:** Moderate-intense aerobic exercise: monotherapy for mild-moderate MDD; adjunctive therapy for moderate-severe MDD. May be helpful in PTSD and schizophrenia. Associated with increased serotonin, phenethylamine, BDNF, endogenous opioids, and cannabinoids acutely, and long-term increases in grey matter.

Exercise is an evidence-based treatment for depression. The neurobiological mechanisms include neuroplasticity (BDNF), monoamine release, and HPA axis regulation. Long-term exercise improves cognition, memory, and stress tolerance.

**Q:** Under the Ontario Mental Health Act, what is Form 1 and what are its key features?

**A:** Form 1: Application by physician to bring a patient to a schedule 1 facility for psychiatric assessment against the patient's will. Any physician can sign. Valid 7 days after examination; hospitalization must occur within 7 days. Expires 72 hours after hospitalization. Patient receives Form 42. No right to review board hearing.

Form 1 is completed when patients are thought to be in imminent danger of harm to themselves (suicide) or others (homicide), or when they are incapable of self-care and suffering from an apparent mental disorder. Form 3 or voluntary admission is the next step.

**Q:** Under the Ontario Mental Health Act, what is Form 2?

**A:** Form 2: Order by Justice of the Peace to bring a patient to a hospital for psychiatric examination against their will. No statutory time restriction on the order; expires 7 days after admission. Based on any combination of the physician's own observations and facts communicated by others. Box A (Serious Harm Test) or Box B completed.

Unlike Form 1 (physician-initiated), Form 2 is issued by a Justice of the Peace. It can be obtained by family members or police when a physician cannot be accessed. The patient is brought to hospital for examination, after which they may be admitted involuntarily or voluntarily.

**Q:** What is Form 3 under the Ontario Mental Health Act?

**A:** Form 3: Certificate of involuntary admission to a schedule 1 facility. Must be signed by a physician other than the one who completed Form 1. Issued before Form 1 expires. Valid 14 days. Patient has right to review board hearing. Patient receives Form 30. Next step: Form 4 or voluntary admission.

Form 3 converts a Form 1 (temporary hold for assessment) into a formal involuntary admission. The physician must assess the patient using the Past/Present Test (current behaviors/threats/attempts) and the Future Test (likelihood of serious harm from the presenting mental disorder).

**Q:** What is Form 4 under the Ontario Mental Health Act, and what are its renewal periods?

**A:** Form 4: Certificate of renewal of involuntary admission. Signed by any physician (usually attending). Must be issued prior to expiration of Form 3. Duration: First renewal 1 month, second renewal 2 months, third renewal 3 months (maximum). Patient has right to review board hearing.

Form 4 is used to continue involuntary admission when the criteria are still met. After three Form 4 renewals, Form 4a (continuation certificate) with mandatory review board hearing is required. The increasing renewal durations reflect the expectation that most patients improve.

**Q:** What are the two criteria boxes (Box A and Box B) for involuntary psychiatric admission in Ontario?

**A:** Box A (Serious Harm Test): Past/Present Test assesses current behaviors/threats/attempts; Future Test assesses likelihood of serious harm from the presenting mental disorder. Box B: Patients with a known mental disorder who are incapable of consenting to treatment (substitute decision-maker needed), have had previous hospitalization, and are at risk of serious harm due to the same mental disorder.

Box B was added to the Ontario Mental Health Act to address 'revolving door' patients who repeatedly decompensate after discharge when they stop treatment. Box A is the primary pathway for acute danger assessments.

**Q:** What is a Community Treatment Order (CTO) in Ontario, and who is it intended for?

**A:** A CTO orders a person with serious mental disorder to receive treatment and supervision in the community. Intended for those who: have a pattern of psychiatric hospital admissions; stop treatment after release leading to destabilization; repeatedly require readmission. CTOs are valid for 6 months unless renewed or terminated.

CTOs address the 'revolving door' phenomenon. They are consent-based (patient or substitute decision-maker must consent) and provide structured community care as a less restrictive alternative to inpatient admission. Violation leads to Form 47 (police bring patient for examination).

**Q:** What are the physician criteria for issuing a Community Treatment Order (CTO) in Ontario?

**A:** 1) Prior psychiatric hospitalization (cumulative >30 days over >2 hospitalizations in past 3 years), or previous CTO in past 3 years. 2) Community treatment plan developed. 3) Physician examination within previous 72 hours. 4) Patient ability to comply assessed. 5) Consultation with rights advisor. 6) Consent of patient or substitute decision-maker.

CTOs are consent-based, distinguishing them from involuntary admission. The 72-hour examination requirement ensures current clinical assessment. The historical hospitalization criterion identifies the target population of revolving-door patients.

**Q:** What are the rights of a person subject to a Community Treatment Order (CTO) in Ontario?

**A:** 1) Right to review by Consent and Capacity Board (with court appeal) each time CTO is issued or renewed. 2) Mandatory review by Consent and Capacity Board every second CTO renewal. 3) Right to request re-examination by issuing physician. 4) Right to review findings of incapacity to consent to treatment. 5) Provisions for rights advice.

These rights ensure that CTOs, while restricting freedom, maintain patient autonomy and legal protections. The consent-based nature means withdrawal of consent can terminate the CTO, though this may trigger Form 47.

**Q:** What happens if a patient violates a Community Treatment Order (CTO) in Ontario?

**A:** The physician can issue a Form 47 — an order for examination that allows police to bring the patient to hospital for examination. Usually the patient is examined and treatment continues as per the CTO.

Form 47 is the enforcement mechanism for CTO violations. The goal is to return the patient to compliance with the community treatment plan rather than automatic inpatient admission. This reflects the CTO's objective of least restrictive care.

**Q:** What did the CATIE trial (NEJM 2005) conclude about first- vs. second-generation antipsychotics for schizophrenia?

**A:** First-generation (perphenazine) and second-generation antipsychotics (olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone, ziprasidone) did not differ significantly in overall effectiveness or benefits. Perphenazine was the most cost-effective. Individual clinical circumstances and patient factors impacted drug effectiveness.

The CATIE trial was a landmark study challenging the assumption that second-generation antipsychotics were superior. It emphasized individualized prescribing and suggested switching medications in poor responders. Perphenazine was excluded from the initial randomization in patients with tardive dyskinesia.

**Q:** What did the STEP-BD trial (NEJM 2007) conclude about antidepressants for bipolar depression?

**A:** Adding standard antidepressants (paroxetine or bupropion) to a mood stabilizer was NOT associated with increased efficacy or increased risk of treatment-emergent affective switch compared to mood stabilizer plus placebo. Recovery rates: 27.3% vs. 23.5% (not significant).

STEP-BD challenged the routine use of antidepressants for bipolar depression. Current guidelines generally recommend against antidepressant monotherapy in bipolar disorder and suggest they should not be the primary treatment added to mood stabilizers for bipolar depression.

**Q:** What did the STAR*D trial (Am J Psychiatry 2006) reveal about treatment-resistant depression?

**A:** Remission rates by step: 36.8% (step 1), 30.6% (step 2), 13.7% (step 3), 13.0% (step 4). Overall cumulative remission: 67%. Patients requiring more steps had higher relapse rates. Conclusion: Patients with persistent MDD benefit from successive treatment steps, but remission decreases with additional strategies.

STAR*D established the benchmark remission rates for MDD treatment in primary and specialty care. It demonstrated that persistence in treating MDD is worthwhile but that early remission predicts better long-term outcomes.

**Q:** What did the TADS trial (JAMA 2004) show about treatment of adolescent depression?

**A:** Combination of fluoxetine + CBT was most effective (71% response rate; significantly superior to placebo P=0.001). Fluoxetine alone > CBT alone (P=0.01). Combination > fluoxetine alone (P=0.02). Combination offered the most favorable benefit-risk tradeoff for adolescents with MDD.

TADS established that combined pharmacotherapy and psychotherapy is superior to either alone for adolescent MDD. The addition of CBT may help mitigate suicidality concerns associated with SSRI use in youth, providing a better safety profile.

**Q:** What did the TRANSFORM-2 trial (Am J Psychiatry 2019) demonstrate about esketamine for treatment-resistant depression?

**A:** Esketamine nasal spray plus a newly initiated oral antidepressant produced significantly greater improvement in MADRS scores at day 28 compared to placebo nasal spray plus antidepressant. Clinically meaningful improvements appeared earlier in the esketamine group. Conclusion: esketamine nasal spray is a safe, rapid-acting, efficacious therapy for treatment-resistant depression.

Esketamine (Spravato) is an intranasal NMDA receptor antagonist approved for treatment-resistant depression. Its rapid onset (hours to days) distinguishes it from traditional antidepressants. It requires supervised administration due to dissociation risk.

**Q:** What did the COMBINE trial (JAMA 2006) show about treatment of alcohol dependence?

**A:** Naltrexone (100mg/d) with medical management improved drinking outcomes. Combined behavioral intervention (CBI) with medical management also improved outcomes. Acamprosate showed NO significant effect vs. placebo, alone or in combination. No combination produced better efficacy than naltrexone or CBI alone with medical management.

The COMBINE trial supports naltrexone as the pharmacological treatment of choice for alcohol dependence (in addition to behavioral interventions). It importantly showed acamprosate was not effective in this US population, though European trials have shown some benefit.

**Q:** What is the difference between a Schedule 1 psychiatric facility and regular hospital admission under the Ontario Mental Health Act?

**A:** Schedule 1 facilities are designated hospitals able to provide intensive inpatient and outpatient psychiatric care. Involuntary psychiatric forms (Forms 1, 3, 4) specifically authorize admission to Schedule 1 facilities.

Not all hospitals are Schedule 1 facilities. This distinction is important because the Mental Health Act powers (involuntary admission, community treatment orders) only apply to patients being admitted to designated Schedule 1 facilities.

**Q:** What is Form 33 under the Ontario Mental Health Act, and what does it communicate?

**A:** Form 33: Notice to patient that the patient is incapable of consenting to treatment of a mental disorder, and/or management of property, and/or disclosure of health information. Issued by the attending physician. Original to patient, notice to rights advisor, copy to chart.

Form 33 triggers substitute decision-making processes. It separates capacity to consent to psychiatric treatment from involuntary admission status — a patient can be voluntary but incapable of consenting to specific treatments.

**Q:** What is Form 42 under the Ontario Mental Health Act?

**A:** Form 42: Notice to the patient that the patient is now on a Form 1 and the reason for this change. Signed by the physician completing Form 1. Original to patient, copy to chart. No right to review board hearing.

Form 42 ensures the patient is informed of their Form 1 status and the reason for the involuntary hold. Informed notification is a fundamental right even for involuntary patients.

**Q:** Which antidepressant combination is most effective for adolescents with MDD according to the TADS trial?

**A:** Fluoxetine combined with cognitive-behavioral therapy (CBT) — 71% response rate, superior to either alone and to placebo.

For adolescent MDD, combined pharmacotherapy and psychotherapy is the evidence-based standard of care. The TADS trial specifically found fluoxetine + CBT superior to fluoxetine alone (P=0.02) and CBT alone (P=0.01). Fluoxetine is the preferred SSRI in pediatric populations.

**Q:** What is the cumulative remission rate in MDD patients who go through all four treatment steps in STAR*D?

**A:** Approximately 67% overall cumulative remission rate. However, patients requiring more steps had higher relapse rates during follow-up.

STAR*D showed that while most patients will eventually achieve remission with sequential treatment steps, those requiring more steps have worse long-term outcomes. This supports early aggressive treatment to achieve remission at the first treatment step.

**Q:** What is magnetic seizure therapy (MST) and how does it compare to ECT?

**A:** MST uses strong magnetic current to induce generalized seizures. Early studies show efficacy for depression and anxiety with reduced memory effects compared to ECT. It remains experimental.

MST combines the therapeutic seizure mechanism of ECT with the focality of TMS. By inducing seizures through magnetic rather than electrical stimulation, MST may achieve therapeutic benefit while reducing cognitive side effects, though it remains under investigation.

**Q:** What are ablative/lesion procedures in neurosurgery for psychiatry, and when are they used?

**A:** Used for MDD or OCD unresponsive to all other treatment forms. Efficacy: 25-75% depending on procedure. Adverse effects related to lesion location/size, with high suicide risk in non-responders. Focused ultrasound therapy (FUS) is an experimental alternative avoiding open skull surgery.

Ablative procedures (e.g., anterior cingulotomy, capsulotomy) are last-resort treatments. They are irreversible, distinguishing them from DBS. The high suicide risk in non-responders underscores the need for careful patient selection and post-operative monitoring.

## Psychiatry Suicide

**Q:** What are the key components of a safety plan for a patient with suicidal ideation?

**A:** A safety plan should include: (1) agreement not to harm themselves, (2) avoid alcohol, drugs, and triggering situations, (3) follow-up at a designated time, (4) contact a healthcare worker, crisis line, or go to the ED if unsafe or if suicidal feelings return/intensify.

Safety planning is a cornerstone of suicide risk management in Canadian practice. It is a collaborative, structured intervention that identifies warning signs, coping strategies, social supports, and professional resources. The goal is to create a personalized action plan the patient agrees to follow when suicidal urges escalate.

**Q:** What is the recommended management approach for a suicidal patient with alcohol or substance use disorder?

**A:** Suicidality usually resolves with abstinence for a few days. If suicidality persists after a few days of abstinence, suspect underlying depression and treat accordingly.

Substance intoxication and withdrawal can precipitate or amplify suicidal ideation. In Canadian emergency settings, it is important to reassess suicidal risk after the patient has become sober before committing to a psychiatric admission solely for substance-related suicidality.

## Psychiatry Psychosis

**Q:** What defines a psychotic disorder and what are the two major symptom categories?

**A:** Psychotic disorders are characterized by significant impairment in reality testing. The two major categories are: (1) Positive symptoms — delusions, hallucinations, grossly disorganized/abnormal motor behaviour, formal thought disorder; (2) Negative symptoms — diminished emotional expression (affective flattening), anhedonia, avolition, alogia, asociality.

Understanding the positive/negative symptom dichotomy is critical for MCCQE1. Positive symptoms are typically more responsive to antipsychotic medications, while negative symptoms are more treatment-resistant and more closely associated with functional impairment.

**Q:** What is the key differentiating feature that distinguishes Brief Psychotic Disorder, Schizophreniform Disorder, and Schizophrenia?

**A:** Duration of illness: Brief Psychotic Disorder lasts <1 month (with full return to premorbid functioning), Schizophreniform Disorder lasts >1 month but <6 months, Schizophrenia lasts >6 months.

Duration of untreated psychosis is a key factor in prognosis. All three disorders require at least one Criterion A positive symptom from schizophrenia criteria. The temporal boundaries are frequently tested on the MCCQE1.

**Q:** What investigations should be ordered for a first-episode psychosis workup?

**A:** Routine: CBC, electrolytes (including extended electrolytes), creatinine, glucose, urinalysis, urine drug screen, TSH, vitamin B12. Baseline before antipsychotic initiation: LFTs, fasting lipids, HbA1C. Optional: ECG (antipsychotics affect cardiac conduction). As clinically indicated: infectious workup, inflammatory markers, brain imaging.

A thorough medical workup is essential to rule out organic causes of psychosis (e.g., thyroid disease, metabolic derangements, substance intoxication, CNS pathology) before labeling a first episode as a primary psychiatric disorder.

**Q:** What is Schizophreniform Disorder and how does it differ from schizophrenia in terms of diagnosis and prognosis?

**A:** Schizophreniform Disorder meets criteria A, D, and E of schizophrenia; episode lasts >1 month but <6 months (symptoms extending >6 months → reclassify as schizophrenia). Prognosis is better than schizophrenia: 1/3 recover within 6 months, 2/3 progress to schizophrenia.

Schizophreniform disorder is conceptually a 'provisional' diagnosis that may resolve or evolve into schizophrenia. Good prognostic features include acute onset, confusion/perplexity at peak illness, good premorbid functioning, and absence of blunted/flat affect.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria and prognosis of Brief Psychotic Disorder?

**A:** Criteria: Criterion A1–A4, D, and E of schizophrenia are met; episode lasts ≥1 day but <1 month with eventual full return to premorbid function. Can occur after a stressful event or postpartum. Treatment: secure/safe environment, antipsychotics, anxiolytics. Prognosis: good, self-limiting, return to premorbid function within 1 month.

Brief Psychotic Disorder is a time-limited condition by definition. The postpartum specifier is important to differentiate from postpartum depression (which is predominantly a mood disorder) and postpartum psychosis (which may meet criteria for Brief Psychotic Disorder or Bipolar I).

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for Schizoaffective Disorder?

**A:** A. Uninterrupted period of illness with a major mood episode (MDE or manic) concurrent with Criterion A of schizophrenia. B. Delusions or hallucinations for ≥2 weeks in the absence of a major mood episode during the lifetime of the illness. C. Symptoms meeting criteria for a major mood episode are present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual illness. D. Not attributable to substances or medical condition. Specifiers: bipolar type or depressive type.

Schizoaffective disorder lies between schizophrenia and mood disorders. Criterion B (at least 2 weeks of psychosis without mood symptoms) distinguishes it from mood disorder with psychotic features, while Criterion C (mood symptoms predominant overall) distinguishes it from schizophrenia.

**Q:** How does schizoaffective disorder differ from schizophrenia and mood disorder with psychotic features in terms of diagnosis?

**A:** Schizoaffective: psychosis present for ≥2 weeks WITHOUT mood symptoms (B), AND mood symptoms present for the MAJORITY of total illness duration (C). Mood disorder with psychotic features: psychosis occurs ONLY during mood episodes. Schizophrenia: mood episodes are brief or rare relative to psychotic symptoms.

The key diagnostic challenge is determining which symptom cluster (psychosis vs. mood) predominates and whether psychosis exists independently of mood disturbance. This distinction guides treatment (antipsychotics + mood stabilizers/antidepressants for schizoaffective vs. predominantly antipsychotics for schizophrenia).

**Q:** What is the epidemiology, treatment, and prognosis of Schizoaffective Disorder?

**A:** Epidemiology: 1/3 as prevalent as schizophrenia; bipolar type more common in young adults, depressive type in older adults; lifetime suicide risk 5%. Treatment: antipsychotics + mood stabilizers + antidepressants. Prognosis: between that of schizophrenia and mood disorder.

The depressive subtype of schizoaffective disorder carries elevated suicide risk, similar to major depressive disorder with psychotic features. Treatment must address both psychotic and mood symptom domains simultaneously.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Delusional Disorder?

**A:** A. ≥1 delusion with duration ≥1 month. B. Criterion A of schizophrenia has never been met (hallucinations, if present, are not prominent and are related to the delusional theme). C. Functioning is not markedly impaired apart from the delusion's impact; behaviour is not obviously bizarre. D. Any mood episodes have been brief relative to the delusional periods. E. Not attributable to substances or another medical condition. Subtypes: erotomanic, grandiose, jealous, persecutory, somatic, mixed, unspecified.

The key distinguishing feature of delusional disorder is relatively preserved functioning outside the delusional domain. The non-bizarre vs. bizarre delusion distinction (situations that could vs. could not occur in real life) helps differentiate from schizophrenia.

**Q:** What is the treatment and prognosis of Delusional Disorder?

**A:** Treatment: antipsychotics, psychotherapy, and antidepressants. Prognosis: may respond to antipsychotics, but most patients refuse treatment and have a chronic, unremitting course. Some maintain high level of functioning; some progress to schizophrenia.

Poor insight is characteristic of delusional disorder — patients typically do not believe they are ill and therefore refuse antipsychotics. Therapeutic alliance and indirect approaches (treating comorbid depression or anxiety) may improve engagement.

**Q:** What is the difference between bizarre and non-bizarre delusions?

**A:** Non-bizarre delusions involve situations that could plausibly occur in real life (e.g., being followed, poisoned, loved at a distance from afar). Bizarre delusions involve situations that could not possibly occur in real life (e.g., being kidnapped by aliens, having one's organs stolen).

This distinction is clinically relevant in differentiating Delusional Disorder (which can include non-bizarre delusions) from Schizophrenia (which often includes bizarre delusions). Bizarre delusions automatically satisfy one Criterion A symptom of schizophrenia.

## Psychiatry Schizophrenia

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 Criterion A symptoms for schizophrenia, and how many are required?

**A:** At least 2 of the following must be present for a significant portion of 1 month, and at least one must be (1), (2), or (3): (1) delusions, (2) hallucinations, (3) disorganized speech, (4) grossly disorganized or catatonic behaviour, (5) negative symptoms (diminished emotional expression or avolition).

The requirement that at least one symptom must be a 'core' symptom (delusions, hallucinations, or disorganized speech) means negative symptoms alone are insufficient for Criterion A. This distinguishes schizophrenia from disorders that may present predominantly with negative features.

**Q:** What are the epidemiological features of schizophrenia (prevalence, sex ratio, age of onset, suicide risk)?

**A:** Prevalence: 0.3–0.7%; Sex ratio: M:F = 1:1; Age of onset: females late-20s (with 2nd peak in mid-life), males early- to mid-20s; Suicide risk: 5–6% die by suicide, 20% attempt suicide.

Schizophrenia affects men and women equally but the age of onset differs — men typically develop schizophrenia earlier. The lifetime suicide risk is substantially elevated, making suicide risk assessment a critical ongoing clinical task.

**Q:** What is the 'dopamine hypothesis' of schizophrenia?

**A:** Excess dopamine activity in the mesolimbic pathway mediates positive symptoms of psychosis (delusions, hallucinations), while decreased dopamine in the prefrontal cortex mediates negative and cognitive symptoms. GABA, glutamate, and ACh dysfunction are also implicated.

The dopamine hypothesis underpins antipsychotic pharmacotherapy — D2 receptor blockade reduces positive symptoms. However, the insufficiency of this model to explain negative symptoms has led to interest in glutamate (NMDA receptor) and other neurotransmitter theories.

**Q:** What are the genetic risk factors for schizophrenia?

**A:** Monozygotic twin concordance: 40%; Dizygotic twins/siblings/children of one affected parent: 10%; If both parents have schizophrenia: 46% risk. Vulnerable genes include: DISC1, NRG1, DTNBP1 (dysbindin), COMT, DAOA, GRM3, BDNF.

The genetics of schizophrenia are complex and polygenic. The MZ concordance rate of only 40% (not 100%) demonstrates that environmental factors also play a significant role in disease expression.

**Q:** What environmental risk factors are associated with schizophrenia?

**A:** Cannabis use, geographic variance, winter season of birth, obstetrical complications, prenatal viral exposure.

Cannabis use is associated with a 2.7-year earlier onset of psychosis in users versus nonusers (meta-analysis, Arch Gen Psychiatry 2011). These environmental factors interact with genetic vulnerability to precipitate illness.

**Q:** What catatonic behaviours may be seen in schizophrenia?

**A:** Catatonic stupor (fully conscious but mute and immobile, may hold bizarre positions), catatonic excitement (uncontrolled aimless motor activity, extreme agitation), stereotypy (repeated non-goal-directed movements), mannerisms (odd goal-directed activities, e.g., grimacing), echopraxia (imitates others' movements), automatic obedience (robot-like execution of commands), negativism (refuses simple requests for no apparent reason), inappropriate affect, neglect of self-care.

Catatonia is a specifier applicable to many psychiatric disorders including schizophrenia, mood disorders, and medical conditions. Recognizing catatonic features is important because lorazepam (benzodiazepine) is first-line treatment.

**Q:** What is the management of schizophrenia (biological and psychosocial components)?

**A:** Biological: Antipsychotics (risperidone, aripiprazole, haloperidol, paliperidone; clozapine if treatment-resistant); long-acting injectables (LAI/depot) more effective for reducing relapse. Adjunctive: mood stabilizers (lithium, valproate, carbamazepine), anxiolytics, ECT. Maintenance: ≥2 yr after first episode, ≥5 yr after multiple episodes. Psychosocial: CBT, ACT (Assertive Community Treatment), social skills training, employment programs, housing support.

Long-acting injectable antipsychotics are particularly beneficial for patients with poor medication adherence, which is common in schizophrenia. Clozapine remains the gold standard for treatment-resistant schizophrenia but requires regular CBC monitoring due to risk of agranulocytosis.

**Q:** What is the long-term prognosis of schizophrenia (rule of thirds)?

**A:** Over time: 1/3 improve, 1/3 remain the same, 1/3 worsen. Positive symptoms typically diminish with treatment; negative symptoms are most persistent; cognitive symptoms may not improve.

The rule of thirds provides a useful framework for counselling patients and families about expected outcomes. The chronicity of negative and cognitive symptoms underscores the importance of psychosocial rehabilitation alongside pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** What is the relationship between Duration of Untreated Psychosis (DUP) and outcomes in schizophrenia?

**A:** Longer DUP is associated with: poorer response to antipsychotic treatment, lower likelihood of remission, poorer social functioning and global outcomes. Shorter DUP is associated with: greater response to antipsychotics, better global and social functioning, improved negative symptoms. Longer DUP was NOT associated with employment, quality of life, or hospital treatment outcomes.

DUP is considered a potentially modifiable prognostic factor, which has driven early psychosis intervention programs in Canada (e.g., PEPP, CAMH early psychosis programs). Early identification and treatment can meaningfully improve long-term outcomes.

**Q:** What is the risk of developing psychosis for first-degree relatives of someone with schizophrenia, and how does parental history change this risk?

**A:** Children/siblings/DZ twins with ONE parent affected: ~10% risk. Both parents have schizophrenia: ~46% risk. MZ twins: ~40% concordance. This demonstrates both genetic loading and the non-purely-genetic nature of schizophrenia (100% concordance not achieved in MZ twins).

Genetic counselling for relatives of patients with schizophrenia is important. The incomplete MZ concordance strongly implicates gene-environment interactions. This also supports the value of targeted early intervention in high-risk populations.

## Psychiatry Mood Disorders

**Q:** What is the DSM-5 mnemonic for Major Depressive Episode (MDE) symptoms and how many are required?

**A:** Mnemonic: MSIGECAPS — Mood (depressed), Sleep (increased/decreased), Interest (decreased/anhedonia), Guilt (worthlessness), Energy (fatigue), Concentration (decreased), Appetite (increased/decreased), Psychomotor (agitation/retardation), Suicidality. ≥5 of 9 symptoms for ≥2 weeks, with at least one being depressed mood or anhedonia.

The MDE criteria require functional impairment (Criterion B) and exclusion of substance/medical causes (Criterion C). The key anchor symptoms are depressed mood and anhedonia — at least one must be present among the ≥5.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 criteria for a Manic Episode and its mnemonic?

**A:** Mnemonic: DIG FAST — Distractibility, Indiscretion (pleasurable activities with painful consequences), Grandiosity, Flight of ideas, Activity (increased goal-directed activity), Sleep (decreased need), Talkativeness (pressure of speech). Criterion A: abnormally and persistently elevated/expansive/irritable mood AND increased goal-directed activity/energy for ≥1 week (or any duration if hospitalization needed). Criterion B: ≥3 of the above (≥4 if mood only irritable). Criterion C: marked impairment, hospitalization, or psychotic features.

A full manic episode that emerges during antidepressant treatment but persists beyond the physiological effect of that treatment counts as a manic episode and warrants a Bipolar I diagnosis. The 'irritable only' criterion requiring 4 symptoms (vs. 3 if elevated/expansive) is frequently tested.

**Q:** How does a Hypomanic Episode differ from a Manic Episode (DSM-5)?

**A:** Both share the same Criterion A and B symptoms. Key differences: (1) Duration: hypomania lasts ≥4 days (mania ≥1 week); (2) Severity: hypomania does NOT cause marked impairment, does NOT require hospitalization; (3) No psychotic features (if present, by definition it is a manic episode).

Distinguishing hypomania from mania is critical for the Bipolar I vs. II distinction. Patients with hypomania may not recognize it as abnormal (it can feel productive), leading to underreporting and delayed/missed diagnosis of Bipolar II.

**Q:** What is a Mixed Features specifier and what is its clinical significance?

**A:** Mixed features is a specifier applied to a manic, hypomanic, or depressive episode indicating concurrent depressive AND manic symptoms. During a full MDE: ≥3 Criterion B manic symptoms present on most days. During full manic/hypomanic episode: ≥3 Criterion A depressive symptoms on most days. Clinical significance: increased suicide risk, appropriate treatment is critical, and its presence in an MDD patient raises suspicion for Bipolar Disorder.

Monotherapy with antidepressants should be avoided in mixed states as it can precipitate or worsen manic symptoms. Mood stabilizers (lithium, divalproex) combined with SGAs (lurasidone, aripiprazole) are preferred for mixed episodes.

**Q:** What is the Canadian epidemiology of Major Depressive Disorder (MDD)?

**A:** Annual prevalence: 5%; lifetime prevalence: 11%; peak prevalence age 15–25 years; female:male ratio = 2:1.

MDD is one of the leading causes of disability in Canada. The female predominance may be related to hormonal, psychological, and sociocultural factors. The peak onset in young adults makes it a major public health concern.

**Q:** What are the biological etiological factors for Major Depressive Disorder (MDD)?

**A:** Genetic: 65–75% MZ twins; 14–19% DZ twins; 2–4 fold increased risk in first-degree relatives. Neurotransmitter: decreased 5-HT, NE, DA; changes in GABA and glutamate. Neuroendocrine: abnormal HPA axis activity. Neuroanatomy: decreased hippocampal volume, increased ventricles, decreased REM latency, increased REM length. Immunologic: increased pro-inflammatory cytokines (IL-6, TNF).

The monoamine hypothesis (decreased 5-HT/NE/DA) underpins traditional antidepressant pharmacotherapy. The role of inflammation and HPA axis dysregulation has spurred interest in novel treatments targeting these pathways.

**Q:** What is the treatment algorithm for Major Depressive Disorder (MDD)?

**A:** Mild: lifestyle (supervised exercise, light therapy for seasonal pattern), psychotherapy. Moderate-severe: 1st line pharmacotherapy — 2nd generation antidepressants (escitalopram, mirtazapine, sertraline, venlafaxine, agomelatine, citalopram show superiority). Non/partial response: optimize dose, switch to superior antidepressant, OR augment (aripiprazole, quetiapine, risperidone). Response timeline: physical symptoms improve at 2 weeks, mood/cognition by 4 weeks. ECT: fastest and most effective for severe/psychotic/treatment-resistant MDD. TMS: 1st line after ≥1 antidepressant failure.

Per Canadian guidelines (CANMAT), adequate antidepressant trials require therapeutic dose for at least 4–6 weeks. TMS provides a non-invasive alternative to ECT with fewer cognitive side effects. ECT remains the gold standard for severe or life-threatening depression.

**Q:** What are the features of depression in the elderly?

**A:** Affects ~15% of community residents >65, up to 50% in nursing homes. High suicide risk (social isolation, chronic illness, decreased independence). Suicide peaks: males ages 80–90, females ages 50–65. Low mood may not be reliable indicator in those >70. Often presents with somatic complaints (weight/sleep/energy changes, chronic pain, anxiety). May have prominent cognitive changes after mood symptom onset (dementia syndrome of depression).

Late-onset depression in the elderly requires careful distinction from dementia. The 'dementia syndrome of depression' (pseudodementia) typically features acute onset, the patient complaining of memory loss, and improvement of cognition with antidepressant treatment — unlike true dementia.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Persistent Depressive Disorder (Dysthymia)?

**A:** A. Depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for ≥2 years (≥1 year in children/adolescents). B. ≥2 of: poor appetite or overeating, insomnia or hypersomnia, low energy/fatigue, low self-esteem, poor concentration/indecisiveness, hopelessness. C. Never without symptoms for >2 months during 2-year period. D–H: No manic/hypomanic episodes; not better explained by psychotic disorder; not due to substances/GMC; causes significant distress/impairment.

Persistent depressive disorder replaced the older terms 'dysthymia' and 'chronic MDD' in DSM-5. 'Double depression' refers to a full MDE superimposed on Persistent Depressive Disorder — these patients have worse outcomes than either condition alone.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and treatment of Persistent Depressive Disorder?

**A:** Epidemiology: lifetime prevalence 2–3%; M = F. Treatment: Psychological — psychotherapy is principal treatment (historically); pharmacological treatment has some (but inferior) benefit; combination may be most efficacious. Biological — SSRIs (sertraline, escitalopram), TCAs (nortriptyline).

Unlike MDD where medications are a mainstay, Persistent Depressive Disorder historically emphasized psychotherapy. However, evidence supports SSRIs as effective agents. The long-term nature of the condition requires sustained treatment engagement.

**Q:** What is Postpartum 'Blues' and how does it differ from Postpartum Depression?

**A:** Postpartum Blues: Transient mild depression, mood instability, anxiety, decreased concentration. Normal response to hormonal changes. Occurs in 50–80% of mothers. Onset 2–4 days postpartum, lasts 48 hours (up to 10 days). Does NOT require psychotropic medication. Postpartum Depression (MDD with peripartum onset): Onset during pregnancy or within 4 weeks of delivery. Lasts 2–6 months (residual up to 1 year). Occurs in 3–6% of mothers. Requires treatment (psychotherapy, SSRIs, ECT if severe/psychotic).

The distinction is important: postpartum blues is self-limiting and does not require medication, while postpartum depression significantly impairs maternal functioning and can affect child development. Screening with tools like the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale is recommended.

**Q:** What is the risk, treatment, and prognosis of MDD with peripartum onset (Postpartum Depression)?

**A:** Risk: up to 3–6% of mothers; 50% recurrence risk. Risk factors: previous mood disorder, family history, psychosocial stressors, unwanted pregnancy, colicky/sick infant, marital conflict. Treatment: psychotherapy (CBT or IPT), SSRIs (short-term safety for breastfeeding established, long-term unknown), ECT if severe/psychotic. Prognosis: treatment of mother improves child outcomes at 8 months.

SSRIs are considered first-line pharmacotherapy for postpartum depression when medication is needed. Per Canadian Paediatric Society, SSRIs and SNRIs in breastfeeding are generally compatible; Poor Neonatal Adaptation Syndrome (PNAS) may occur in ~1/3 of infants exposed in utero but is generally mild and self-limiting.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 definitions of Bipolar I and Bipolar II Disorder?

**A:** Bipolar I: At least one manic episode (if manic symptoms lead to hospitalization or include psychotic features → Bipolar I diagnosis). MDEs are common but not required. Bipolar II: At least 1 MDE + 1 hypomanic episode + NO manic episodes. Bipolar II is NOT a 'milder' form of Bipolar I (50% of time spent depressed vs. 32% for Bipolar I).

Bipolar II is often missed because the depressive episodes dominate the clinical picture and hypomanic episodes may not be recognized or reported. MDE history features favouring bipolar over unipolar: early onset (<25 yr), multiple MDEs, psychotic symptoms, postpartum onset, antidepressant 'poop-out,' hypersomnia/hyperphagia.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of Bipolar Disorder?

**A:** Lifetime prevalence: Bipolar I 1%, Bipolar II 1.1%, Subthreshold BD 2.4%; M:F = 1:1. Mean age of onset: 25 years (usually MDE first, manic episode 6–10 years later; average age of first manic episode: 32 years). 90% of those with a single manic episode will have another.

The mean age of first manic episode (~32 years) occurs years after the first depressive episode, which explains why many patients with Bipolar II are initially (and incorrectly) treated for unipolar depression — a dangerous situation given that antidepressant monotherapy can precipitate mania.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of a MDE history that should raise suspicion for Bipolar Disorder over Unipolar MDD?

**A:** Early age of onset (<25 yr), increased number of MDEs, psychotic symptoms, postpartum onset, anxiety disorders (especially separation anxiety, panic), antidepressant failure due to early 'poop out' or hypomanic symptoms, early impulsivity and aggression, substance misuse, cyclothymic temperament, family history of bipolar disorder, more suicide attempts, atypical depressive symptoms (hypersomnia, hyperphagia), psychomotor impairment.

Recognizing bipolar spectrum illness before prescribing antidepressant monotherapy is critical — antidepressants alone can precipitate manic/hypomanic switches or induce mixed states, increasing suicide risk. Adding a mood stabilizer first is safer in suspected Bipolar II.

**Q:** What is the pharmacological treatment of Bipolar Disorder for each phase?

**A:** Treating mania: lithium, divalproex, carbamazepine (2nd line), SGAs, ECT (2nd line), benzodiazepines (acute agitation). Preventing mania: same as above at lower doses (minus ECT and BZDs). Treating bipolar depression ('4 Ls'): Lithium, Lamotrigine, Lurasidone, seroquel (Quetiapine); antidepressants are 2nd line only WITH mood stabilizer; ECT (2nd line). Preventing depression: above + aripiprazole, divalproex; quetiapine is 1st line for Bipolar II depression. Mixed episode/rapid cycling: lithium or divalproex + SGA (lurasidone, aripiprazole). Avoid antidepressant monotherapy.

Rapid cycling (≥4 mood episodes per year) is associated with hypothyroidism — checking TSH and treating hypothyroidism is important. The '4 Ls' mnemonic (Lithium, Lamotrigine, Lurasidone, seroqueL) aids recall of bipolar depression treatment options.

**Q:** What is Cyclothymia and how is it treated?

**A:** Cyclothymia: Numerous periods of hypomanic and depressive symptoms (NOT meeting full criteria for hypomanic episode or MDE) for ≥2 years; never without symptoms for >2 months. Has never met criteria for MDE, manic, or hypomanic episode. Not due to substances/GMC; causes clinically significant distress/impairment. Treatment: similar to Bipolar I — mood stabilizer + psychotherapy.

Cyclothymia lies on the bipolar spectrum. Unlike Bipolar II, full hypomanic or depressive episodes are absent. The 2-year minimum duration distinguishes it from transient mood fluctuations. Approximately 15–50% of those with cyclothymia eventually develop Bipolar I or II.

**Q:** What is the prognosis and course of Bipolar Disorder?

**A:** High suicide rate: 15% mortality from suicide, especially in depressive/mixed episodes. Chronic relapsing-remitting course; depressive symptoms more frequent and longer than manic. Can achieve high functioning between episodes. 90% recurrence of mania within 5 years after a manic episode. Long-term Bipolar I: 15% well, 45% well with relapses, 30% partial remission, 10% chronically ill. Patients with BD at higher suicide risk when switching from depression to mania due to impulsive behaviour during manic phase.

Bipolar II patients spend approximately 50% of their time in depressive episodes, contributing to the elevated suicide risk. Rapid cycling, mixed features, and substance use disorder are associated with a worse prognosis.

**Q:** Why should antidepressant monotherapy be avoided in Bipolar Disorder depression?

**A:** Antidepressant monotherapy without a mood stabilizer can cause a switch from depression into mania (or hypomania), and can increase the risk of rapid cycling and mixed states — all associated with increased suicide risk and worse overall prognosis.

Per Canadian and international guidelines, antidepressants should only be used as 2nd line adjuncts in bipolar depression, always in combination with a mood stabilizer. First-line options for bipolar depression include lithium, lamotrigine, lurasidone, and quetiapine (the '4 Ls').

**Q:** What is the DSM-5 diagnosis of MDD (Major Depressive Disorder) — key criteria?

**A:** A. Presence of a single MDE (≥5 symptoms for ≥2 weeks). For recurrent MDD, ≥2 MDEs with ≥2 consecutive months between episodes. B. MDE not better accounted for by schizoaffective disorder or superimposed on schizophrenia spectrum. C. No lifetime manic or hypomanic episode (if substance/treatment-induced manic episodes, these are excluded). Specifiers include: anxious distress, mixed features, melancholic, atypical, mood-congruent/incongruent psychotic features, catatonia, peripartum onset, seasonal pattern.

The exclusion of manic/hypomanic episodes (Criterion C) is what separates MDD from Bipolar Disorder. However, antidepressant-induced or medically-induced manic episodes do not count against this criterion — demonstrating the importance of clarifying the context of any prior manic symptoms.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of untreated MDD at 1 year?

**A:** At 1 year after MDD diagnosis without treatment: 40% still meet full criteria for MDD, 20% have some symptoms but no longer meet full criteria, 40% have no symptoms.

This 'rule of 40-20-40' demonstrates that MDD is not universally self-limiting — 60% of untreated patients continue to have significant symptoms at 1 year. This underscores the importance of active treatment rather than watchful waiting in moderate-to-severe depression.

## Psychiatry Anxiety

**Q:** What is the definition and pathophysiology of anxiety disorders?

**A:** Pathological fear (anxiety) occurs when: fear is greatly out of proportion to the threat, response continues beyond the existence of the threat, or social/occupational functioning is impaired. Physiologically: the amygdala is the main brain structure involved; neurotransmitters include 5-HT, cholecystokinin, epinephrine, norepinephrine, and DA. Behaviourally: avoidance perpetuates the fear-avoidance cycle.

Understanding the anxiety fear-avoidance cycle is crucial for explaining why exposure-based CBT is effective — graduated exposure prevents avoidance reinforcement and allows extinction learning. The amygdala's central role makes it a target for pharmacological intervention (e.g., SSRIs modulate 5-HT input to amygdala).

**Q:** What medical conditions can cause or mimic anxiety disorders?

**A:** Cardiovascular: post-MI, arrhythmia, CHF, pulmonary embolus, mitral valve prolapse. Respiratory: asthma, COPD, pneumonia. Endocrine: hyperthyroidism, hypoglycemia, hyperadrenalism, hyperparathyroidism. Metabolic: B12 deficiency, folate deficiency, porphyria, hypoxemia, hypercalcemia. Neurologic: neoplasm, vestibular dysfunction, encephalitis, trauma, MS, temporal lobe epilepsy, migraine. Infectious: meningitis, HIV, syphilis. GI: gastritis, esophageal spasm. Substance-induced: intoxication (caffeine, cannabis, amphetamines, cocaine) or withdrawal (benzodiazepines, alcohol).

The differential diagnosis of anxiety disorders is broad. A thorough medical workup is required before attributing anxiety to a primary psychiatric cause. Hyperthyroidism and pheochromocytoma are classic 'great mimickers' of panic disorder.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Panic Disorder?

**A:** A. Recurrent unexpected panic attacks — abrupt surge of intense fear peaking within minutes with ≥4 of: palpitations/tachycardia, sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, choking, chest pain, nausea/abdominal distress, dizziness/lightheadedness, chills/heat sensations, paresthesias, derealization/depersonalization, fear of losing control/'going crazy,' fear of dying. B. ≥1 attack followed by ≥1 month of: persistent worry about additional attacks OR significant maladaptive behavioural change related to attacks. Mnemonic: STUDENTS FEAR the 3 Cs (Sweating, Trembling, Unexpected, Derealization/depersonalization, Excessive heart rate/palpitations, Nausea, Tingling, Shortness of breath; Fear of dying/losing control; 3 Cs: Chest pain, Chills/hot flashes, Choking).

Panic disorder is characterized by the unexpectedness of attacks and the persistent concern about future attacks (anticipatory anxiety). The catastrophic misinterpretation of somatic symptoms (e.g., interpreting palpitations as a heart attack) perpetuates the cycle.

**Q:** What is the medical workup for anxiety disorders?

**A:** Recommended routine screening: vitals, physical exam, CBC, electrolytes, thyroid function test (TSH), glucose, ECG. Additional screening (as needed): extended electrolytes, vitamin B12, B-HCG, folate, chest X-ray, and other tests per differential diagnosis.

Thyroid function testing is essential given that hyperthyroidism can present identically to panic disorder or generalized anxiety disorder. ECG is important to rule out cardiac arrhythmias, which can cause palpitations and be misattributed to anxiety.

## Psychiatry Substance Use

**Q:** What is the DSM-5 severity classification for substance use disorders based on number of criteria met within 12 months?

**A:** Mild: 2-3 criteria; Moderate: 4-5 criteria; Severe: 6 or more criteria.

Substance use disorders are diagnosed on a continuum of severity. A single patient can have multiple co-occurring substance use disorders (e.g., moderate alcohol use disorder AND mild stimulant use disorder). Severity guides treatment intensity.

**Q:** List the DSM-5 criteria for substance use disorder using the mnemonic PEC WITH MCAT.

**A:** P – use despite Physical/psychological problem; E – failure to fulfill External roles; C – Craving; W – Withdrawal; I – continued use despite Interpersonal problems; T – Tolerance; H – use in physically Hazardous situations; M – More substance used/longer than intended; C – unsuccessful attempts to Cut down; A – Activities given up; T – excessive Time spent using/finding substance.

All 11 criteria apply across substance types. Diagnosis requires at least 2 criteria in a 12-month period. Neither intoxication alone nor a positive urine screen constitutes a substance use disorder.

**Q:** What is the role of urine drug screens and serum toxicology in diagnosing substance use disorders?

**A:** Urine drug screens detect recent drug use but cannot diagnose substance use disorders. Serum toxicology measures recent alcohol consumption but has no relation to the diagnosis of alcohol use disorder. Positive results cannot determine frequency or amount used; negative results cannot rule out use.

Toxicology is useful for detecting recent use and differentiating withdrawal from other psychiatric disorders. However, substance use disorder is a clinical diagnosis based on DSM-5 criteria, not on laboratory findings alone.

**Q:** What are the symptoms of intoxication vs. withdrawal from CNS depressants (alcohol, opioids, barbiturates, benzodiazepines)?

**A:** Intoxication: euphoria, slurred speech, disinhibition, confusion, poor coordination, coma. Withdrawal: anxiety, anhedonia, tremor, seizures, insomnia, psychosis, delirium, death (in severe cases).

CNS depressant withdrawal is medically dangerous and can be fatal — unlike stimulant or hallucinogen withdrawal. This high-stakes distinction is commonly tested. Benzodiazepines are the mainstay of withdrawal management.

**Q:** What are the symptoms of stimulant intoxication and withdrawal (amphetamines, cocaine, MDMA)?

**A:** Intoxication: euphoria, mania, psychomotor agitation, anxiety, psychosis (especially paranoia), insomnia, cardiovascular complications (stroke, MI, arrhythmias), seizure. Withdrawal: 'crash,' craving, dysphoria, suicidality.

Stimulant withdrawal is not life-threatening but carries significant risk of suicide during the 'crash.' There are no approved pharmacologic agents for cocaine or stimulant use disorder — psychological interventions are the mainstay.

**Q:** Which substances typically have NO significant withdrawal syndrome?

**A:** Hallucinogens (LSD, mescaline, psilocybin, PCP, ketamine, ibogaine, salvia) typically do not produce a clinically significant withdrawal syndrome.

Physical dependence and withdrawal are not thought to occur with hallucinogens, although psychological dependence and harmful use patterns can develop. Tolerance to hallucinogens develops rapidly (within hours to days).

**Q:** What are the post-ingestion urine detection windows for common substances of abuse?

**A:** Amphetamine: 48 h; Barbiturates: 1–21 d; Short-acting benzodiazepine: 72 h; Long-acting benzodiazepine: 30 d; Cocaine: 12–72 h; Morphine: 48–72 h; Methadone: 72 h; Oxycodone: 2–4 d; PCP: 8 d; Cannabis: 3 d (single use) to >30 d (heavy users).

Detection windows reflect the pharmacokinetics of the substance and its metabolites. Cannabis has the longest and most variable window due to its lipid solubility and accumulation in fat tissue with heavy use. Long-acting benzodiazepines (e.g., diazepam) can be detected for up to 30 days.

**Q:** Why are beta-blockers contraindicated in cocaine overdose management?

**A:** Beta-blockers (including labetalol) are not recommended in cocaine overdose because they cause unopposed alpha-adrenergic stimulation, which can lead to coronary artery vasoconstriction and worsened hypertension.

Cocaine blocks presynaptic reuptake of norepinephrine and epinephrine causing vasospasm and hypertension. Using a beta-blocker leaves alpha-adrenergic receptors unopposed, worsening vasoconstriction and potentially precipitating MI or stroke. Benzodiazepines are the preferred treatment for cocaine-induced seizures and agitation.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of cocaine's action and what ECG finding can appear in overdose?

**A:** Cocaine: (1) blocks presynaptic reuptake of serotonin (euphoria), dopamine (addiction), norepinephrine and epinephrine (vasospasm, HTN); (2) sodium channel blockade (slows nerve conduction, local anesthetic effect). In overdose, cardiac Na+ channel blockade can produce QRS complex prolongation on ECG.

Cocaine's sodium channel blockade is similar to class I antiarrhythmic drugs. QRS widening in cocaine overdose is a marker of severe toxicity. Cocaine has a short biological half-life of ~1 hour, prompting binge use patterns to maintain effect.

**Q:** What are the cardiovascular complications of cocaine use?

**A:** Arrhythmias, MI, cerebrovascular accident (stroke), ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm (AAA), intracranial hemorrhage, and chest pain (40% of all cocaine-related ED visits).

Cocaine's vasoconstrictive and sympathomimetic effects make it a major cardiovascular toxin. Even young patients without traditional cardiovascular risk factors can present with MI. QRS prolongation due to sodium channel blockade contributes to arrhythmias.

**Q:** What are the features of amphetamine intoxication and chronic use psychosis?

**A:** Intoxication: euphoria, improved concentration, sympathetic and behavioural hyperactivity; at high doses, can mimic psychosis or mania; can cause coma. Chronic use: psychosis resembling schizophrenia with agitation, paranoia, delusions, and hallucinations. Withdrawal: dysphoria, fatigue, restlessness.

Amphetamine-induced psychosis is treated acutely with antipsychotics; benzodiazepines for agitation; beta-blockers for tachycardia and HTN. Crystal methamphetamine is associated with formication (tactile hallucination of insects crawling under skin) and can cause stroke with long-term use.

**Q:** What are the clinical manifestations and evidence-based medical uses of cannabis?

**A:** Intoxication: tachycardia, conjunctival vascular engorgement, dry mouth, altered sensorium, increased appetite, muscle relaxation; euphoria, impaired cognitive and psychomotor performance, impaired short-term memory. High doses: depersonalization, paranoia, anxiety, psychosis. Chronic use: tolerance, amotivational syndrome, respiratory problems. Evidence-based uses: chemotherapy-induced nausea/vomiting, spasticity (MS, spinal cord injury), chronic neuropathic pain.

Inhaled cannabis has onset within minutes, peaking at 15–30 min. Ingested cannabis has delayed onset (30–90 min), peaking 2–3 h and lasting 4–12 h. Cannabis use is associated with increased risk of psychotic disorders in a dose-dependent manner, particularly in genetically predisposed individuals.

**Q:** What is Cannabinoid Hyperemesis Syndrome (CHS) and how do patients typically self-medicate?

**A:** CHS is a syndrome associated with chronic heavy cannabis use characterized by cyclical, recurrent severe nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain with no specific GI pathology identified. Patients typically self-medicate with hot baths or showers, which provides temporary relief.

CHS is increasingly recognized as cannabis potency increases. The mechanism of hot bath relief is not fully understood but may relate to transient receptor potential vanilloid (TRPV1) channels. Cessation of cannabis is the definitive treatment.

**Q:** What is Hallucinogen Persisting Perception Disorder (HPPD)?

**A:** HPPD is a DSM-5 diagnosis characterized by long-lasting, spontaneous, intermittent, distressing recurrences of visual perceptual changes reminiscent of those experienced during prior hallucinogen exposure ('flashbacks'). These can occur months to years after the last use.

HPPD is distinct from acute hallucinogen intoxication. It causes significant distress and functional impairment. It is associated predominantly with 5-HT2A agonist hallucinogens (LSD, psilocybin, mescaline). Management is generally supportive, with benzodiazepines for distress.

**Q:** What are the 3 main types of hallucinogens classified by primary mechanism of action with examples?

**A:** 1. 5-HT2A agonists (most common): LSD, mescaline (peyote), psilocybin, DMT (ayahuasca). 2. NMDA antagonists: PCP (phencyclidine), ketamine. 3. κ-opioid agonists: salvia divinorum, ibogaine.

5-HT2A agonist intoxication causes tachycardia, HTN, mydriasis, tremor, hyperpyrexia, and perceptual/mood changes. NMDA antagonists (PCP, ketamine) produce dissociative states with amnesia, analgesia, and hallucinations. PCP overdose features horizontal/vertical nystagmus, myoclonus, and prolonged agitated psychosis treated with haloperidol.

**Q:** What are the characteristic adverse effects and management of MDMA ('Ecstasy') toxicity?

**A:** MDMA acts on serotonergic and dopaminergic pathways, with properties of both a hallucinogen and stimulant. Adverse effects: diaphoresis, tachycardia, fatigue, jaw clenching (muscle spasms), ataxia, hyperthermia, arrhythmias, DIC, rhabdomyolysis, renal failure, seizures, death.

Hyperthermia is one of the most dangerous complications of MDMA use and a common cause of MDMA-related deaths. MDMA causes serotonin syndrome in severe cases. Management is supportive: cooling, hydration, control of hyperthermia and agitation, monitoring for multi-organ failure.

**Q:** What is GHB (gamma-hydroxybutyrate) and what makes it particularly dangerous?

**A:** GHB has a biphasic dopamine response (inhibition then release) and releases opiate-like substances. Effects: euphoria, increased aggression, impaired judgment. Severe withdrawal from abrupt cessation of high doses causes tremor, seizures, and psychosis. Used as a date rape drug (tasteless, odourless).

GHB withdrawal can be life-threatening, similar to alcohol and benzodiazepine withdrawal. GHB is also known as 'liquid ecstasy' or '6.' Along with flunitrazepam (Rohypnol) and ketamine, GHB is a major date rape drug due to its ability to cause rapid sedation, amnesia, and reduced inhibition.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of PCP (phencyclidine) intoxication and overdose management?

**A:** Intoxication: amnestic, euphoric, hallucinatory state. Adverse effects: horizontal and/or vertical nystagmus (classic), myoclonus, ataxia, autonomic instability, prolonged agitated psychosis, high risk for suicide and violence toward others. High doses can cause coma. Management: diazepam IV for autonomic instability; haloperidol for prolonged agitated psychosis.

PCP nystagmus is classically both horizontal AND vertical — an important distinguishing feature from most other substances causing only horizontal nystagmus. PCP was originally developed as a veterinary anesthetic. Ketamine has a similar but less severe profile.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for developing a substance use disorder?

**A:** Genetic/neurobiological factors; psychological factors; environmental factors: low socioeconomic status, peer influence, adverse childhood or traumatic experiences, social isolation, systemic racism, chronic stress. Comorbid conditions: mental illness, chronic disease, acute/chronic pain. Environmental exposure (e.g., over-prescription of opioids in North America fuelled the opioid use disorder crisis).

Substance use disorders are multifactorial. The biopsychosocial model integrates genetic vulnerability (neurobiological reward pathways), individual psychology, and environmental exposures. Understanding this helps clinicians take a non-judgmental, trauma-informed approach to assessment and treatment.

**Q:** What are the principles of harm reduction in substance use disorders?

**A:** Harm reduction principles: safe sex practices; avoid driving while intoxicated; avoid substances with child care responsibilities; safe needle practices and needle exchange programs; pill-testing kits; reducing (not necessarily eliminating) tobacco use; naloxone kit distribution for those at risk of opioid overdose.

Harm reduction is an evidence-based public health approach that accepts substance use as a reality and focuses on minimizing harms without requiring abstinence. In Canada, programs like RAAM (Rapid Access to Addiction Medicine) clinics in Ontario offer low-barrier specialized services by self-referral.

**Q:** What is formication and with which substances is it classically associated?

**A:** Formication is a tactile hallucination characterized by the sensation of insects or snakes crawling over or under the skin. It is especially associated with crystal methamphetamine use, but can also occur with cocaine and PCP.

Formication (from Latin 'formica' = ant) is a type of tactile hallucination seen in stimulant intoxication and withdrawal. In cocaine use, it is sometimes called 'cocaine bugs' or 'Magnan's sign.' It can lead to excoriation and skin picking injuries.

## Psychiatry Alcohol

**Q:** What are the CAGE criteria for screening alcohol use disorder, and what scores are considered positive?

**A:** C – Have you ever felt the need to Cut down? A – Have you ever felt Annoyed by criticism of your drinking? G – Have you ever felt Guilty about your drinking? E – Have you ever needed a drink first thing in the morning (Eye opener)? Positive screen: ≥2 for men; ≥1 for women.

CAGE is a validated, brief screening tool used widely in Canadian primary care. A positive result warrants further assessment to distinguish problem drinking from alcohol use disorder. Women are screened at a lower threshold due to greater physiological vulnerability.

**Q:** What are Canada's Low-Risk Alcohol Drinking Guidelines for moderate drinking?

**A:** Men: ≤3 drinks/day (≤15/week); Women: ≤2 drinks/day (≤10/week); Elderly: ≤1 drink/day.

Canadian Low-Risk Alcohol Drinking Guidelines emphasize that lower consumption reduces risk of chronic disease, injury, and dependence. Exceeding these limits, particularly chronically, increases risk of alcohol use disorder and organ damage.

**Q:** What are the biochemical markers of prolonged alcohol use?

**A:** Elevated liver function tests (AST, ALT, GGT), elevated MCV, and elevated carbohydrate-deficient transferrin (CDT). AST:ALT ratio >2:1 and elevated GGT are particularly suggestive of alcohol use.

CDT is a sensitive and specific marker for chronic heavy drinking. GGT is highly sensitive but less specific. An AST:ALT ratio >2:1 classically suggests alcoholic liver disease vs. viral hepatitis (ratio typically <1). MCV reflects macrocytosis from nutritional deficiency or direct alcohol toxicity.

**Q:** What is the legal blood alcohol concentration (BAC) limit for impaired driving in Canada, and at what BAC level do most signs of intoxication appear?

**A:** Legal limit: BAC ≥0.08% (≥80 mg/dL or ≥17.4 mmol/L), typically reached after 4 drinks in women or 5 drinks in men in 2 hours. Signs of intoxication appear at >21.7 mmol/L (100 mg/dL).

Respiratory depression and arrest can occur at >60 mmol/L in non-tolerant drinkers and 90–120 mmol/L in tolerant drinkers. The legal BAC threshold applies throughout all Canadian provinces and territories.

**Q:** Describe the 4 stages of alcohol withdrawal and their typical time of onset.

**A:** Stage 1 (4–12 h): tremor, sweating, agitation, anorexia, cramps, diarrhea, sleep disturbance, anxiety — most withdrawals are mild-moderate (Stage 1 only). Stage 2 (12–24 h): alcoholic hallucinosis (visual, auditory, olfactory, or tactile hallucinations). Stage 3 (12–48 h): tonic-clonic seizures (usually non-focal and brief). Stage 4 (48–96 h): delirium tremens (confusion, delusions, hallucinations, agitation, autonomic hyperactivity — medical emergency).

Alcohol withdrawal is a medical emergency with 20% mortality if untreated in severe delirium tremens. Young patients typically have full recovery; the elderly are often left with cognitive deficits. Not all patients progress through all stages.

**Q:** What are the features of delirium tremens (DTs) and what is the mortality rate if untreated?

**A:** DTs occur 48–96 h after last drink. Features: autonomic hyperactivity (diaphoresis, tachycardia, increased respiration), hand tremor, insomnia, psychomotor agitation, fever, confusion/disorientation, delusions, and hallucinations. Untreated mortality: 20%.

DTs represent the most severe form of alcohol withdrawal and constitute a medical emergency. The CIWA-A scale monitors severity. Benzodiazepines (diazepam) are the cornerstone of management; thiamine must be given to prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy.

**Q:** What does the CIWA-A scale assess in alcohol withdrawal, and how are severity thresholds defined?

**A:** CIWA-A assesses: Physical (5): paroxysmal sweats, headache, agitation, nausea/vomiting, tremor. Psychological/cognitive (2): anxiety, orientation/clouding of sensorium. Perceptual (3): tactile, auditory, and visual disturbances. Scoring: 0–7 per category (except sensorium: 0–4); max score 67. Mild <10, Moderate 10–20, Severe >20.

The CIWA-A scale guides symptom-triggered benzodiazepine dosing in alcohol withdrawal. Symptom-triggered therapy is preferred over fixed dosing as it reduces total benzodiazepine required. Scores guide when to escalate care.

**Q:** What is the standard alcohol withdrawal treatment protocol (CIWA-A based), and what modifications are made for special populations?

**A:** Basic: Diazepam 20 mg PO q1–2 h PRN until CIWA-A <10 + thiamine 100–250 mg IM then 100 mg PO daily x3 d + supportive care. History of seizures: Diazepam 20 mg PO q1 h x ≥3 doses regardless of CIWA score. Age >65 / severe liver disease / severe asthma / respiratory failure: Use short-acting BZD (lorazepam 1–4 mg PO/SL/IM q1–2 h). Hallucinations present: Add haloperidol 2–5 mg IM/PO q1–4 h (max 5 doses/d) + diazepam x3 doses as seizure prophylaxis (haloperidol lowers seizure threshold).

Long-acting benzodiazepines (diazepam) are preferred for their smooth self-tapering pharmacokinetics. However, short-acting agents (lorazepam) are safer in elderly and hepatically impaired patients due to reduced accumulation risk. Admit if still in withdrawal after 80 mg diazepam, DTs, recurrent arrhythmias, multiple seizures, or medically ill.

**Q:** What is Wernicke's encephalopathy, and how does it differ from Korsakoff's syndrome?

**A:** Wernicke's encephalopathy: acute and reversible; due to thiamine deficiency; triad of oculomotor dysfunction (nystagmus, CN VI palsy), gait ataxia, and confusion. Lesions in mammillary bodies, thalamus, and brainstem. Korsakoff's syndrome: chronic and only 20% reversible with treatment; anterograde amnesia and compensatory confabulation; cannot occur only during acute delirium or dementia.

Wernicke's can progress to Korsakoff's if untreated. Thiamine must be given BEFORE glucose in malnourished/alcoholic patients to avoid precipitating Wernicke's. IV glucose without thiamine can rapidly deplete remaining thiamine stores.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Wernicke's encephalopathy (preventive vs. acute) and Korsakoff's syndrome?

**A:** Wernicke's preventive (any patient in withdrawal): thiamine 100–250 mg IM/IV x1 dose. Wernicke's acute treatment: thiamine 500 mg IV BID/TID x72 h, then reassess. Korsakoff's: IV treatment as for Wernicke's, then thiamine 100 mg PO TID x 3–12 months.

High-dose IV thiamine is critical in acute Wernicke's as oral absorption is unreliable in alcoholic patients. Korsakoff's has only 20% reversibility, underscoring the importance of early Wernicke's recognition and treatment. Never give glucose before thiamine in at-risk patients.

**Q:** What are the pharmacological agents used to treat alcohol use disorder and their mechanisms?

**A:** 1. Naltrexone (Revia®): opioid antagonist — reduces 'high' and cravings; can start while drinking; not safe with acute liver failure or opioid use in last 10 days. 2. Acamprosate (Campral®): NMDA glutamate receptor antagonist — maintains abstinence; not safe with renal failure. 3. Disulfiram (Antabuse®): blocks acetaldehyde dehydrogenase — aversive reaction to alcohol; only used when goal is abstinence; contraindicated in severe renal disease, pregnancy, psychoses, cardiac disease; RCT evidence generally poor due to adherence issues.

Naltrexone and acamprosate are first-line Health Canada-approved agents. Disulfiram is used adjunctively when motivation for abstinence is high. Gabapentin, topiramate, and ondansetron have some evidence as anti-craving agents but are not Health Canada approved for this indication.

## Psychiatry Opioids

**Q:** What is the classic opioid overdose triad (RAM), and how is it managed acutely?

**A:** RAM: Respiratory depression, Altered mental status (decreased LOC), Miosis (pinpoint pupils; exception: meperidine). Management: ABCs; IV glucose for hypoglycemia; naloxone (Narcan®) 0.4 mg up to 2 mg IV for diagnosis and treatment; may need intubation + naloxone drip up to >48 h with long-acting opioids. Caution: opioids have longer half-life than naloxone — observe ≥24 h.

Meperidine causes mydriasis (not miosis) due to its anticholinergic properties — an important exception. Naloxone's short half-life (~60 min) means re-sedation can occur after reversal. The fentanyl-contaminated drug supply in Canada has dramatically increased overdose fatalities, making naloxone kit distribution a public health priority.

**Q:** What are the symptoms of opioid withdrawal and how is it managed?

**A:** Symptoms: dysphoric mood, insomnia, drug craving, myalgias, nausea/vomiting, yawning, chills, lacrimation, rhinorrhea, pupillary dilation, piloerection, sweating, diarrhea, fever. Onset: 6–12 h (depending on opioid half-life); Duration: 5–10 days. Management: long-acting oral opioids (methadone, buprenorphine), α2-adrenergic agonists (clonidine for autonomic symptoms). Monitor with Clinical Opioid Withdrawal Scale (COWS).

Opioid withdrawal is severely uncomfortable but not life-threatening (unlike alcohol/benzo withdrawal). A critical complication is loss of tolerance with overdose on relapse — patients who leave treatment are highly vulnerable. Complications also include miscarriage and premature labour.

**Q:** What is Suboxone® and why does it contain naloxone?

**A:** Suboxone® is a buprenorphine/naloxone combination tablet for opioid use disorder maintenance. Naloxone is included to prevent injection abuse: when injected, naloxone precipitates opioid withdrawal and blocks buprenorphine's opioid effect. When taken sublingually (as prescribed), naloxone has negligible bioavailability and does not antagonize buprenorphine's effect. Also available as a monthly IM formulation (Sublocade).

Buprenorphine is a partial opioid agonist/antagonist with a ceiling effect on respiratory depression, making it safer than methadone. The naloxone component serves as a deterrent to misuse. Methadone is a full opioid agonist and can cause QTc prolongation — baseline ECG screening is recommended.

**Q:** What are the evidence-based pharmacologic and psychological treatments for opioid use disorder?

**A:** Pharmacologic (maintenance treatment): methadone (opioid agonist) — risk of QTc prolongation, ECG screening recommended; buprenorphine (partial agonist-antagonist); extended-release naltrexone injection (opioid antagonist — for motivated patients after detox). Psychological: addiction counselling, CBT, contingency management, motivational enhancement therapy (MET), 12-step programs (Narcotics Anonymous). Social: family education and support.

Methadone and buprenorphine maintenance are associated with improved treatment retention, reduced illicit opioid use, decreased craving, and improved social function. Naltrexone injections are an emerging option. In Canada, carrying a naloxone kit is a life-saving public health strategy encouraged for patients and their close contacts.

## Psychiatry Somatic

**Q:** What are the general characteristics of somatic symptom and related disorders?

**A:** Physical symptoms lacking objective medical support, emerging in the presence of psychological factors. Key features: symptoms produced unconsciously (not malingering/factitious disorder); cause significant distress or functional impairment; may involve primary gain (symbolic resolution of unconscious conflict, reduces anxiety) or secondary gain (external benefits, sick role).

The key distinction is that somatic symptoms are unconsciously produced. Malingering involves intentional production of symptoms for external reward (secondary gain). Factitious disorder involves intentional production without external reward (patient seeks nurturance/attention). This distinction is clinically and medicolegally important.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Somatic Symptom Disorder?

**A:** A. One or more somatic symptoms causing distress or disruption of daily life. B. Excessive thoughts, feelings, or behaviours (at least one): (1) disproportionate/persistent thoughts about symptom seriousness, (2) persistently high anxiety about health/symptoms, (3) excessive time/energy devoted to symptoms. C. Symptomatic state persistent (typically >6 months). Prevalence: 5–7%; F > M.

Somatic symptom disorder replaces the older DSM-IV category of 'somatization disorder.' A key change is that physical symptoms do not need to be 'medically unexplained' — comorbid medical illness can coexist. Common comorbidities: anxiety and depression (up to 80%). Rule out organic causes (e.g., MS) as somatic disorders are often misdiagnosed.
