**Q:** How does illness anxiety disorder differ from somatic symptom disorder?

**A:** Illness anxiety disorder: preoccupation with fear of having/developing a serious disease; minimal or no somatic symptoms (not acute); convictions persist despite negative investigations and reassurance; patient CAN acknowledge possible absence of disease (unlike delusion); high anxiety; maladaptive behaviour (excessive checking OR avoidance); duration ≥6 months; onset 3rd–4th decade. Prevalence: 3–5% of primary care patients. Somatic symptom disorder: prominent somatic symptoms are present.

The ability to acknowledge the possibility that the feared disease is not present distinguishes illness anxiety disorder from a somatic delusion. SSRIs may be helpful due to high anxiety levels. Specifiers: care-seeking type vs. care-avoidant type.

**Q:** What is Conversion Disorder (Functional Neurological Symptom Disorder) and what neurological signs can help differentiate it from organic disease?

**A:** Conversion disorder: one or more symptoms/deficits affecting voluntary motor or sensory function mimicking neurological or GMC (e.g., paralysis, impaired coordination, double vision, non-epileptic seizures). Spontaneous remission: 95% of acute cases, 50% of chronic cases (>6 months). Neurological testing signs: Hoover's sign (involuntary extension of 'paralyzed' leg when patient flexes contralateral leg against resistance); dermatome testing inconsistency. La belle indifférence: inappropriately cavalier attitude — associated but NOT diagnostic.

Conversion disorder does NOT require a preceding psychological event per DSM-5, but this is still worth exploring. Incidence: 2–5/100,000 general population; up to 20–25% of neurology inpatients. More common in rural populations. Incompatible findings on neurological examination help differentiate from organic disease.

**Q:** What are the key differences between somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder, conversion disorder, factitious disorder, and malingering?

**A:** Somatic symptoms: present in somatic symptom disorder / conversion disorder (neurological) / factitious disorder & malingering (can falsify); mild or absent in illness anxiety disorder. Symptom production: unconscious in somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety, and conversion disorder; conscious in factitious disorder and malingering. External reward (secondary gain): absent in somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety, conversion, and factitious disorder; PRESENT in malingering.

The two key axes are: (1) conscious vs. unconscious production, and (2) presence vs. absence of external secondary gain. Factitious disorder is conscious production WITHOUT external reward (the patient seeks nurturance, sympathy). Malingering is conscious production WITH external reward (e.g., disability, avoiding legal consequences).

**Q:** What is the management approach for somatic symptom and related disorders?

**A:** Non-pharmacological (mainstay): brief regularly scheduled GP visits (e.g., q4–6 wk); limit physicians involved; minimize unnecessary investigations; coordinate care; focus on functional improvement (physiotherapy, OT); psychoeducation; psychotherapy (CBT, psychodynamic, mindfulness, biofeedback). Pharmacological: minimize psychotropics; short-term anxiolytics only (associated with worse long-term outcomes); antidepressants for comorbid depression/anxiety.

The therapeutic relationship with a single GP is central to management. Emphasize mechanism of symptoms over searching for a cause; focus on what the patient can control. Functional recovery is the goal, not symptom elimination. Avoid iatrogenic harm from unnecessary investigations and procedures.

## Psychiatry Dissociative

**Q:** What are the key features of Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID)?

**A:** DID: disruption of identity characterized by ≥2 distinct personality states or experience of possession; sudden alterations in sense of self and agency (ego-dystonic emotions, behaviours, speech); recurrent amnesia (declarative or procedural); episodes of depersonalization and derealization. Rare (<1%). Major risk factor: childhood physical/sexual abuse or neglect. High risk of suicide and self-harm.

DID is associated with significant trauma history. The DDx includes borderline personality disorder (also linked to trauma) and PTSD. Psychotherapy (psychodynamic, CBT) is the mainstay of treatment. There is limited evidence for pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** What is dissociative amnesia, and how does it differ from generalized vs. localized/selective amnesia?

**A:** Dissociative amnesia: inability to recall important autobiographical information (usually traumatic/stressful), inconsistent with normal forgetting, not due to psychiatric disorder, medical illness, or substance. Localized/selective: failure to recall all/some events during a specific period (procedural memory preserved). Generalized (rare): complete loss of all life history ± procedural and semantic knowledge; sudden onset; presents with perplexity, disorientation, aimless wandering. Increased risk of suicide as amnesia resolves.

Dissociative fugue is a specifier of dissociative amnesia involving sudden unexpected travel away from home accompanied by amnesia for identity or other autobiographical details. Procedural memory (how to ride a bike) is classically preserved in localized dissociative amnesia.

**Q:** What is depersonalization/derealization disorder and what is preserved during episodes?

**A:** Persistent or recurrent episodes of: depersonalization (detachment from oneself, feelings of unreality, outside observer of one's own thoughts/feelings/actions) and/or derealization (unreality/detachment from surroundings, as if in a dream, world appears foggy). Key: reality testing is INTACT (patients know experiences are not real). Episodes can last hours to years. Transient experiences are common in the general population.

Intact reality testing distinguishes depersonalization/derealization disorder from psychosis. Patients are often distressed and fear 'going crazy.' Predisposing factors include severe stress or trauma. Treatment: psychotherapy (CBT, psychodynamic); limited evidence for pharmacotherapy.

## Psychiatry Sleep

**Q:** What are the sleep changes associated with depression?

**A:** Depression is associated with: decreased delta (slow-wave, deep) sleep; decreased REM latency (enters REM sleep faster than normal); increased REM density.

These sleep architecture changes are measurable on polysomnography and are important in understanding the neurobiology of depression. Neurotransmitters implicated in psychiatric illness also regulate sleep: increased ACh and decreased monoamine activity are associated with greater REM sleep, while decreased adrenergic and cholinergic activity is associated with NREM sleep.

**Q:** What are evidence-based sleep hygiene recommendations?

**A:** Wake up and go to bed at the same time daily (including weekends); avoid long wakefulness in bed; use bed only for sleep and sex (not reading, TV, work); avoid screens (smartphones, iPads) in the hour before bed; avoid napping; reduce/stop alcohol, caffeine, drugs; exercise ≥3–4x/week (but not in the evening if it disrupts sleep.

Sleep hygiene is a simple, effective, but often underutilized intervention. It forms the basis of CBT for insomnia (CBT-I), which is considered first-line treatment for chronic insomnia. These behavioural principles target conditioned arousal and circadian misalignment.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for chronic insomnia, and what pharmacological options are preferred?

**A:** First-line: Cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia (CBT-I). Pharmacological (preferred over benzodiazepines): non-benzodiazepines ('z-drugs': zopiclone/eszopiclone/zolpidem, lemborexant, low-dose doxepin) — effective for short-term use but must be regularly re-evaluated as long-term use causes dependency. Avoid benzodiazepines: risk of abuse/dependence, rebound insomnia, cognitive impairment, daytime somnolence, suppresses deep and REM sleep. Off-label options for comorbid psychiatric symptoms: trazodone, mirtazapine, quetiapine; for comorbid pain: low-dose amitriptyline.

Lemborexant is an orexin receptor antagonist that reduces wake drive rather than increasing sleep drive — a novel mechanism. Avoid lemborexant in narcolepsy or cataplexy. Common z-drug side effect: bitter metallic taste (improved by acidic foods like orange juice). Screen for complex sleep behaviours before and after prescribing sleep medications.

## Psychiatry Disruptive Disorders

**Q:** What are the three symptom domains required for a diagnosis of Oppositional Defiant Disorder (ODD)?

**A:** 1. Angry/irritable mood (easily loses temper, touchy/easily annoyed, often angry and resentful)
2. Argumentative/defiant behaviour (argues with adults/authority, defies requests/rules, deliberately annoys, blames others)
3. Vindictiveness (spiteful or vindictive at least twice in the past 6 months)

DSM-5 requires a pattern of negativistic, hostile, and defiant behaviour for at least 6 months involving a non-sibling, with at least 4 symptoms across these three domains. The behaviours must exceed what is normative for age, gender, and culture, and cause significant distress or impairment.

**Q:** How does frequency of ODD symptoms differ between children under 5 years versus those 5 years and older?

**A:** Under age 5: symptoms must occur on most days. Age 5 and older: symptoms must occur at least weekly.

This frequency threshold distinguishes ODD from normal developmental behaviour. The behaviours must cause significant distress or functional impairment and not occur exclusively during psychotic, substance use, or mood disorder episodes.

**Q:** What disorder supersedes an ODD diagnosis if criteria for both are met?

**A:** Disruptive Mood Dysregulation Disorder (DMDD) supersedes ODD if criteria for both are met.

DMDD is characterized by severe recurrent temper outbursts and a persistently irritable/angry mood, and takes diagnostic precedence over ODD when both sets of criteria are fulfilled, reflecting a higher level of mood dysregulation.

**Q:** What are the four categories of behaviour required for a Conduct Disorder (CD) diagnosis, and how many criteria and timeframe are needed?

**A:** Four categories: (1) Aggression to people and animals, (2) Destruction of property, (3) Deceitfulness or theft, (4) Violation of rules (TRAP mnemonic). At least 3 criteria in the past 12 months, with at least 1 in the past 6 months.

CD prevalence is 1.5–3.4% with a M:F ratio of 4:1. Diagnosis requires significant impairment in social, academic, or occupational functioning, and if the patient is ≥18 years old, criteria for ASPD must not be met. Early-onset type (≥1 criterion before age 10) has a worse prognosis.

**Q:** What percentage of children with Conduct Disorder develop Antisocial Personality Disorder as adults?

**A:** Approximately 50% of children with Conduct Disorder develop ASPD as adults.

Poor prognostic indicators for CD include early-age onset, high frequency and variety of behaviours, pervasiveness across settings (home, school, community), comorbid ADHD, early sexual activity, and substance misuse. Early intervention is more effective and long-term follow-up is required.

**Q:** What are the two manifestations of Intermittent Explosive Disorder (IED), and what are the minimum frequency and duration requirements for each?

**A:** 1. Verbal or physical aggression NOT causing damage to others/property: at least twice per week for 3 months
2. Physical aggression causing damage to a person, animal, or property: at least 3 outbursts in the last 12 months

IED can be diagnosed in patients aged ≥6 years and can be comorbid with ADHD, CD, ODD, or ASD when outbursts exceed those typically seen in those disorders. Outbursts must be out of proportion to triggers, not premeditated, and cause significant distress, functional impairment, or financial/legal consequences.

**Q:** What is the ODD prevalence and how does the sex distribution change with puberty?

**A:** Prevalence: 2–16%
Sex distribution: Before puberty, more common in males. After puberty, prevalence is equal between males and females (M = F after puberty).

ODD typically first appears during preschool and rarely begins later than early adolescence. It is associated with poor school performance, few friends, strained parent-child relationships, and risk of developing mood disorders later. It often precedes Conduct Disorder. Treatment includes parent management training, behavioural therapy, school interventions, and pharmacotherapy for comorbid disorders.

**Q:** What are the poor prognostic indicators for Conduct Disorder?

**A:** 1. Early age of onset
2. High frequency of behaviours
3. Variety of antisocial behaviours
4. Pervasiveness (home, school, and community)
5. Comorbid ADHD
6. Early sexual activity
7. Substance misuse

50% of children with CD develop ASPD as adults. Early intervention is more effective. Treatment includes parent management training, anger replacement training, CBT, family therapy, educational/employment programs, social skills training, and pharmacotherapy for comorbid disorders. Culturally safe and trauma-informed care is essential.

## Psychiatry Psychotherapy

**Q:** What common factors account for the majority of positive psychotherapy outcomes?

**A:** Common factors account for 60–90% of therapy outcomes and include: warmth (unconditional positive regard), accurate empathy, genuineness, goodness of fit, and the therapeutic relationship with the provider.

Only 10–40% of therapy outcome is due to specific factors (techniques particular to each modality). Research shows that effective psychotherapy creates observable changes in brain circuitry and connectivity, but these changes differ from those produced by pharmacological treatments.

**Q:** What are the 4 key problem areas addressed in Interpersonal Therapy (IPT), and what conditions is it primarily indicated for?

**A:** 4 problem areas: (1) Grief and loss, (2) Role transitions, (3) Conflict, (4) Interpersonal deficits.
Primarily indicated for: mood disorders (depression, bipolar disorder).

IPT involves weekly sessions over 12–20 sessions. Ideal candidates have depression or bipolar disorder with some insight and difficult social functioning. It is not appropriate for patients with severe psychotic processes, personality disorders, or comorbid substance use disorders.

**Q:** What are the four behavioural techniques used in CBT and how does each work?

**A:** 1. Systematic desensitization: gradually approach anxiety-provoking situations in a relaxed state
2. Flooding: confront feared stimulus for prolonged periods until no longer frightening
3. Positive reinforcement: strengthen behaviour by rewarding it
4. Negative reinforcement: increase behaviour frequency by removing an aversive stimulus when desired behaviour occurs
Also: Extinction (diminish behaviour by not rewarding) and Punishment/aversion therapy (diminish behaviour by applying noxious stimulus)

CBT combines cognitive (thought records to identify automatic thoughts) and behavioural components. It is indicated for most mental health disorders including mood, anxiety, OCD, personality, eating, substance use, and psychotic disorders. Typically 12–20 weekly sessions.

**Q:** What are the four skill areas and four components of Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT)?

**A:** 4 skill areas: (1) Mindfulness, (2) Emotional regulation, (3) Interpersonal effectiveness, (4) Distress tolerance
4 components: (1) Individual therapy, (2) Group skills training, (3) Phone consultations, (4) Consultation team

DBT combines CBT techniques with Buddhist Zen mindfulness and dialectical philosophy. It is primarily indicated for borderline personality disorder (BPD) or borderline traits with severe emotional dysregulation, impulsivity, or self-harm. Duration is typically 1 year with weekly individual and group therapy.

**Q:** What do the acronyms CAPE, RULE, and OARS stand for in Motivational Interviewing (MI)?

**A:** CAPE (Spirit of MI): Compassion, Acceptance, Partnership, Evocation
RULE (Principles): Resist righting reflex, Understand client reasons for change, Listen, Empower (convey hope and support autonomy)
OARS (Techniques): Open-ended questions, Affirmations, Reflections, Summaries

MI is primarily indicated for substance use disorders and maladaptive behaviour patterns. Brief interventions can be effective with as little as 15 minutes. Motivational Enhancement Therapy (MET) consists of 4 sessions. Addiction is a chronic condition requiring boosters over time.

**Q:** Which psychotherapy modality is most time-intensive, and what are its indications and techniques?

**A:** Psychoanalytic/Psychodynamic Therapy is the most time-intensive:
- Psychoanalysis: 4–5 sessions/week for 3–7 years
- Psychodynamically oriented therapy: 2–3 sessions/week for fewer years
Indications: Anxiety, obsessional thinking, conversion disorder, depression
Techniques: Free association, dream interpretation, transference analysis
Ideal candidates: Psychologically minded, highly motivated, able to withstand difficult emotions, high level of function

The theory explores the meaning of early experiences (recollection), reliving them with the therapist (repetition), and gaining insight (working through). Not suitable for patients with acute psychosis or inability to tolerate difficult emotions without fleeing or self-destructive acts.

## Psychiatry Pharmacotherapy

**Q:** What is the overall mechanism of action of antipsychotic medications?

**A:** Antipsychotics functionally antagonize, to varying degrees, D2 (dopamine) activity in target brain pathways.

All antipsychotics are considered equally effective except for clozapine, which is the most effective for treatment-resistant schizophrenia. Atypical (second-generation) antipsychotics have lower risk of EPS and tardive dyskinesia but more metabolic side effects compared to typical (first-generation) antipsychotics.

**Q:** What are the Canadian guidelines for treating acute psychosis in the emergency setting?

**A:** 1. Haloperidol 5 mg IM + lorazepam 2 mg IM
2. Loxapine PO or IM 25 mg + lorazepam 2 mg IM
3. Olanzapine 2.5–10 mg (PO, IM, or oral quick dissolve — time to peak is 4–6 h same as regular PO)
4. Risperidone 2 mg (M-tab or liquid)

These are the Canadian guidelines for acute agitation management. Inhaled loxapine is also available mainly for acute agitation. Long-acting depot preparations are generally better than oral preparations for overall outcomes, particularly for patients with schizophrenia who relapse due to non-adherence.

**Q:** Which antipsychotic is the most effective for treatment-resistant schizophrenia, and what monitoring is required?

**A:** Clozapine (Clozaril®) is the most effective for treatment-resistant schizophrenia. Monitoring: weekly blood counts (CBC) for the first 6 months, then every 2 weeks, due to 1% risk of agranulocytosis.

Clozapine has the highest starting dose of 25 mg PO BID, a maintenance dose of 300–600 mg/day, and maximum 900 mg/day. It is contraindicated with existing leukopenia/neutropenia and should not be combined with other drugs that cause bone marrow suppression. It also reduces seizure threshold and carries risks of weight gain, metabolic effects, sedation, orthostatic hypotension, constipation/fecal impaction, and cardiomyopathy.

**Q:** What is the risk of weight gain ranking among atypical antipsychotics?

**A:** Clozapine > Olanzapine > Quetiapine > Risperidone (lowest weight gain risk among these four).

Weight gain and metabolic effects are major concerns with atypical antipsychotics. Aripiprazole has less weight gain and lower risk of metabolic syndrome compared to olanzapine, and also has a lower incidence of EPS compared to haloperidol. Quetiapine is associated with slightly less weight gain than clozapine and olanzapine.

**Q:** Which antipsychotic has the highest risk of EPS/TD among second-generation antipsychotics (SGAs), and why might this be clinically relevant?

**A:** Risperidone (and its active metabolite paliperidone) has the highest risk of EPS/TD among SGAs. It should be avoided in patients at high risk for EPS, those with existing movement disorders, and the elderly. It also causes elevated prolactin (sexual dysfunction, galactorrhea, gynecomastia, menstrual disturbance, infertility).

Risperidone causes EPS at higher doses (>8 mg) but has lower incidence than typical antipsychotics at lower doses. Elevated prolactin distinguishes risperidone from most other SGAs. It is available in long-acting formulations (Risperdal Consta® IM q2 weeks and Invega Sustenna® q1 month / Trinza® q3 months for paliperidone).

**Q:** What is Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS), and what are its clinical features, treatment, and mortality?

**A:** NMS is a psychiatric emergency caused by strong DA blockade from antipsychotics.
Clinical features (tetrad): Mental status changes (first), fever, rigidity, autonomic instability — develops over 24–72 hours.
Labs: Elevated CPK, leukocytosis, myoglobinuria.
Treatment: Discontinue antipsychotic, hydration, cooling blankets, dantrolene (muscle relaxant), bromocriptine (DA agonist).
Mortality: 5%

Risk factors include high-potency or depot antipsychotics, sudden dose increase, male sex, young adults, medical illness, dehydration, exhaustion, poor nutrition, and external heat load. NMS must be distinguished from serotonin syndrome, which has different features and treatment.

**Q:** Compare acute dystonia and tardive dyskinesia: onset, high-risk populations, presentation, and treatment.

**A:** Acute Dystonia:
- Onset: within 5 days
- High risk: young Asian and Black males
- Presentation: sustained muscle contractions, abnormal posture, oculogyric crisis, laryngospasm, torticollis
- Treatment: benztropine or diphenhydramine IM

Tardive Dyskinesia:
- Onset: >90 days, more commonly years
- High risk: older patients
- Presentation: purposeless involuntary constant movements of face/mouth musculature, rarely limbs or diaphragm
- Treatment: no good standard treatment; consider clozapine, reduce dose, or valbenazine/deutetrabenazine (FDA-approved)

Anticholinergic agents (benztropine, diphenhydramine) must NOT be given for tardive syndromes as they worsen the condition. Anticholinergics should only be prescribed when the patient is at high risk for acute EPS or when acute EPS develops — not routinely with all antipsychotics.

**Q:** What are the features of Akathisia, and how does it differ from Parkinsonism as a drug-induced movement disorder?

**A:** Akathisia:
- Onset: within 10 days (acute) or >90 days (tardive)
- Presentation: motor restlessness, crawling sensation in legs relieved by walking; very distressing, increases suicide risk and poor adherence
- Treatment: lorazepam, propranolol, benztropine, or diphenhydramine; best approach is to reduce dose or switch to lower-potency atypical

Parkinsonism:
- Onset: within 30 days (acute)
- Presentation: tremor, cogwheel rigidity, akinesia, postural instability, decreased arm swing, stooped posture, shuffling gait
- Treatment: benztropine (acute); reduce dose or change antipsychotic

Both akathisia and parkinsonism are more common in older females and are related to dopamine blockade. Akathisia is particularly important to recognize because patient distress can be mistaken for worsening psychosis, leading to inappropriate antipsychotic dose increases.

**Q:** What is the onset of effect of antidepressants for neurovegetative symptoms versus emotional/cognitive symptoms?

**A:** Neurovegetative/physical symptoms: 1–3 weeks
Emotional/cognitive symptoms: 2–6 weeks

This lag period is clinically critical because neurovegetative symptoms (energy, sleep, appetite) may improve first while emotional symptoms (suicidal ideation, hopelessness) persist. Patients — especially children and adolescents — are at increased risk for suicidal behaviour during the first 2 weeks of antidepressant therapy and require vigilant monitoring.

**Q:** Which SSRI is safest in pregnancy and breastfeeding, and which is most used in children and adolescents?

**A:** Safest in pregnancy and breastfeeding: Sertraline (Zoloft®)
Most used in children and adolescents (most evidence in this age group): Fluoxetine (Prozac®)

Fluoxetine does not require a taper due to its long half-life and is the most activating SSRI (should be taken in the AM). Citalopram and escitalopram have the fewest drug interactions and are sleep-wake neutral. Fluvoxamine is sedating (taken in PM) and involved in many drug-drug interactions.

**Q:** Why must antidepressants not be used as monotherapy for bipolar depression?

**A:** Antidepressant monotherapy in bipolar disorder can provoke the emergence of mania. They should only be used in combination with a mood stabilizer or antipsychotic, and maintenance with antidepressants alone is generally not advisable.

Patients with bipolar disorder (bipolar depression) should not be treated with an antidepressant as first-line therapy. The risk of switching to mania or hypomania with antidepressant monotherapy outweighs the benefit. CANMAT guidelines recommend mood stabilizers or atypical antipsychotics as first-line.

**Q:** What are the key distinguishing features of bupropion (Wellbutrin®) compared to other antidepressants?

**A:** - Class: NDRI (norepinephrine-dopamine reuptake inhibitor)
- Less sexual dysfunction, less weight gain, less sedation than SSRIs/SNRIs
- Increased seizure risk at higher doses
- Contraindicated with history of seizure, stroke, brain tumour, or head injury
- NOT recommended for anxiety disorders (stimulating effects)
- Useful for seasonal depression and smoking cessation

Bupropion is available in IR, SR, and XL formulations (XL is the longest-acting). Dose: 100 mg starting, therapeutic 300–450 mg/day. Its lack of sexual side effects makes it useful as monotherapy for those with sexual dysfunction on SSRIs/SNRIs or as an augmenting agent.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and treatment of Serotonin Syndrome?

**A:** Causes: Combinations producing excess serotonin — SSRI+SSRI, SSRI+SNRI, SSRI/SNRI+MAOI, SSRI+tryptophan, MAOI+meperidine
Symptoms (FINISH mnemonic): Flu-like symptoms, Insomnia, Nausea, Imbalance, Sensory disturbances, Hyperarousal (anxiety/agitation); can progress to myoclonus, hyperthermia, rigor, hypertonicity
Treatment: Discontinue medication and provide emergency medical care

Serotonin syndrome results from overstimulation of the serotonergic system. It is rare but potentially life-threatening. It must be distinguished from NMS (which is caused by dopamine blockade and features lead-pipe rigidity and slower onset). Early recognition and drug discontinuation are critical.

**Q:** What is Antidepressant Discontinuation Syndrome? Which drugs are most associated with it, and how is it treated?

**A:** Caused by abrupt cessation of antidepressants, most commonly paroxetine, fluvoxamine, and venlafaxine (shortest half-lives).
Symptoms begin 1–3 days after stopping: anxiety, insomnia, irritability, mood lability, N/V, dizziness, headache, dystonia, tremor, chills, fatigue, myalgia ('flu-like symptoms')
Treatment: Restart antidepressant at the same dose and taper slowly over several weeks; symptoms resolve within 24 hours of restarting.

Fluoxetine does NOT require a taper due to its long half-life, making it the least likely to cause discontinuation syndrome. Symptoms can last 1–3 weeks if untreated. The clinical significance is avoiding short-half-life drugs or planning adequate tapering before stopping.

**Q:** What are the indications, therapeutic levels, and key monitoring requirements for lithium?

**A:** Indications: Acute mania (monotherapy or with adjunct SGA), Bipolar I depression, Bipolar disorder maintenance, augmentation of antidepressants in MDD and OCD, schizoaffective disorder, chronic aggression
Therapeutic levels: Adults 0.8–1.0 mmol/L (1.0–1.25 mmol/L for acute mania); Geriatric: 0.6–0.8 mmol/L
Monitoring: Serum levels every 5–7 days until therapeutic (always 12 h after last dose), then monthly, then q2–3 months; thyroid function and creatinine q6 months

Before initiating any mood stabilizer, baseline investigations include CBC with differential and platelets, ECG (if >45 years or cardiovascular risk), BUN, creatinine, extended electrolytes, TSH, and LFTs (for divalproex and carbamazepine). NSAIDs, thiazides, ACEIs, and metronidazole decrease lithium clearance and increase toxicity risk.

**Q:** What is the major dermatological risk of lamotrigine and how is it mitigated?

**A:** Stevens-Johnson Syndrome (SJS) — a potentially life-threatening skin emergency.
Mitigation: Very slow titration of lamotrigine dose. Dose must be adjusted (reduced) in patients taking other anticonvulsants such as divalproex. Monitor for skin rash; consider discontinuing if rash develops.

Lamotrigine is a first-line mood stabilizer primarily for bipolar I depression and maintenance. It has limited efficacy in preventing mania, especially as monotherapy. Starting dose is 100–200 mg/day (slower with divalproex co-administration). It is generally well tolerated aside from the SJS risk.

**Q:** What are the major risks of divalproex (valproate) in women of reproductive age?

**A:** 1. Teratogenicity — neural tube defects (spina bifida)
2. Polycystic Ovarian Syndrome (PCOS)
A reliable contraceptive strategy is required for women of reproductive age taking divalproex.

Carbamazepine also carries neural tube defect risk in pregnancy. Both divalproex and carbamazepine are teratogenic and require careful reproductive counselling. Divalproex monitoring includes LFTs weekly for 1 month, then monthly, then q2–3 months (risk of liver dysfunction), plus platelet monitoring and serum drug levels.

**Q:** What hematological risk is associated with carbamazepine and what monitoring is required?

**A:** Agranulocytosis (rare) and transient leukopenia (10%). Monitoring: Weekly blood counts for the first month due to risk of agranulocytosis. Watch for signs of blood dyscrasias: fever, rash, sore throat, easy bruising.

Carbamazepine is a second-line mood stabilizer for acute mania and bipolar maintenance. It is particularly useful for rapid cycling bipolar disorder and mixed phase/dysphoric mania. It also carries a 5% risk of skin rash and the risk of Stevens-Johnson syndrome (consider discontinuing). Neural tube defects can occur in pregnancy.

**Q:** What are the side effects of TCAs in overdose, and how is TCA toxicity treated?

**A:** Presentation of TCA toxicity: Anticholinergic effects, CNS stimulation → depression and seizures, ECG: prolonged QRS and QTc (reflecting severity)
Treatment: Activated charcoal, cathartics, supportive treatment, IV diazepam for seizures, physostigmine salicylate for coma. Do NOT give ipecac (causes rapid neurologic deterioration and seizures).

TCAs are highly lethal in overdose; 3× the therapeutic dose may be lethal. They are used for OCD (clomipramine is gold standard), melancholic depression, and anxiety disorders. ECG monitoring is required when using TCAs. Secondary amines (nortriptyline, desipramine) are preferred over tertiary amines due to lower anticholinergic adverse effects.

**Q:** What dietary restriction is required with MAOIs, and what is the most dangerous interaction?

**A:** MAOIs require a strict low-tyramine diet (avoid aged cheeses, wine, cured meats, fermented foods, etc.).
Most dangerous interaction: Tyramine-rich foods cause hypertensive crises. Also: combining with serotonergic drugs (SSRI, SNRI, tryptophan) causes serotonin syndrome; MAOI + meperidine is particularly dangerous.

MAOIs (phenelzine, tranylcypromine) are indicated for moderate/severe depression unresponsive to other antidepressants, atypical depression, and anxiety disorders. RIMAs (moclobemide) are reversible inhibitors of MAO-A and require the MAOI diet but carry a wider margin of safety in overdose than TCAs.

**Q:** What are the SSRI-mediated serotonin receptor side effects and the receptor responsible for each?

**A:** - 5-HT1A (central): Relief of depression, anxiolytic effect
- 5-HT2A (spinal cord): Sexual dysfunction — delayed ejaculation, anorgasmia, decreased libido
- 5-HT2A/2C (brain): Activation — anxiety, insomnia (worst with fluoxetine and paroxetine; warn patients about worsening anxiety in first 1–2 weeks)
- 5-HT3A (gut): GI upset — nausea, vomiting, bloating

Understanding receptor-mediated effects guides clinical management. For example, GI side effects typically improve within 1–2 weeks. Activation/anxiety symptoms initially worsen and then improve. Sexual dysfunction tends to persist and may require dose reduction, drug switch, or augmentation with bupropion or mirtazapine.

**Q:** What are the strategies for managing an antidepressant that provides only a partial response?

**A:** 1. Optimization: increase dose to maximum tolerated or highest therapeutic dose
2. Augmentation: add a non-antidepressant (e.g., thyroid hormone, lithium, atypical antipsychotics — aripiprazole, quetiapine, olanzapine, risperidone)
3. Combination: add another antidepressant (e.g., bupropion or mirtazapine to an SSRI or SNRI)
4. Switch: change the primary antidepressant (within or outside class)

According to CANMAT guidelines, full treatment to remission (not just response) is essential to decrease relapse rates. The sequence typically follows optimization → augmentation/combination → switch. Treatment to remission is the goal as residual symptoms predict relapse.

**Q:** What is the role of long-acting injectable (LAI) antipsychotics, and what is required before the first injection?

**A:** Role: Originally for schizophrenia patients who relapse due to non-adherence; current evidence suggests LAIs may be better than oral preparations overall for outcomes.
Requirement before first injection: Patient must have been exposed to an oral or short-term injectable form of the antipsychotic first.
Side effects: Similar to oral preparation; may have pain at injection site.

LAIs are formulated in oil for IM injection and are given on an outpatient basis. Dosing is guided by equivalence tables for oral dosing or pre-set starting schedules. Examples include Risperdal Consta® (q2 weeks), Invega Sustenna® (monthly), and Invega Trinza® (every 3 months).

**Q:** What are the indications and SSRI advantages of mirtazapine (Remeron®)?

**A:** Indications: Depression with prominent insomnia, agitation, or cachexia/weight loss.
Class: NaSSA (noradrenergic and specific serotonergic agent)
Mechanism: Inhibits presynaptic α-2 adrenergic receptors (disinhibits serotonin and NE release); antihistamine effects cause sedation and weight gain.
Advantages: Sedation useful for insomnia; weight gain useful in patients with cachexia; fewer sexual side effects than SSRIs.

Mirtazapine starting dose is 15 mg, therapeutic 15–45 mg/day. Paradoxically, lower doses tend to be more sedating due to greater antihistamine activity. It is commonly combined with SSRIs/SNRIs (California rocket fuel with venlafaxine) due to complementary mechanisms. Key side effect: increased cholesterol and triglycerides.

**Q:** What is the difference between augmentation and combination in antidepressant therapy?

**A:** Augmentation: Addition of a medication that is NOT an antidepressant to an existing antidepressant regimen (e.g., thyroid hormone, lithium, atypical antipsychotics such as aripiprazole, quetiapine, olanzapine, risperidone)
Combination: Addition of ANOTHER antidepressant to an existing antidepressant regimen (e.g., adding bupropion or mirtazapine to an SSRI or SNRI)

Both strategies are used when optimization (dose increase) has been insufficient. The distinction is important for MCCQE1 as questions may ask specifically about the definition of each. Both approaches aim for remission rather than just symptom response, as residual symptoms increase relapse risk.

**Q:** What are the antipsychotic-associated side effects by receptor system?

**A:** - Anticholinergic: dry mouth, urinary retention, constipation, blurred vision, confusion
- α-adrenergic blockade: orthostatic hypotension, erectile dysfunction, failure to ejaculate
- Dopaminergic blockade: EPS, galactorrhea, amenorrhea, erectile dysfunction, weight gain
- Anti-histamine: sedation, weight gain
- Hematologic: agranulocytosis (clozapine)
- Hypersensitivity: liver dysfunction, blood dyscrasias, skin rashes, NMS, altered temperature regulation
- Endocrine: metabolic syndrome
- Cardiac: QT prolongation

Understanding the receptor basis of antipsychotic side effects helps predict which drug is most appropriate for a given patient. For example, high-potency typicals (haloperidol) have more EPS but fewer metabolic effects; low-potency typicals (chlorpromazine) have more anticholinergic, sedating, and orthostatic effects.

**Q:** Which antidepressant class is contraindicated in combination with MAOIs and why?

**A:** SSRIs, SNRIs, TCAs (especially clomipramine), and tryptophan are all contraindicated with MAOIs due to the risk of serotonin syndrome. Meperidine (opioid) is also specifically dangerous with MAOIs. These combinations can cause life-threatening serotonin toxicity.

MAOIs inhibit the breakdown of serotonin, dopamine, and norepinephrine. Adding any serotonergic drug creates excess serotonin — serotonin syndrome. A washout period is required when switching between MAOIs and these agents (typically 2 weeks, or 5 weeks when stopping fluoxetine due to its long half-life).
